## I A Bombshell / 一 炸弹

===Original===
“Bless my soul! I nearly forgot,” exclaimed Colonel Jinks, as he came back into the store. “Tomorrow is Sam’s birthday and I promised Ma to bring him home something for a present. Have you got anything for a boy six years old?”

“Let me see,” answered the young woman behind the counter, turning round and looking at an upper shelf. “Why, yes; there’s just the thing. It’s a box of lead soldiers. I’ve never seen anything like them before”—and she reached up and pulled down a large cardboard box. “Just see,” she added as she opened it. “The officers have swords that come off, and the guns come off the men’s shoulders; and look at the—”

“Never mind,” interrupted the colonel. “I’m in a hurry. That’ll do very well. How much is it?”

And two minutes later he went out of the store with the box in his hand and got into his buggy, and was soon driving through the streets of Homeville on his way to his farm.

===Chinese===
“哎呀我的天！差点儿忘了。”金克斯上校（Colonel Jinks）一边回到店里一边嚷道，“明天是萨姆（Sam）的生日，我答应了孩子他妈，要带件东西回去给他当礼物。你们这儿有适合六岁男孩的玩意儿吗？”

“我瞧瞧。”柜台后面那位年轻女店员应了一声，转过身朝上面的货架望去。“哟，有啦，正好有这么个东西——是一盒铅制士兵，我还从没见过这么好的呢。”说着，她伸手取下一只大纸板盒。“您看，”她打开盒子，又补了一句，“军官的军刀能卸下来，枪也能从士兵肩上取下来；您再看这——”

“不用看了。”上校打断了她，“我赶时间，这个就挺好。多少钱？”

两分钟后，他手提盒子走出店铺，上了自己的轻便马车，不多时便驶过霍姆维尔（Homeville）的街道，往城外自家的农场去了。

===Original===
No one had ever asked Colonel Jinks where he had obtained his title. In fact, he had never put the question to himself. It was an integral part of his person, and as little open to challenge as his hand or his foot. There are favored regions of the world’s surface where colonels, like poets, are born, not made, and good fortune had placed the colonel’s birthplace in one of them. For the benefit of those of my readers who may be prejudiced against war, and in justice to the colonel, it should be stated that the only military thing about him was his title. He was a mild-mannered man with a long thin black beard and a slight stoop, and his experience with firearms was confined to the occasional shooting of depredatory crows, squirrels, and rats with an ancient fowling-piece. Still there is magic in a name. And who knows but that the subtle influence of the title of colonel may have unconsciously guided the searching eyes of the young saleswoman among the Noah’s arks and farmyards to the box of lead soldiers?

===Chinese===
从没有人问过金克斯上校，他这个头衔是打哪儿得来的。其实他自己也从未拿这个问题问过自己。这头衔早已同他这个人长在了一起，就像他的手、他的脚一样，无从置疑。世界上有些得天独厚的角落，上校同诗人一样，是天生的，不是造就出来的，而上校有幸，偏偏就出生在这样一处地方。为了照顾我那些也许对战事抱有偏见的读者，也为了还上校一个公道，这里应当说明：他身上同军队沾边的，唯有这个头衔。他是个举止温和的人，蓄着一部稀疏的黑色长须，背稍稍有些驼，平生同火器打交道的全部经验，不过是用一支老式猎枪，偶尔打打糟蹋庄稼的乌鸦、松鼠和老鼠罢了。然而名号自有魔力。谁知道呢？说不定正是“上校”这个头衔的微妙影响，在冥冥之中引着那位年轻女店员四处搜寻的目光，越过一排排诺亚方舟和农场模型，落在了那盒铅制士兵上。

===Original===
The lad for whom the present was intended was a happy farmer’s boy, an only child, for whom the farm was the whole world and who looked upon the horses and cows as his fellows. His little red head was constantly to be seen bobbing about in the barnyard among the sheep and calves, or almost under the horses’ feet. The chickens and sparrows and swallows were his playmates, and they seemed to have no fear of him. The black colt with its thick legs and ruffled mane ran behind its gray dam to hide from everyone else, but it let Sam pat it without flinching. The first new-hatched chicken which had been given to him for his very own turned out to be a rooster, and when he found that it had to be taken from him and beheaded he was quite inconsolable and refused absolutely to feast upon his former friend. But with this tenderness of disposition Sam had inherited another still stronger trait, and this was a deep respect for authority, and such elements of revolt as revealed themselves in his grief over his rooster were soon stifled in his little heart. He bowed submissively before the powers that be. From the time when he first lisped he had called his parents “Colonel Jinks” and “Mrs. Jinks.” His mother had succeeded with great difficulty in substituting the term “Ma” for herself, but she could not make him address his father as anything but “Colonel,” and after a time his father grew to like it. No one knew how Sam had acquired the habit; it was simply the expression of an inherently respectful nature. He reverenced his father and loved his father’s profession of farmer. His earliest pleasure was to hold the reins and drive “like Colonel Jinks,” and his earliest ambition was to become a teamster, that part of the farm work having peculiar attractions for him.

===Chinese===
这份礼物要送的孩子，是个快活的农家男孩，家中独子；农场在他就是整个世界，马呀牛呀都是他的同伴。谷仓院子里，他那颗红头发的小脑袋总在羊群和牛犊中间钻来钻去，有时几乎钻到马蹄底下。母鸡、麻雀和燕子都是他的玩伴，它们见他来似乎毫无惧色。那匹四腿粗壮、鬃毛蓬乱的小黑马，一见旁人就躲到灰色母马身后，唯独让萨姆抚摸时毫不躲闪。头一只新孵出来、完全归他所有的小鸡，后来竟发现是只公鸡；当他得知这只鸡终究要被从他手里夺走、砍掉脑袋时，他伤心得无可慰藉，死活不肯把这位昔日的朋友吃进肚里。不过，在这副柔肠之外，萨姆还继承了另一种更强烈的禀性——对权威的深深敬畏；他在痛失公鸡一事上显露出的那一点点反叛苗头，很快便在他幼小的心田里窒息了。他在一切既成的权威面前俯首帖耳。打从咿呀学语起，他就管自己的父母叫“金克斯上校”和“金克斯太太（Mrs. Jinks）”。母亲费了九牛二虎之力，才让他改口管自己叫“妈”，可她怎么也没法让他用别的称呼叫父亲，只肯叫“上校”；日子一久，父亲自己也喜欢上这个称呼了。没有人知道萨姆这习惯是打哪儿来的；那不过是一种生来恭敬的天性的自然流露。他敬重父亲，也热爱父亲当农夫的营生。他最早的乐趣，便是攥着缰绳，“像金克斯上校那样”赶车；他最早的志向，是做一名赶车的把式——农活里头，数这一样对他最有吸引力。

===Original===
In the afternoon on which we were introduced to the Colonel, Sam was watching on the veranda for his father’s return, and was quick to spy the parcel under his arm, and many were the wild guesses he made as to its contents. The Colonel left it carelessly upon the hall table, and Sam could easily have peeped into it, but he would as soon have thought of cutting off his hand.

“What’s in that box in the hall, Colonel Jinks?” he asked in an embarrassed voice at supper, as he fingered the edge of the tablecloth and looked blushingly at his plate.

“Oh, that?” replied his father with a wink—“that’s a bombshell.” And a bombshell indeed it proved to be for the Jinks family.

===Chinese===
就在我们结识上校的那个下午，萨姆正在游廊上守着等父亲回来，一眼便瞧见他腋下夹着的包裹，对里面装的是什么做出了种种不着边际的猜想。上校随手把包裹撂在门厅的桌上，萨姆本可以轻易偷看上一眼，可他宁可想剁掉自己一只手，也不会动这个念头。

“门厅里那个盒子里装的是什么呀，金克斯上校？”晚饭时他难为情地问道，一边用手指捻着桌布边，一边红着脸瞅自己的盘子。

“哦，那个？”父亲眨了眨眼答道，“那是一颗炸弹。”而这颗炸弹，对金克斯一家来说，日后果然名副其实。

===Original===
The box was put upon a table in the room in which little Sam slept with his parents, and he was told that he could have it in the morning. He was a long time going to sleep that night, trying to imagine the contents of the mysterious box. Not until he had quite made up his mind that it was a farmyard did he finally drop off. At the first break of day Sam was out of bed. With bare feet he walked on tiptoe across the cold bare floor and seized the precious box. He lifted the lid at one corner and put in his hand and felt what was there, and tried to guess what it could be. Perhaps it was a Noah’s Ark; but no, if those were people there were too many of them. He would have to give it up. He took off the cover and looked in. It was not a farmyard, at any rate, and the corners of his mouth became tremulous from disappointment. No, they were soldiers. But what did he want of soldiers? He had heard of such things, but they had never been anything in his life. He had never seen a real soldier nor heard of a toy-soldier before, and he did not quite know what they were for. He crept back to bed crestfallen, his present in his arms. Sitting up in bed he began to investigate the contents of the box. It was a complete infantry battalion, and beautiful soldiers they were. Their coats were red, their trousers blue, and they wore white helmets and carried muskets with bayonets fixed. Sam began to feel reconciled. He turned the box upside-down and emptied the soldiers upon the counterpane. Then he noticed that they were not all alike. There were some officers, who carried swords instead of rifles. He began to look for them and single them out, when his eye was caught by a magnificent white leaden plume issuing from the helmet of one of them. He picked up this soldier, and the sight of him filled him with delight. He was taller and broader than the rest, his air was more martial—there was something inspiring in the way in which he held his sword. His golden epaulets were a miracle of splendor, but it was the plume, the great white plume, that held the boy enthralled. A ray of light from the morning sun, reflected by the window of the stable, found its way through a chink in the blind and fell just upon this plume. The effect was electric. Sam was fascinated, and he continued to hold the lead soldier so that the dazzling light should fall on it, gazing upon it in an ecstasy.

===Chinese===
那只盒子被摆在萨姆同父母合睡的房间里的一张桌上，大人告诉他，要到早晨才归他。那天夜里，他久久不能入睡，一门心思揣想那只神秘盒子里装的是什么。直到拿定主意，认定那是一套农场模型，他才终于合眼睡着。天刚破晓，萨姆就下了床。他光着小脚丫，踮起脚尖穿过冰凉的光地板，一把抱住了那只宝贝盒子。他把盒盖掀起一角，伸手进去摸了摸，想猜猜里面究竟是什么。没准儿是一套诺亚方舟？不对，倘若那些是小人儿，数目也太多了些。他只好死了这条心。他揭开盖子朝里看。反正不是农场模型，他的嘴角失望得直打颤。不是，是一队士兵。可他要士兵做什么呢？这种东西他倒是听说过，可在他的生活里它们从来不曾有过什么位置。他从未见过一个真正的士兵，也从没听说过玩具士兵，他简直不知道这些东西是干什么用的。他抱着礼物，垂头丧气地爬回床上，坐起身来，开始清点盒中之物。那是一支建制齐全的步兵营，士兵们个个漂亮极了。他们红上衣、蓝裤子，头戴白色头盔，肩扛上了刺刀的火枪。萨姆渐渐回心转意了。他把盒子翻了个底朝天，将士兵尽数倒在床罩上。这时他发觉，这些士兵并非千人一面。其中有几个军官，别人扛火枪，他们却佩着军刀。他开始寻找他们、把他们一个个挑出来，忽然，其中一个士兵头盔上插着的一支气派非凡的白色铅翎攫住了他的目光。他拾起这个士兵，一望之下满心欢喜。他比其余的更高更壮，气宇更为英武——他持刀的姿势里有一种振奋人心的东西。他那金色的肩章辉煌得堪称奇迹，然而真正令这孩子心醉神迷的，还是那支翎毛，那支硕大的白翎。一缕晨光经马厩的窗子反射，从百叶窗的一道缝隙里漏进屋来，恰恰落在那支翎毛上。那效果如同电击。萨姆着了迷，他一直举着那个铅兵，好让那道耀眼的光落在上面，如痴如醉地凝望着它。

===Original===
Sam spent that entire day in the company of his new soldiers—nothing could drag him away from them. He made his father show him how they should march and form themselves and fight. He drew them up in hollow squares facing outward and in hollow squares facing inward, in column of fours and in line of battle, in double rank and single rank.

“What are the bayonets for, Colonel Jinks?”

“To stick into bad people, Sam.”

“And have the bad people bayonets, too?”

“Yes, Sam.”

“Do they stick their bayonets into good people?”

“Oh, I suppose so. Do stop bothering me. If I’d known you’d ask so many questions, I’d never have got you the soldiers.”

===Chinese===
萨姆一整天都同他的新士兵们厮守在一起——什么也没法把他从它们身边拉走。他让父亲演示给他看，这些士兵该怎样行军、列队、作战。他把他们摆成朝外的空心方阵，又摆成朝内的空心方阵，摆成四路纵队，又摆成战斗横队，双列也好，单列也罢。

“刺刀是干什么用的，金克斯上校？”

“用来捅坏人的，萨姆。”

“坏人也有刺刀吗？”

“有的，萨姆。”

“他们也会把刺刀捅进好人身上吗？”

“哦，大概是吧。快别缠着我了。早知道你要问这么多问题，我才不会给你买这些士兵呢。”

===Original===
His parents thought that a few days would exhaust the boy’s devotion to his new toys, but it was not so. He deserted the barnyard for the lead soldiers. They were placed on a chair by his bed at night, and he could not sleep unless his right hand grasped the white-plumed colonel. The smell of the fresh paint as it peeled off on his little fingers clung to his memory through life as the most delicious of odors. He would tease his father to play with the soldiers with him. He would divide the force in two, and one side would defend a fort of blocks and books while the other assaulted. In these games Sam always insisted in having the plumed colonel on his side. Once when Sam’s colonel had succeeded in capturing a particularly impregnable fortress on top of an unabridged dictionary his father remarked casually:

===Chinese===
父母以为，用不了几天，孩子对这份新玩具的热乎劲儿就会过去，可事实并非如此。他抛下谷仓院，一心扑到了铅兵身上。夜里，这些士兵被摆在他床边的椅子上，右手不攥着那位插白翎的上校，他就睡不着。新漆的气味在小手指上剥落下来，那股气息作为天下最香甜的气味，一辈子萦绕在他的记忆里。他会缠着父亲陪他玩士兵。他把部队一分为二，一方守卫一座用积木和书本搭成的堡垒，另一方发起强攻。玩这种游戏时，萨姆总要坚持让那位插翎上校归到自己一边。有一回，萨姆的上校攻克了一座筑在一部足本大词典顶上的尤其固若金汤的堡垒，父亲随口说了一句：

===Original===
“He’s quite a hero, isn’t he, Sam?”

“A what?” said Sam.

“A hero.”

“What is a hero, Colonel Jinks?” And his father explained to him what a hero was, giving several examples from history and fiction. The word took the boy’s fancy at once. From that day forward the officer was colonel no longer, he was a “hero,” or rather, “the hero.” Sam now began to save his pennies for other soldiers, and to beg for more and more as successive birthdays and Christmases came round. He played at soldiers himself, too, coaxing the less warlike children of the neighborhood to join him. But his enthusiasm always left them behind, and they tired much sooner than he did of the sport. He persuaded his mother to make him a uniform something like that of the lead soldiers, and the stores of Homeville were ransacked for drums, swords, and belts and toy-guns. He would stand on guard for hours at the barnyard gate, saluting in the most solemn manner whoever passed, even if it was only a sparrow. The only interest in animals which survived his change of heart was that which he now took in horses as chargers. He would ride the farm-horses bareback to the trough, holding the halter in one hand and a tin sword in the other with the air of a field-marshal. When strangers tapped him on the cheek and asked him—as is the wont of strangers—“What are you going to be, my boy, when you grow up?” he answered no longer, as he used to do, “A driver, sir,” but now invariably, “A hero.”

===Chinese===
“他可真是个英雄，是不是，萨姆？”

“个什么？”萨姆问。

“英雄。”

“英雄是什么呀，金克斯上校？”于是父亲给他解释了什么是英雄，还从历史和小说里举了好些例子。这个词一下子就把孩子迷住了。从那一天起，这位军官便不再是上校了，他成了一位“英雄”，或者更确切地说，“那位英雄”。打那时起，萨姆开始把一个个铜板攒起来，好添置别的士兵；随着一个个生日和圣诞节接踵而至，他张口要的士兵也越来越多。他自己也扮起士兵来，哄着街坊里那些不那么好战的孩子们入伙。可他的热乎劲儿总是把他们远远甩在后头，他们对这游戏厌烦得远比他快。他说动母亲给他做了一套同那些铅兵相仿的军装，又把霍姆维尔的各家店铺搜罗了个遍，就为置办鼓、军刀、腰带和玩具枪。他会在谷仓院大门口一站岗就是几个钟头，向一切过路的人以最庄严的姿势敬礼，哪怕过路的只是一只麻雀。他改了心思之后，对动物残存的唯一兴趣，是把马当作战马来看待。他会骑上不备鞍的农场的马去水槽，一手攥着笼头，一手握着一把锡皮军刀，一副元帅的气派。每逢生人捏捏他的脸蛋，问出那句生人惯问的话——“小伙子，你长大了想干什么呀？”——他再也不像从前那样答一声“赶车，先生”，而是无一例外地答：“当英雄。”

===Original===
It so happened some two or three years after Sam’s mind had begun to follow the paths of warfare that his father and mother took him one day to an anniversary celebration of the Methodist Church at Homeville, and a special parade of the newly organized “John Wesley Boys’ Brigade” of the church was one of the features of the occasion. If Mrs. Jinks had anticipated this, she would doubtless have left Sam at home, for she knew that he was already quite sufficiently inclined toward things military; but even she could not help enjoying the boy’s unmeasured delight at this, his first experience of militarism in the flesh. The parade was indeed a pretty sight. There were perhaps fifty boys in line, ranging from six to eighteen years of age. Their gray uniforms were quite new and the gilt letters “J.W.B.B.” on their caps shone brightly. They marched along with their miniature muskets and fixed bayonets, their chubby, kissable faces all a-smile, as they sang “Onward, Christian Soldiers,” with words adapted by their pastor:

> “Onward, Christian soldiers,


 ‘Gainst the heathen crew!


 In the name of Jesus


 Let us run them through.”

By a curious coincidence their captain had a white feather in his cap, suggesting at a considerable distance the plume of the leaden “hero.” Sam was overcome with joy. He pulled the “hero” from his pocket (he always carried it about with him) and compared the two warriors. The “hero” was still unique, incomparable, but Sam realized that he was an ideal which might be lived up to, not an impossible dream, not the denizen of an inaccessible heaven. From that day he bent his little energies to the task of removing his family to Homeville.

===Chinese===
萨姆的心思走上兵戈之道两三年后的一天，父母带他去参加霍姆维尔卫理公会（Methodist Church）的周年庆典，而新组建的教会少年组织“约翰·卫斯理少年旅”（John Wesley Boys’ Brigade）的一场专场游行，正是当天的一大看点。倘若金克斯太太早料到有这一出，她准会把萨姆留在家里，因为她知道，孩子对军事这一套早已迷恋得够可以了；可就连她，眼见孩子头一回亲眼见识活生生的军国主义时那无以复加的欢喜，也忍不住跟着高兴。这场游行的确好看。队列里大约有五十个男孩，年龄从六岁到十八岁不等。他们的灰色制服崭新笔挺，帽子上的镀金字母“J.W.B.B.”闪闪发亮。他们扛着小型火枪、端着刺刀行进，一张张胖乎乎、叫人想亲一口的脸蛋个个笑开了花，一边走一边唱着《基督精兵前进》（Onward, Christian Soldiers），歌词由他们的牧师改编如下：

> “基督精兵前进，


  杀向异教群丑！


  奉耶稣的名，


  把他们捅个透！”

说来也巧，他们的队长帽子上正插着一根白羽毛，隐隐约约令人联想到那位铅制“英雄”的翎毛。萨姆喜不自胜。他从口袋里掏出“英雄”（他一向随身带着它），把这两位武士比了又比。“英雄”依旧独一无二、无与伦比，但萨姆意识到，他是一个可以效仿企及的理想，不是一场实现不了的梦，也不是那高不可攀的天堂里的居民。从那天起，他便把他小小的全部力气，用在把家搬到霍姆维尔这件大事上了。

===Original===
It is not so much strength as perseverance which moves the world. Colonel Jinks had laid up a competence and had always intended to retire, when he could afford it, to the market town. Among other things, the school facilities would be much better in town than in the country. Mrs. Jinks in a moment of folly took the side of the boy, and, whatever may have been the controlling and predominating cause, the fact is that, when Sam had attained the age of twelve, the Colonel sold the farm and bought one of the best houses in Homeville. Sam at once became a member of the John Wesley Brigade and showed an aptitude for soldiering truly amazing. Before he was fourteen he was captain, and wore, himself, the coveted white feather, and his military duties became the absorbing interest of his life. He thought and spoke of nothing else, and he was universally known in the town as “Captain Jinks,” which was often abbreviated to “Cap.” No one ever passed boyhood and youth in such congenial surroundings and with such complete satisfaction as “Cap” Jinks of the John Wesley Boys’ Brigade.

===Chinese===
推动这个世界的，与其说是力量，不如说是坚忍。金克斯上校早已攒下一份殷实的家底，一直打算等宽裕了，便退居到那座市镇去。再说，城里的学校条件总要比乡下好得多，这也算得一条缘故。金克斯太太一时糊涂，站到了孩子一边。且不管左右大局的究竟是什么缘故，总之，萨姆满十二岁那年，上校卖掉了农场，在霍姆维尔买下了数一数二的好房子。萨姆随即加入了约翰·卫斯理少年旅，而且显露出一副当兵的真本事，令人叹为观止。不到十四岁，他便当上了队长，自己也戴上了那支人人艳羡的白羽毛，军中差事成了他生活的头等大事。他心里想的、嘴上说的，再没有别的；全镇上下都管他叫“金克斯上尉”（Captain Jinks），又常常简称一声“上尉”。世上再没有谁，能在这样如鱼得水的环境里、怀着这样十全十美的满足，度过自己的少年时光和青春岁月了——约翰·卫斯理少年旅的“金克斯上尉”，便是如此。

## XI A Dinner Party at Gin-Sin / 十一 金辛的一场晚宴

===Original===
During the past months great events had taken place in the ancient empire of Porsslania. Many years earlier the various churches had sent missionaries to that benighted land to reclaim its inhabitants from barbarism and heathenism. These emissaries were not received with the enthusiastic gratitude which they deserved, and some of the Porsslanese had the impudence to assert that they were a civilized people when their new teachers had been naked savages. They proved their barbarism, however, by indulging in the most unreasonable prejudices against a foreign religion, and when cornered in argument they would say to the missionaries, “How would you like us to convert your people to our religion?” an answer so illogical that it demonstrates either their bad faith or the low development of their intellects. The missionaries of some of the sects, by the help of their governments, gradually obtained a good deal of land and at the same time a certain degree of civil jurisdiction. The foreign governments, wishing to bless the natives with temporal as well as celestial advantages, followed up the missionary pioneers with traders in cheap goods, rum, opium, and firearms, and finally endeavored to introduce their own machinery and factory system, which had already at home raised all the laboring classes to affluence, put an end to poverty, and realized the dream of the prophets of old. The Porsslanese resolutely resisted all these benevolent enterprises and doggedly expressed their preference for their ancient customs. In order to overcome this unreasonable opposition and assure the welfare of the people, the various Powers from time to time seized the great ports of the Empire. The fertile diplomacy of the courts found sufficient grounds for this. Most frequently the pretext was an attack upon a missionary or even a case of cold-blooded murder, and it became a proverb among the Porsslanese that it takes a province to bury a missionary. Finally, all the harbors of the Empire were in the hands of foreigners, who used this advantageous position to confer blessings thick and fast upon the reluctant population, who richly deserved, as a punishment, to be left to themselves. At last a revolutionary party sprang up among this deluded people, claiming that their own Government was showing too much favor to foreign religions and foreign machines. The Government did not put down this revolt. Some said that it did not have the power and that the provinces were practically independent of the central authority. Others whispered that the Imperial Court secretly favored the rebels. However this may be, the Fencers, as the rebels were called from their skill with the native sword, succeeded without much difficulty in getting possession of the imperial city and imprisoning the foreign embassies and legations in the enclosure of the Anglian Embassy. The Imperial Court meanwhile fled to a distant city and left the entire control of the situation in the hands of the Fencers. The peril of the legations was extreme. They were cut off completely from the coast, which was many miles distant, and the foreign newspaper correspondents amused themselves by sending detailed accounts of the manner in which they had been tortured and murdered. The principal men among the Porsslanese assured the Powers that the legations were safe, but they were not believed. A great expedition was organized in which all the great Powers took a part. The forts near the sea were stormed and taken. The intermediate city of Gin-Sin was besieged and finally fell, and the forces advanced to the gates of the Capital. Before long they succeeded in taking possession of the great city. The Fencers fled in confusion, and at least two-thirds of the population fled with them, fearing the vengeance of the foreigners. The legations were saved, after one ambassador had been shot by an assassin. The city was divided into districts, each of which was turned over to the safekeeping of one of the foreign armies, and the object of the expedition had been accomplished. In the meantime many foreign residents, including many missionaries in various parts of the Empire, had been murdered, the inhabitants not recognizing the obvious fact that they and their countrymen were their best friends.

===Chinese===
过去几个月里，古老的波斯兰尼亚（Porsslania）帝国发生了许多大事。许多年前，各家教会便派遣传教士前往那片蒙昧之地，要把当地居民从野蛮与异教中拯救出来。这些使者并未受到他们理所应得的热情感激，有些波斯兰（Porsslanese）人竟放肆到声称：早在他们的新老师还是赤身露体的野人时，他们自己已是一个文明的民族了。然而，他们对一种外来宗教抱持最不可理喻的偏见，恰恰证明了自己的野蛮；每逢辩到理屈词穷，他们便会反问传教士：“倘若我们让你们的百姓改信我们的宗教，你们乐意吗？”——这回答竟是如此不合逻辑，足见他们不是居心不良，便是智力低下。某些教派的传教士，借着本国政府的撑腰，渐渐攫取了大片土地，同时还取得了某种程度的民事司法权。各国政府一心想让土著在尘世的好处之外兼得天国的好处，便紧随着传教先锋的足迹，派去了贩卖廉价杂货、朗姆酒、鸦片和火器的商人，末了又致力于引进他们本国的机器和工厂制度——那制度在他们国内早已使全体劳动阶级富贵荣华、根绝了贫穷、实现了古时先知的梦想。波斯兰人对这些慈善事业一概顽抗不屈，固执地表示他们更中意自己的古老风俗。为克服这等无理的阻挠、确保万民的福祉，列强便不时夺取帝国的大港口。各国外交的丰富才思，总能为这类行径找到充足的理由。最常用的口实，莫过于某位传教士挨了打，甚至一桩冷血的凶杀案；久而久之，波斯兰人中便生出一句谚语：埋一名传教士，得赔上一个省。到后来，帝国所有的港口尽入外人之手；外国人便借这有利地位，把恩泽又密又急地赐给那些百般不情愿的百姓——这些百姓实在罪有应得，罚他们自生自灭才好。终于，在这群被引入迷途的民众中间，兴起了一个革命党派，声言他们自己的政府对外国宗教和外国机器太施恩宠了。政府并没有镇压这场叛乱。有人说政府无力镇压，各行省实际上早已不听中央号令；也有人交头接耳，说朝廷暗地里偏向叛党。不管真相如何，“剑民”（Fencers）——叛党因娴于本土刀剑之术而得的称号——没费多大周折便占据了京城，把各国公使使馆统统围困在安格利亚（Anglian）使馆的大院里。朝廷则逃到了远处一座城市，把整个局面的处置权一骨脑儿丢给了剑民。各国使馆危在旦夕。他们同远在许多英里之外的海岸完全断了联系，各国外报的记者们则以发去一篇篇绘声绘色的通讯自娱，详述使馆人员如何受尽酷刑、如何惨遭屠戮。波斯兰的头面人物再三向列强担保使馆安然无恙，却无人相信。于是一支庞大的远征军组成了，各大强国无不参与。临海的炮台被强攻攻克。居中的要冲金辛（Gin-Sin）城被围困多时，终告陷落，大军随即推进到京城城下。不久，他们便攻占了这座大都。剑民仓皇出逃，城中百姓慑于外国人的报复，至少有三分之二也随之逃散。使馆总算得救了，此前已有一位公使死于刺客枪下。全城被划分为若干区域，各交由一支外国军队妥为看管，远征的目的算是达到了。这期间，帝国各地的许多外国侨民遇害了，其中包括许多传教士——当地居民竟认不清这个明摆着的事实：这些人和他们的同胞，本是当地居民最好的朋友。

===Original===
Affairs had reached this position when orders came to Havilla for Colonel Jinks to proceed to join the army in Porsslania, where he would be placed in command of a regiment. His fidus Achates, Cleary, had also received permission from his journal to accompany him, and the two set sail on a transport which carried details of troops. It is true that these troops could ill be spared from the Cubapines, as the country was still in the hands of the natives with the exception of here and there a strip of the seacoast, and there was much illness among the troops, many being down with fever and worse diseases. But it was necessary for the Government to make as good a showing in Porsslania as the other Powers, and the reinforcements had to go.

It was on a hot summer day that Sam and Cleary looked over the rail of the transport as they watched the troops come on board. It was a remarkable scene, for a crowd of native women were on the shore, weeping and arguing with the men and preventing them from getting into the boats.

===Chinese===
时局到了这个地步，命令传到了哈维拉（Havilla），着金克斯上校开赴波斯兰尼亚军前，到那边将统带一个团。他那位须臾不离的忠实伙伴克利里（Cleary），也得到报社的准许随行，两人便登上一艘运载分遣部队的运输舰启航了。诚然，这些部队从古巴平（Cubapines）抽调出去实属勉为其难：那地方除了零零星星几段海岸，依然握在土著手里；况且军中疫病流行，害热病的、生恶疾的比比皆是。然而政府必须在波斯兰尼亚摆出与其余列强同样气派的阵仗，援军非去不可。

正是在一个炎热的夏日，萨姆和克利里凭倚运输舰的栏杆，望着部队登船。那场面煞是可观：岸上聚着一群土著妇女，哭哭啼啼，同士兵们拉拉扯扯，不让他们登上小艇。

===Original===
“Who on earth are they?” asked Sam.

“It’s a pretty mean practical joke,” said Cleary. “That regiment has been up in the interior, and they’ve all had wives up there. They buy them for five dollars apiece. And the Governor of the province there, a friendly native, has sent more than a hundred of the women down here, to get rid of them, I suppose, and now the poor things want to come along with their young men. Some of them have got babies, do you see?”

===Chinese===
“她们到底是些什么人？”萨姆问。

“这是个相当缺德的恶作剧。”克利里说，“那个团一直驻在内陆，人人在那边都娶了老婆，一个人花五块钱就能把她们买下。那省的总督——一个亲善的土著——把一百多个这样的女人送到这儿来，我看是想甩掉她们。如今这些可怜虫要跟着她们的年轻汉子一起走。有几个还抱着娃娃呢，你瞧见没有？”

===Original===
After a long and noisy delay the captain of the transport, assisted by the officers of the regiment in question, persuaded the women to stay behind, giving a few coppers to each and making the most reckless and unabashed promises of return. The steamer then weighed anchor and was soon passing the sunken Castalian fleet.

===Chinese===
吵吵嚷嚷折腾了好一阵子之后，运输舰舰长在那个团的军官们协助下，总算劝得那些女人留在了岸上：每人发几枚铜板，再许下最信口开河、脸不红心不跳的凯旋归来的诺言。随后轮船起锚，不多时便驶过沉没的卡斯塔利亚（Castalian）舰队。

===Original===
“The Court at Whoppington has just allowed prize-money to the officers and men for sinking those ships,” said Cleary. “They didn’t get as much as they wanted, but it’s a good round sum.”

“I’m glad they will get some remuneration for their hard work,” said Sam.

“Do you see that native sloop over there?” said Cleary. “She’s a pirate boat we caught down in the archipelago. She had sunk a merchant vessel loaded with opium or something of the kind, very valuable. They’d got her in shallow water and had killed some of the crew, and the rest swam ashore, and they were dividing up the swag when they were caught. They would have had I don’t know how many dollars apiece. They were all hanged.”

“Serves them right,” said Sam. “We must put down piracy. Goodbye, Havilla,” he added, waving his hat toward the capital. “It makes me feel happy to think that I have actually ended the war by capturing Gomaldo.”

“Not much!” cried Cleary. “Didn’t you hear the news this morning? The Cubapinos are twice as active as ever. They’re rising everywhere.”

===Chinese===
“沃平顿（Whoppington）的朝廷刚批准发给击沉那些军舰的官兵一笔捕获赏金。”克利里说，“数目没有他们想要的那么多，可也是相当可观的一笔整数。”

“他们辛苦一场能得些酬报，我很高兴。”萨姆说。

“你瞧见那边那艘土著单桅帆船了吗？”克利里说，“那是我们在群岛南边抓到的一艘海盗船。它凿沉了一艘装载鸦片之类的商船，货物贵重得很。他们把商船逼进浅水，杀了船上一部分水手，其余的游水上了岸；他们正分赃呢，就被逮住了。不然每人分到手的美元还不知道有多少呢。他们全被绞死了。”

“活该。”萨姆说，“我们必须剿灭海盗。”他朝着都城挥了挥帽子，又补了一句：“再会了，哈维拉。一想到我实实在在靠俘获戈马尔多（Gomaldo）终结了这场战争，我心里就美滋滋的。”

“美什么美！”克利里嚷道，“你今早没听新闻吗？古巴平人（Cubapinos）闹得比先前加倍欢实，四面八方都起了事。”

===Original===
Not many days later, and after an uneventful voyage, the transport sailed into the mouth of the Hai-Po River and came to anchor off the ruins of the Porsslanese forts. Colonel Jinks had orders to proceed at once to Gin-Sin, and he left with Cleary on a river steamer. They were much struck by the utter desolation of the country. There were no signs of life, but here and there the smoking ruins of a town showed where human beings had been. They noticed something floating in the water with a swarm of flies hovering over it.

===Chinese===
没过多少天，一路平安无事，运输舰便驶入海波（Hai-Po）河口，在波斯兰炮台的废墟外抛锚停泊。金克斯上校奉命即刻前往金辛，便同克利里登上了一艘江轮。两岸满目荒凉，令他们大为震动：不见一点人烟，只有东一处西一处还在冒烟的城镇废墟，标出人类曾经生息的地方。他们瞥见水面上漂着个什么东西，上头盘旋着一群苍蝇。

===Original===
“Good heavens! it’s a corpse,” said Cleary. “It’s a native. That’s a handsome silk jacket, and it doesn’t look like a soldier’s either. Look at that vulture. It’s sweeping down on it.”

The vulture circled round in the air, coming close to the body, but did not touch it.

“It has had enough to eat already,” said an Anglian passenger who was standing near them. “Did you ever see such a fat bird? You’ll see plenty of bodies before long. Do you observe those vultures ahead there? You’ll find floating bodies wherever they are.”

===Chinese===
“天哪！是具尸首。”克利里说，“是个土人。那件绸子褂子还挺讲究，瞧着也不像兵勇穿的。看那只秃鹫，正朝它俯冲下去呢。”

秃鹫在空中绕着圈子，一度贴到尸身近旁，却没有碰它。

“它早吃饱了。”站在他们近旁的一位安格利亚乘客说，“你见过这么肥的鸟吗？用不了多久，你就能看到数不清的尸首。瞧见前头那些秃鹫没有？它们在哪儿，哪儿就漂得有尸首。”

===Original===
“I suppose they are the bodies of soldiers,” said Sam.

“No, indeed, not all of them by any means. These Porsslanese must be stamped out like vipers. I’m thankful to say most of the armies are doing their duty. They don’t give any quarter to native soldiers, and they despatch the wounded too. That’s the only way to treat them, and they don’t feel pain the way we do. In fact, they rather like it. The Tutonians are setting a good example; they shoot their prisoners. I saw them shoot about seventy. They tied them together four by four by their pigtails and then shot them. It’s best, though, to avoid taking prisoners; that’s what most of them do.”

“But you say these bodies are not all soldiers,” said Cleary.

“No, of course not. You see the Mosconians kill any natives they please. Then those who are out at night are killed as a matter of course, and those who won’t work for the soldiers naturally have to be put out of the way. It’s the only way to enforce discipline. Look at these bodies now.”

===Chinese===
“我猜那些是士兵的尸首吧。”萨姆说。

“不，绝非如此，也绝不能一概而论。这些波斯兰人就得像对付毒蛇似的斩尽杀绝。谢天谢地，多数军队都在恪尽职守：对土著士兵绝不留活口，伤兵也一并打发掉。对付他们就只有这一个法子；再说，他们挨起痛来跟咱们不一样——说实话，他们还挺乐意呢。图托尼亚（Tutonian）人正树着好榜样：他们枪毙俘虏。我亲眼见他们枪毙了七十来个，把俘虏四个一组用辫子拴在一起，然后开枪。不过顶好的法子还是干脆别收俘虏，多数军队都这么办。”

“可你刚才说，这些尸首并不全是士兵。”克利里说。

“那当然不是。你也知道，莫斯科尼亚（Mosconian）人想杀哪个土人就杀哪个。再者，夜里出门的一律格杀勿论；不肯给军队做工的，自然也得清除掉。要维持纪律，舍此别无他法。瞧，尸首这又漂过来了。”

===Original===
Corpses were now coming down the river one after another. Each had its attendant swarm of flies, and vultures soared in flocks in the air. The river was yellow with mud, and the air oppressively hot and heavy. Now and then a whiff of putrid air was blown across the deck. The three men watched the bodies drifting past, brainless skulls, eyeless sockets, floating along many of them as if they were swimming on their backs. “It is really a fine example of the power of civilization,” said the stranger. “I don’t approve of everything that has been done, by any means. Some of the armies have treated women rather badly, but no English-speaking soldiers have done that. In fact, your army has hardly been up to the average in effectiveness. You and the Japs have been culpably lenient, if you will permit me to say so.”

===Chinese===
这时尸首顺流而下，一具接着一具。每具都有一群苍蝇随侍，秃鹫成群翱翔在空中。江水黄浊如泥汤，空气闷热得压人，腐臭的气息不时一阵阵刮过甲板。三人望着尸身漂过——脑浆流空的颅骨、眼珠无存的眼窝，好些仰面朝天漂着，倒像在仰泳似的。“这真是文明力量的一个好样板。”那位陌生人说，“话说回来，干出来的事我不见得件件赞成。有些军队对待妇女相当不像话，不过说英语的士兵可从没干过这种事。老实说，贵军的效率还够不上各军的平均水准呢——恕我直言，贵军同日本佬都宽大得不可原谅。”

===Original===
“We are only just starting out on our career as a military nation,” said Sam. “You must not expect too much of us at first. We’ll soon get our hand in. As for the Japs, why they’re heathen. They can hardly be expected to behave like Christians. But we were afraid that the war was over and that we should find nothing to do.”

“The war over! What an absurdity! I have lived in Porsslania for over thirty years and I ought to know something about it by now. There’s an army of at least forty thousand Fencers over there to the northwest and another twenty-five thousand in the northeast. The Tutonians are the only people who understand it. Their first regiments have just arrived, and they are going to do something. They say the Emperor is coming himself, and he will put an end to this state of affairs. He is not a man to stand rebellion. All we can say is that we have made a good beginning. We have laid the whole province waste, and it will be a long time before they forget it.”

===Chinese===
“我们作为一个尚武之国，才刚刚起步呢。”萨姆说，“起初你们可不能对我们期望太高。用不了多久我们就能上手。至于日本佬，他们本是异教徒，总不能指望他们像基督徒那样行事。先前我们倒一直担心仗打完了，到了这儿没事可干呢。”

“仗打完了！真是天大的笑话！我在波斯兰尼亚住了三十多年，到如今总该懂点儿行情了。西北边至少还有一支四万人的剑民大军，东北边另有二万五千人。只有图托尼亚人才明白这是怎么回事。他们的先头团队刚刚开到，就要大干一场了。听说皇帝要御驾亲征，他会亲手了结这个局面——他可不是容得下叛乱的人。眼下我们只能说开了个好头：整省整省的地方都叫我们夷成了白地，他们要忘掉这一课，日子还长着呢。”

===Original===
The journey was hot and tedious; the desolated shore, the corpses and vultures, and an occasional junk with square-rigged sails and high poop were the only things upon which to fix the eye. When at last our travelers arrived at the city of Gin-Sin, Sam learned that his regiment had proceeded to the Capital and was in camp there, and it would be impossible for him to leave until the following day. He stopped with Cleary at the principal hotel. The city was in a semi-ruined condition, but life was already beginning to assume its ordinary course. The narrow streets, hung with banners and lanterns and cabalistic signs, were full of people. Barbers and scribes were plying their trades in the open air, and war was not always in sight. Sam’s reputation had preceded him, and he had scarcely gone to his room when he received an invitation from a leading Anglian merchant to dine with him that evening. Cleary was anxious to go too, and it so happened that he had letters of introduction to the gentleman in question. He made his call at once and was duly invited.

===Chinese===
旅程又热又乏味；满目疮痍的江岸、尸首和秃鹫，还有偶尔一艘方帆高艉的戎克帆船，是仅可供眼睛落脚的东西。两位旅人终于抵达金辛城时，萨姆得知他那个团已经开赴京城、在城外扎营，他得等到次日方能启程。他便同克利里在城里首屈一指的旅馆住下。城市半毁未毁，生计却已渐渐照旧运转。狭窄的街道上挂满招幌、灯笼和天书般的字号，人头攒动。剃头匠和代书先生在露天里操着各自的营生，打仗的事并非随处得见。萨姆的名声早在他本人未到之先已经传开，他刚进客房不多时，便收到本地一位安格利亚巨商的晚宴请柬。克利里也巴不得去，恰巧他带着写给这位先生的介绍信，当即登门拜会，顺理成章也得了邀请。

===Original===
There were a dozen or more guests at dinner, all of them men. Indeed, there were few white women left at Gin-Sin. With the exception of Sam and Cleary all the guests were Anglians. There was the consul-general, a little man with a gray beard, a tall, bald-headed, gray-mustached major-general in command of the Anglian forces at Gin-Sin, two distinguished missionaries of many years’ experience, several junior officers of the army, and a merchant or two. When dinner was announced they all went in, each taking precedence according to his station. Sam knew nothing of such matters, and was loath to advance until his host forced him to. He found a card with his name on it at the second cover on the right from his host. On his right was the card of a young captain. The place on his left and immediately on the right of the host bore no card, and the consul-general and the major-general both made for it. The former got there first, but the military man, who was twice his size, came into violent collision with him, pushed him away and captured the seat, while the consul-general was obliged to retreat and take the seat on the left of his host. The whole party pretended very hard to have noticed nothing unusual.

===Chinese===
晚宴上宾客十多位，清一色是男人。其实，此时留在金辛的白人妇女已经寥寥无几。除萨姆和克利里外，宾客全是安格利亚人：有总领事，是个蓄灰胡子的矮个子；有一位统带金辛安格利亚驻军的高个子秃顶、灰白唇髭的少将；有两位资历深厚的著名传教士；有几名陆军下级军官；还有一两个商人。宣布开席时，众人鱼贯而入，各按身份地位依次入座。萨姆全然不懂这套规矩，迟迟不肯上前，还是主人硬把他推了上去。他发现自己的名卡摆在主人右手第二个餐位上。他右手边是一位年轻上尉的名卡。他左手边、紧挨主人右手的那席没有放名卡，总领事和少将双双直奔那席位而去。总领事先到一步，可那位军人块头有他两个大，同他结结实实撞了个满怀，把他一搡，夺了座位；总领事只得败退，坐到主人左手边去。满座的人都极力装作什么异样也没瞧见。

===Original===
“Rather odd performance, eh?” whispered the captain to Sam. “You see how it is. Old Folsom says he takes precedence because he represents the Crown, but the general says that’s all rot, for the consul’s only a commercial agent and a K.C.Q.X. Now the general is a G.C.Q.X., and he says that gives him precedence. Nobody can settle it, and so they have to fight it out every time they meet.”

“I see,” said Sam. “I don’t know anything about such things, but I should think that the general was clearly in the right. He could hardly afford to let the army be overridden.”

“Quite so,” said the captain. “I don’t suppose you know these people,” he added.

===Chinese===
“刚才那出戏够古怪的吧？”上尉凑到萨姆耳边低语，“你明白是怎么回事了。老福尔瑟姆（Folsom）说，他代表王冠，自然该他优先；将军却说全是胡扯，领事不过是个商务代办，外加一个 K.C.Q.X.。如今将军本人可是 G.C.Q.X.，他说这才轮到他优先。谁也断不了这桩公案，于是两人每回碰头，都非得打上一架见分晓不可。”

“明白了。”萨姆说，“这类事我一窍不通，不过依我看，将军显然占理。总不能让军队屈居人后吧。”

“正是。”上尉说，又补了一句：“这些人想必你一个也不认识吧。”

===Original===
“Not one of them, except my friend, Mr. Cleary. We only arrived today.”

“The general is a good deal of a fellow,” said the captain. “I was with him in Egypt and afterward in South Africa.”

“Were you, indeed?” cried Sam. “Do tell me all about those wars. They were such great affairs.”

“Yes, they were. Not much like this business here. Nothing could stop us in the Sudan, and when we dug up the Mahdi and threw his body away there was nothing left of the rebellion. I believe the best way to settle things here would be to dig up somebody—Confusus, for instance. If there’s anything of that kind to be done our army could do it in style.”

===Chinese===
“一个也不认识，除了我这位朋友克利里先生。我们今天刚到。”

“将军是条响当当的汉子。”上尉说，“我在埃及跟过他，后来在南非又跟过。”

“真的吗！”萨姆嚷道，“快把那几场战争原原本本讲给我听。那可是了不起的大事业啊。”

“不错，了不起。跟此地这档子事可大不一样。在苏丹，没有什么拦得住我们；等我们把马赫迪（Mahdi）从坟里刨出来、把尸首一扔，叛乱便荡然无存了。依我看，要把这儿的事情了结，最好的法子就是也刨个什么人出来——比方说孔夫子。这路活计若真派得上用场，咱们军队保管干得风风光光。”

===Original===
“It must be a very effective means of subjugating people,” said Sam.

“Yes, and would you believe it? the natives objected to it. They asked us what we would think of it if they dug up our Queen. Just think of it! The impudent niggers! As if there was any similarity in the two cases.”

“Outrageous,” said Sam.

“And even at home and in Parliament, when our general was sitting in the gallery hearing them discuss how much money they would give him, some of the members protested against our digging the old fraud up. It was a handsome thing for the general to go there and face them down.”

“It showed great tact, and I may say—delicacy,” said Sam.

“Yes, indeed,” said the captain. “That’s his strong point.”

===Chinese===
“这一定是驯服人民的极有效手段。”萨姆说。

“是啊，可你信不信？土人竟不答应。他们还问我们，倘若他们把咱们的女王刨出来，咱们会作何感想。你想想！好放肆的黑鬼！倒好像这两码事有半点相像似的。”

“岂有此理。”萨姆说。

“甚至在本土的议会里，我们那位将军坐在旁听席上，听他们商量给他多少钱的时候，还有议员抗议我们刨那个老骗子的事呢。将军能亲自到那儿，当面把他们压下去，真是漂亮之举。”

“这显示了极高的圆通，我甚至可以说——体贴入微。”萨姆说。

“可不是嘛。”上尉说，“这正是他的长处。”

===Original===
“But I suppose that the war in South Africa was even greater,” said Sam.

“Rather. Why we captured four thousand of those Boers with only forty thousand men. No wonder all Anglia went wild over it. Lord Bobbets went home and they gave him everything they could think of in the way of honors. It was a fitting tribute.”

“The war is quite over there now, isn’t it?” asked Sam.

“Yes,” answered the captain, somewhat drily. “And so is yours in the Cubapines, I understand.”

“Yes,” said Sam. “I think the Cubapine war and the South African war are about equally over.”

===Chinese===
“不过我想，南非那场战争规模更大吧。”萨姆说。

“那还用说。我们光用四万人马就俘了四千布尔人。怪不得整个安格利亚（Anglia）都为之狂热。博贝茨勋爵（Lord Bobbets）回国后，人们把想得出的荣誉搜罗了个遍，一股脑儿奉给他。这份荣典，恰如其分。”

“那边的仗如今算是打完了吧？”萨姆问。

“是的。”上尉答道，语气有些干巴巴，“据我所知，你们在古巴平的仗也一样打完了。”

“是的。”萨姆说，“依我看，古巴平战争和南非战争，打完的程度不相上下。”

===Original===
“Do you see that lieutenant there between your friend and the parson?”

“Yes.”

“He got the Victorious Cross in South Africa. He saved a sergeant’s life under fire. You see his cross?”

“How interesting!” said Sam. “He must be a hero.”

“That chap with the mustache at the bottom of the table really did more once. He saved three men from drowning in a shipwreck in the Yellow Sea. He’s got a medal for it.”

“Why doesn’t he wear it, too?” asked Sam.

“Civilians never do,” said the captain. “It would look rather odd, wouldn’t it, for him to wear a lifesaving medal? You may be sure he keeps it locked up somewhere and never talks about it.”

“It is strange that civilians should be so far behind military men in using their opportunities,” said Sam.

===Chinese===
“你看见你朋友和那位牧师之间的那个中尉了吗？”

“看见了。”

“他在南非得了胜利十字勋章（Victorious Cross）。他在枪林弹雨下救过一位中士的命。瞧见他戴的十字了吧？”

“多有意思！”萨姆说，“他一定是个英雄。”

“坐在桌子下手那位留小胡子的小子，有一回干的其实更了不起。黄海一场海难里，他救起了三个快淹死的人，还为这个得了一枚奖章。”

“那他怎么不也戴上呢？”萨姆问。

“平民从来不戴。”上尉说，“让他挂一枚救生奖章，瞧着未免怪怪的，是不是？你尽管放心，那枚奖章他准锁在什么地方，从来不提。”

“真奇怪，平民在利用机会这方面，竟远远落在军人后面。”萨姆说。

===Original===
“That old fellow with the long beard is Cope, the inventor of the Cope gun. He’s a wonder. He was out here in the employ of the Porsslanese Government. Most of their artillery was designed by him. What a useful man he has been to his country! First he invented a projectile that could go through any steel plate then known, and all the navies had to build new steel-clad ships on a new principle that he had invented to prevent his projectiles from piercing them. Then what does he do, but invent a new projectile that could go through that, and they had to order new guns for it and build new ships to withstand it. He’s done that four times. And he’s got a rifle now that will penetrate almost anything. If you put two hundred Porsslanese of the same height in a row it would go through all their heads at five hundred yards. I hope they’ll try the experiment before this affair is over.”

===Chinese===
“那个长胡子老头是科普（Cope），科普炮的发明人。他是个奇才。他当初受雇于波斯兰尼亚政府，他们的火炮大半出自他的设计。他对自己的祖国可真是个大有用处的人！起初他发明了一种炮弹，能穿透当时已知的任何钢板；于是各国海军只好按他发明的新原理，另造新的装甲舰，好挡住他的炮弹。接下来他又干了什么？又发明一种连那也穿得透的新炮弹；于是大家又得为新炮弹订购新炮，再造抗得住它的新舰。这套把戏他玩了四回。如今他手里还有一支几乎无坚不穿的步枪。你让二百个一般高的波斯兰人排成一排，五百码开外，这一枪能挨个儿打穿他们所有的脑袋。但愿这场风波了结之前，他们能试一试这个实验。”

===Original===
The major-general had by this time exhausted all possible subjects of conversation with his host and sat silent, and Sam felt obliged to turn his attention to him, and was soon engaged in relating his experience in the Cubapines. Meanwhile Cleary had been conversing with the brave young lieutenant at his side and the reverend gentlemen beyond him. They had been discussing the slaughter of the Porsslanese, the lieutenant sitting back from the table while his neighbors talked across him.

===Chinese===
这时少将同主人把一切可能的话题谈尽，坐着没了言语；萨姆觉得不好冷落他，便把注意力转了过去，不久就讲起自己在古巴平的阅历来。另一边，克利里一直同身旁那位勇敢的年轻中尉以及再过去那几位可敬的教士交谈。他们谈论的正是屠杀波斯兰人的事；中尉往后靠在椅背上，任两位邻座隔着他说东道西。

===Original===
“I confess,” said the Rev. Mr. Parker, “that I am not quite satisfied with our position here. This wholesale killing of noncombatants is revolting to me. Surely it can not be Christian.”

“I have had some doubts about it too,” said the young man. “I don’t mind hitting a man that hits back. I didn’t object to the pig-sticking in South Africa, and I believe that man-hunting is the best of all sports; but this killing of people who don’t resist, and even smile in a sickly way while you do it and almost thank you—it really does go against me.”

“Yes,” said Cleary, “perhaps there is something in that.”

===Chinese===
“恕我直言，”帕克牧师（Rev. Mr. Parker）说，“我对我们在此地的所作所为并不十分心安。这种对非战斗人员的大规模屠戮令我作呕。这断乎算不得基督徒所为。”

“这事我也犯过嘀咕。”年轻人说，“打还手的人，我不在乎。在南非猎野猪我没有二话，我还相信猎人是所有运动中最上等的；可是杀那些并不抵抗的人——你动手时，他们甚至还惨然微笑，几乎要向你道谢——这实在叫我心里过不去。”

“是啊，”克利里说，“这话也许有点道理。”

===Original===
“Oh, my dear young friend!” cried the clergyman, turning toward the lieutenant, “you don’t know what joy it gives me to hear you say that. I have spoken in this way again and again, and you are the first man I have met who agrees with me. Won’t you let your fellow officers know what you think? It will come with so much more force from a military man, and one of your standing as a V.C. Won’t you now tell this company that you think we are going too far?”

“Really, Doctor,” said the young man, blushing, “really, I think you exaggerate my importance. It wouldn’t do any good. Perhaps I have said a little more to you than I really meant. This champagne has gone to my head a little.”

“Just repeat what you said to us. I will get the attention of the table.”

“No, Doctor, for God’s sake don’t!” cried the lieutenant, laying his right hand on the missionary’s arm while he toyed with his cross with the other. “To tell you the truth, I haven’t the courage to say it. They would think I was crazy. I would be put in Coventry. I have no business to make suggestions when a general’s present.”

Mr. Parker sighed and did not return to the subject.

===Chinese===
“哦，我亲爱的年轻朋友！”牧师嚷道，转向中尉，“你不知道，听你这么说，我心里有多欢喜。这样的话我讲了一遍又一遍，你是头一个赞成我的人。可愿意让你军中同僚也知道你的想法？这话出自军人之口，尤其出自你这样一位佩戴胜利十字的人物之口，分量不知要重多少！现在就请告诉满座宾客，说你觉得我们做得太过分了，好不好？”

“说真的，博士，”年轻人红着脸说，“说真的，您把我的分量看得太重了。说了也无济于事。方才我对您说的话，兴许比心里想的多说了一点。这香槟有点上头。”

“把方才的话原样再说一遍吧。我来唤起满桌的注意。”

“别，博士，看在上帝分上千万别！”中尉嚷道，右手按住牧师的胳膊，另一只手捻弄着胸前的十字勋章，“跟您说句实话，我没有胆量说出口。他们会当我疯了。我会被大伙儿晾到没处站。有将军在场，哪轮得着我来提意见。”

帕克先生叹了口气，没有再回到这个话题。

===Original===
After dinner Sam was introduced to Canon Gleed, another missionary, who seemed to be on very good terms with himself, and stood rubbing his hands with a benignant smile.

===Chinese===
饭后，有人把萨姆引见给另一位传教士格利德法政牧师（Canon Gleed）。那副样子，倒像同他自己相处得再好不过：站在那儿搓着双手，满面慈祥的笑容。

===Original===
“These are great days, Colonel Jinks,” he said. “Great days, indeed, for foreign missions. What would St. John have said on the island of Patmos if he could have cabled for half-a-dozen armies and half-a-dozen fleets, and got them too? He would have made short work of his jailers. As he looks down upon us tonight, how his soul must rejoice! The Master told us to go into all nations, and we are going to go if it takes a million troops to send us and keep us there. You are going on to the Capital tomorrow? You will meet a true saint of the Lord there, your own fellow countryman, the Rev. Dr. Amen. He is a true member of the Church Militant. Give him my regards when you see him.”

===Chinese===
“如今可是伟大的日子，金克斯上校。”他说，“千真万确，是海外传教的伟大日子。倘若圣约翰（St. John）当年在拔摩岛（Patmos）上能拍电报调六支陆军、六支舰队，而且真调来了，你想他会怎么说？他会三下五除二就把看守收拾利索。今夜他从天上俯瞰我们，他的灵魂该怎样欢欣鼓舞啊！主吩咐我们往万民中去，哪怕要派一百万大军把我们送去、再把我们留在那儿，我们也非去不可。您明天就要上京城去？您会在那儿见到一位真正的主的圣徒，您的同胞，阿门牧师博士（Rev. Dr. Amen）。他是战斗教会（Church Militant）一名真正的战士。见到他时代我致意。”

===Original===
“I see there is another clergyman here,” said Sam, looking at Mr. Parker.

“Yes, and I must say I am surprised to see him. Let me warn you, Colonel. He is, I fear, altogether heterodox. I don’t know what kind of Christianity he teaches, but he has actually kept on good terms with the Porsslanese near his mission throughout all these events. He is disloyal to our flag, there can be no question of it, and he openly criticizes the actions of our governments. He should not be received in society. He ought to be sent home—but, hist! someone is going to sing.”

===Chinese===
“我看这儿还有一位牧师。”萨姆说，朝帕克先生望了一眼。

“是的，而且我得说，看见他在这儿我很意外。容我提醒您一句，上校。依我看，此人十足是个异端。不知道他传的是哪一路基督教，可这许多事变当中，他居然同他传教站左近的波斯兰人一直和睦相处。他对我们的旗帜不忠，这一点毫无疑义，他还公然批评我们各国政府的行动。社交场中不该接待这种人。他早该被遣送回国——嘘！有人要唱歌了。”

===Original===
It was the young lieutenant who had seated himself at the piano and was clearing his throat as he ran his hands over the keys. Then he began to sing in a rather feeble voice:

> “Let the Frenchy sip his cognac in his caffy,

 Let the Cossack gulp his kvass and usquebaugh;

 Let the Prussian grenadier

 Swill his dinkle-doonkle beer,

 And the Yankee suck his cocktail through a straw,

 Through a straw,

 And the Yankee suck his cocktail through a straw.

> “Let the Ghoorka drink his pugaree and pukka,

 Let the Hollander imbibe old schnapps galore.

 Tommy Atkins is the chap

 Who has broached a better tap,

 For he takes his ’arf-and-’arf in blood and gore.

 Blood and gore,

 For he takes his ’arf-and-’arf in blood and gore.

> “When at ’ome he may content himself with whisky,

 But if once he lands upon a foreign shore—

 On the Nile or Irrawady—

 He forgets his native toddy,

 And he takes his ’arf-and-’arf in blood and gore.

 Blood and gore,

 And he takes his ’arf-and-’arf in blood and gore.

> “He’s a connoisseur of every foreign vintage,

 From the claret of the fat and juicy Boer

 To the thicker nigger brand

 That he spills upon the sand,

 When he draws his ’arf-and-’arf in blood and gore.

 Blood and gore,

 When he draws his ’arf-and-’arf in blood and gore.”

===Chinese===
在钢琴前坐下、一面在琴键上溜着手一面清嗓子的，正是那位年轻中尉。随后他用相当有气无力的嗓音唱了起来：

> “让法国佬在咖啡间里小口啜他的科涅克白兰地，

 让哥萨克大口灌他的格瓦斯和老威士忌；

 让普鲁士的掷弹兵

 牛饮他的叮当嘟当啤酒，

 让扬基佬用麦秆嘬他的鸡尾酒，

 用麦秆嘬，

 让扬基佬用麦秆嘬他的鸡尾酒。

> “让廓尔喀兵喝下他的包头巾和“帕卡”，

 让荷兰佬开怀畅饮陈年杜松子酒。

 汤米·阿特金斯那小子

 才敲开了更妙的酒桶塞，

 因为他喝的半半酒是血污和脑浆。

 血污和脑浆，

 因为他喝的半半酒是血污和脑浆。

> “在家时他喝点威士忌便能心满意足，

 可一旦他踏上异国的海岸——

 在尼罗河上或伊洛瓦底江畔——

 他便忘了家乡的热托迪酒，

 喝起他的血污脑浆半半酒。

 血污和脑浆，

 喝起他的血污脑浆半半酒。

> “他是各国名酿的品鉴行家，

 从肥美多汁的布尔人的红葡萄酒，

 到更醇厚的黑鬼牌，

 泼洒在黄沙上的那一款，

 每当他汲起血污脑浆半半酒。

 血污和脑浆，

 每当他汲起血污脑浆半半酒。”

===Original===
“Fine, isn’t it!” exclaimed Sam’s neighbor, the captain, who was standing by him, as they all joined in hearty applause. “I tell you Bludyard Stripling ought to be our poet laureate. He’s the laureate of the Empire, at any rate. Why, a song like that binds a nation together. You haven’t any poet like that, have you?”

“No-o,” answered Sam, thinking in shame of Shortfellow, Slowell, and Pittier. “I’m afraid all our poets are old women and don’t understand us soldiers.”

“Stripling understands everything,” said the captain. “He never makes a mistake. He is a universal genius.”

“I don’t think we ever drink cocktails with a straw,” ventured Sam.

“Oh, yes, you must. He never makes a mistake. You may be sure that, before he wrote that, he drank each one of those drinks, one after another.”

“Quite likely,” whispered Cleary to Sam, as he came up on the other side.

“I wish I could hear it sung in Lunnon,” said the captain. “A chorus of duchesses are singing it at one of the biggest music-halls every evening, and then they pass round their coronets, lined with velvet, you know, and take up a collection of I don’t know how many thousand pounds for the wounded in South Africa. It stirs my blood every time I hear it sung.”

===Chinese===
“好听吧，是不是！”萨姆的邻座上尉扬声赞道；这时众人都报以热烈的掌声，他正站在萨姆身边。“我告诉你，布拉德亚德·斯特里普林（Bludyard Stripling）就该封作咱们的桂冠诗人。不管怎么说，他已是帝国事实上的桂冠诗人。这样一首歌，能把整个民族拧成一股绳呢。你们没有这样的诗人吧？”

“没——有。”萨姆答道，一面羞愧地想起短费罗（Shortfellow）、斯洛威尔（Slowell）和皮蒂埃（Pittier）来，“只怕我们的诗人全是些老婆子，不懂我们当兵的人。”

“斯特里普林什么都懂。”上尉说，“他从不出错。他是位全能的天才。”

“我可觉得我们喝鸡尾酒从来不用吸管。”萨姆斗胆说。

“哦不，一定用的。他从不出错。你尽管放心，写这首歌之前，歌里那些酒，他一样一样挨着个儿全喝过一遍。”

“多半是这样。”克利里凑到萨姆另一边耳语道。

“但愿我能亲耳听见它在伦敦唱起来。”上尉说，“每个晚上，一队公爵夫人在一家顶大的杂耍剧场里合唱这首歌；唱完了，她们就把那内衬天鹅绒的小冠冕传上一圈，你晓得，为南非的伤兵募捐，一募就是不知几千镑。每回听人唱起这首歌，我的血都跟着沸腾。”

===Original===
The party broke up at a late hour, and Sam and Cleary walked back together to the hotel.

“Interesting, wasn’t it?” said Cleary.

“Yes,” said Sam.

===Chinese===
宴会到深夜方散，萨姆和克利里一道步行回旅馆去。

“有意思吧？”克利里说。

“嗯。”萨姆说。

===Original===
“Canon is a good title for that parson, isn’t it? He’s a fighter. They ought to promote him. ‘Bombshell Gleed’ would sound better than ‘Canon Gleed,’ ” said Cleary.

“ ’M,” said Sam.

“And that old general looked rather queer in that red and gilt bobtailed Eton jacket,” said Cleary.

“Yes, rather.”

“Convenient for spanking, I suppose.”

===Chinese===
“‘法政’这头衔安在那位牧师身上，再合适不过了，是不是？他是员战将。他们该给他升升级。‘炸弹格利德’总比‘格利德法政’听着响亮。”克利里说。

“唔。”萨姆含糊应了一声。

“还有那位老将军，穿着那件红底金绦的伊顿式短外套，瞧着也够古怪的。”克利里说。

“是够古怪的。”

“想必是打起屁股来方便。”

===Original===
“The captain next to me told me a lot about Bobbets,” said Sam. “Wasn’t he nearly kidnaped in South Africa?”

“Yes; that comes of sending generals away from home who only weigh ninety-five pounds. We hadn’t any such trouble with Laughter. They’d have had to kidnap him with a derrick.”

“I never thought of that,” said Sam. “Perhaps that’s the real reason they selected him. I shouldn’t wonder.”

“Of course it was,” responded Cleary.

===Chinese===
“我旁边那位上尉跟我讲了好些博贝茨的事。”萨姆说，“他在南非不是差点儿让人绑走吗？”

“可不是；这就是派体重才九十五磅的将军出国门的下场。咱们那位劳夫特（Laughter）将军可就没这份麻烦。要绑他，非得动起重机不可。”

“这我可从没想过。”萨姆说，“说不定那才是选他当统帅的真正缘故呢。我不会觉得稀奇。”

“本来就是。”克利里应道。

===Original===
“What sort of a chap was the one with the V.C. next to you?” asked Sam.

“A fine fellow,” said Cleary. “But it does seem queer, when you think of it, to wear a cross like that, that says ‘I’m a hero,’ just as plain as the beggar’s placard says, ‘I am blind.’ ”

“I don’t see why,” said Sam.

“On the whole I think that a placard would be better,” said Cleary. “Everybody would be sure to understand it. ‘I performed such and such an heroic action on such and such a day, signed John Smith.’ Print it in big letters and then stand around graciously so that people could read it through when they wanted to. I’ll get the idea patented when I get home.”

“It’s a pity we don’t give more attention to decorations at home,” said Sam. “But I don’t quite like the placard idea.”

===Chinese===
“你旁边那位戴胜利十字的是个什么样的人？”萨姆问。

“是条好汉。”克利里说，“不过细想起来也是桩怪事：挂那么一枚十字勋章，等于明明白白宣告‘我是英雄’，同叫花子胸前挂的牌子写‘我是瞎子’一般直白。”

“我不明白这有什么怪。”萨姆说。

“总的掂量下来，我看挂块牌子倒更好。”克利里说，“人人一看便懂。‘本人于某年某月某日建下如此这般一件英雄功业，签名：约翰·史密斯（John Smith）。’用大字印出来，然后雍容大度地站在一旁，让人家想看的时候从头读到尾。等回了家，我就把这个主意拿去申请专利。”

“可惜国内对勋章这桩事不够上心。”萨姆说，“不过挂牌子那个主意，我不大喜欢。”

## X A Great Military Exploit / 十 一场伟大的军事壮举

===Original===
One day while Sam was still waiting for Cleary to carry out his designs, his secretary told him that a sergeant wished to see him, and Sam directed him to show him into his office. The man was a rather sinister-looking individual, and his speech betrayed his Anglian origin.

===Chinese===
一天，萨姆还在等着克利里（Cleary）去推行他的那些计划，他的秘书进来禀报，说有个中士求见，萨姆便吩咐让人到办公室来。来人是个相貌颇为阴鸷的家伙，一张嘴便露出了英格兰（Anglian）人的出身。

===Original===
“Colonel,” said he, after the door was closed and they were alone, “I’m only a sergeant promoted from the ranks, but I’m not just an ordinary common soldier. I know a thing or two, and I’ve got a plan and I thought perhaps you would be glad to ’ear of it. I ’ave the ’abit of observing things, and most soldiers don’t. Why, bless me, you can march them into a country and out again, and with their eyes front, they don’t see a bloomin’ thing. They’re trained to see nothin’. They’re good for nothin’ but to do as they’re bid. I used to be in the army in the old country, and once at Baldershot I saw Lord Bullsley come along on horseback and stop two soldiers carryin’ a soup-pail.

“ ‘Give me a taste of that,’ says he, and one of them runs off and gets a ladle and gives him a taste. He spits it out and makes a face and shouts:

“ ‘Good heavens! man, you don’t call that stuff soup, do you?’

“ ‘No, sir,’ says the man. ‘It’s dishwater that we was a-hemptyin’.’ That’s the soldier all over again. He ’adn’t sense enough to tell him beforehand.”

===Chinese===
“上校，”门关上，屋里只剩他们两个，他才开口，“俺不过是个从行伍里提拔上来的中士，可不是那种普普通通的大头兵。俺多少有点见识，俺心里有个主意，寻思着您听了没准儿乐意。俺生来有个爱观察的习惯，当兵的十个里头九个没有。嗐，老天爷，您领着他们开进一个国家再开出来，他们眼望着正前方，连一点儿名堂也瞧不见。他们受的训练，就是教他们啥也看不见。除了叫干啥就干啥，别的啥也不顶用。俺早先在老家的军队里当过差，有一回在巴尔德肖特（Baldershot），俺瞧见布尔斯利勋爵（Lord Bullsley）骑着马过来，拦下两个抬汤桶的士兵。

“‘给我尝一口，’他说。其中一个跑去取来一把长柄勺，舀给他尝了尝。他一口啐出来，做了个鬼脸，大声嚷道：

“‘天哪！老兄，你们管这玩意儿叫汤？’

“‘不是，长官，’那士兵答道，‘这是俺们正要倒掉的刷锅水。’当兵的就是这副德行——他就没那个脑子，事先先跟人家说明白。”

===Original===
“I don’t see, sergeant, what that has to do with me,” said Sam curtly.

“Well, sir, perhaps it hasn’t. But I only wanted to say that I ain’t that kind of a man. I sees and thinks for myself. Now I ’ear that they’ve got a letter captured from Gomaldo askin’ General Baluna for reinforcements, and that they’ve got some letters from Baluna too, and know his handwritin’. I only wanted to say that I used to be a writin’-master and that I can copy any writin’ goin’ or any signature either, so you can’t tell them apart. Now why couldn’t we forge an answer from Baluna to Gomaldo and send the first reinforcements ourselves? He wants a ’undred men at a time. And then we could capture Gomaldo as easy as can be. We could find him in the mountains. I know a lot of these natives ’ere who would go with us if we paid them well.”

===Chinese===
“我看不出，中士，这跟我有什么相干。”萨姆没好气地说。

“是，长官，兴许是没相干。俺不过想说明白，俺不是那路人。俺自己长眼、自己动脑子。俺听说，咱们缴获了戈马尔多（Gomaldo）的一封信，是向巴卢纳将军（General Baluna）讨援兵的；还听说咱们手里也有几封巴卢纳的信，认得他的笔迹了。俺只想说一句：俺从前干过写字先生，眼下什么样的笔迹、什么样的签名俺都能仿，仿出来叫你分不出真假。那咱们何不伪造一封巴卢纳给戈马尔多的回信，头一批援兵由咱们自己送去？他一批要一百人。这么着，捉拿戈马尔多还不是易如反掌。咱们能上山里寻着他。这儿的当地人，俺认得一大帮，只要钱给足，就肯跟咱们走。”

===Original===
“We should have to dress them up in the native uniform,” said Sam. “I don’t know whether that would be quite honorable.”

The sergeant smiled knowingly, but said nothing.

“Do you think we could get native officers to do such a thing?” Sam asked.

“Oh, yes! Plenty of them. I know one or two. At first they wouldn’t like it. But give them money enough and commissions in our army, and they’d do it.”

===Chinese===
“那得给他们穿上当地人的军装，”萨姆说，“我说不好这算不算得上正大光明。”

中士会意地笑了笑，什么也没说。

“你看，能找着肯干这种事的当地军官吗？”萨姆问。

“嗐，能！有的是。俺就认得一两个。起先他们自然不乐意。可只要钱给够，再许他们在咱们军队里封个官，他们就干了。”

===Original===
“How different they are from us!” mused Sam. “Nobody in our army, officer or man, could ever be approached in that way.”

“It seems to me I’ve read somewhere of one of our principal generals—Maledict Donald, wasn’t it?”

Sam thought best not to hear this.

===Chinese===
“他们跟咱们多么不一样啊！”萨姆沉吟道，“咱们军队里，无论军官还是士兵，谁也不可能让人这样收买。”

“俺记得好像在哪儿读到过，咱们有位顶要紧的将军——马利迪克特·唐纳德（Maledict Donald）来着，是不是？”

萨姆只当没听见。

===Original===
“But we would have to send some of our own officers on such an expedition,” he said. “We couldn’t disguise them as natives.”

“That wouldn’t be necessary. They can go as if they were prisoners—you and two or three others you could pick out. I’d like to go too. And then I’d expect good pay if the thing went through, and a commission as lieutenant.”

“There’d be no trouble about that,” answered Sam. “I’ll think it over, and perhaps consult the general about it and let you know by tomorrow.”

“Very good, sir. I’m Sergeant Keene of the 5th Company, 39th Infantry.”

===Chinese===
“可是这样的远征，总得派几名咱们自己的军官随行，”他说，“总不能把他们化装成当地人。”

“那倒用不着。他们只管装成俘虏——您，再加上您挑得出的两三个人。俺也想跟去。话说在前头，这事儿要是办成了，俺指望一份厚赏，外加一个中尉军衔。”

“这好商量。”萨姆答道，“容我想一想，兴许再跟将军商量商量，明天给你回话。”

“好极了，长官。俺是第39步兵团第5连的基恩中士（Keene）。”

===Original===
As the sergeant went out Cleary came in, and Sam laid the matter before him.

“I know that fellow by sight,” said Cleary. “They say he’s served several terms for forgery and counterfeiting. I don’t like his looks. That’s a great scheme though, if it does seem a little like bunco-steering. It’s all right in war perhaps.”

“Yes,” said Sam. “We have a higher standard of honor than civilians. I’ll go and see the general about it now.”

===Chinese===
中士出去，克利里恰好进来，萨姆便把这件事原原本本说给他听。

“这人我打过照面，”克利里说，“听说他因为伪造文书、私铸假币，坐过好几回牢。我不喜欢他那副长相。不过这计策倒真叫高明，虽说有点儿坑蒙拐骗的味道。打起仗来嘛，兴许就不算什么了。”

“是啊，”萨姆说，“咱们军人的荣誉标准，比老百姓高出一截。我这就去见将军，把这事说给他听。”

===Original===
After some consultation the general approved the plan and authorized Sam to carry it out. The latter set Keene to work at once at forging a letter from Baluna acknowledging receipt of the orders for reinforcements and informing Gomaldo that he was sending him the first company of one hundred troops. Meanwhile he selected three officers of the Regular Army to accompany him besides Keene, and through the latter approached three native officers who had been captured at San Diego. One of these was a close confidential friend of Gomaldo’s, but Keene succeeded after much persuasion in winning them all over. It was an easier task to make up a company of native privates, who readily followed their officers when a small payment on account had been given to each man.

===Chinese===
商量了一番，将军批准了这个计划，并授权萨姆付诸实施。萨姆随即派基恩着手伪造一封巴卢纳的回信，信中确认收到请求增援的信函，并通知戈马尔多：他将派出第一批援军，一个整连一百名士兵。与此同时，除基恩之外，他又挑选了三名正规军军官随行，并通过基恩去接洽三名在圣地亚哥（San Diego）被俘的当地军官。其中一人是戈马尔多的心腹密友，可基恩费尽唇舌，终究把他们全都劝转了过来。至于凑足一连当地士兵，那就容易了——每人先支了一小笔饷钱，他们便乐得跟随长官开拔。

===Original===
“I don’t quite like the job,” Sam confessed to Cleary, “but the general says it’s all right and so it must be.”

===Chinese===
“这桩差事我不大喜欢，”萨姆向克利里坦言，“可将军说没问题，那就一定没问题。”

===Original===
At last the expedition started out. All the natives were dressed in the native uniform, and the five white men were clad as privates in the invading army and held as prisoners. After passing the outposts near San Diego they turned toward the south in the direction of the mountains where Gomaldo’s captured letter had been dated. They were received with rejoicings in each native village as soon as they showed the forged letter of Baluna and exhibited their white prisoners. The villagers showed much interest in the latter, but treated them kindly, expressing their pity for them and offering them food. They had no difficulty in obtaining exact directions as to Gomaldo’s situation, but found that it lay in the midst of an uninhabited district where it was impossible to obtain supplies, the village where he had established his headquarters being the only one within many miles. They scraped together what food they could in the shape of rice, Indian corn, and dried beef, and set out on the last stage of their journey. There had been heavy rains recently, and the mountain paths were almost impassable. There were swift rivers to cross, precipices to climb, and jungles to penetrate. The heat was intense, and the men began to suffer from it. The advance was very slow, and soon the provisions gave out. It began to seem probable that the whole expedition would perish in the mountains. Sam called a council of war, and, at Keene’s suggestion, picked out the two most vigorous privates, who went ahead bearing the alleged Baluna letter and another from Gomaldo’s renegade friend, who was nominally in command, asking for speedy succor. The two ambassadors were well schooled in what they should say, and were promised a large sum of money if they succeeded.

===Chinese===
远征队终于出发了。当地人一律穿本地军装，五个白人则扮成侵略军的列兵，被当作俘虏押解着。过了圣地亚哥附近的哨所，他们便折向南面，朝那封缴获的戈马尔多信上落款的山中进发。每到一座当地村庄，他们一亮出伪造的巴卢纳信件、一展出白人俘虏，便受到欢天喜地的接待。村人们对俘虏大为好奇，待他们却很厚道，口口声声怜惜他们，还奉上吃食。要打听戈马尔多驻地的确切位置，他们毫不费力；可随即发现，那地方坐落在一片荒无人烟的地带，粮秣无从筹措——方圆多少英里之内，只有他安营的那一座村子。他们东拼西凑弄到些大米、玉米和牛肉干，便踏上旅程的最后一程。近来连降大雨，山路几乎无法通行。湍急的河流要蹚，陡峭的悬崖要攀，密林要钻。暑气炙人，士兵们开始吃苦头了。行进极慢，粮草很快告罄。眼看着整支远征队多半要葬身山里。萨姆召集军事会议，依基恩的主意，挑出两名最精壮的士兵先行，带着那封所谓的巴卢纳信件，还有名义上统领全队的那位戈马尔多叛友的一封信，赶去求援。这两名信使该说什么话，都被反复调教周全，并得了许诺：事成之后，重金酬谢。

===Original===
For two long days the party waited entirely without food, and they were just beginning to despair, when the two men returned with a dozen carriers sent by Gomaldo bringing an ample supply of bread and meat. He also delivered a letter in which the native general congratulated his friend on his success in leading the reinforcements and in capturing the prisoners, and gave express instructions that the latter should be treated with all consideration. The carriers were commanded by a native lieutenant, who insisted that the prisoners should share equally with the native troops, and saw to it personally that Sam and his friends were served. His kindness cut Sam to the heart. After a few hours’ delay the expedition set out again, and on the following day it reached the mountain village where Gomaldo had established himself.

===Chinese===
整整两天，一行人粒米未进，正要开始绝望，那两个人回来了，还带着戈马尔多派来的十二名挑夫，驮来丰足的面包和肉食。戈马尔多还捎来一封信，这位当地将军在信里祝贺他的朋友率领援兵、押解俘虏一路顺利，并特别叮嘱务必善待俘虏。挑夫由一名当地中尉带领，他坚持俘虏应与本地士兵同等待遇，还亲自过问，看着把吃食分到萨姆和他的同伴手里。他这份好意，像刀子一样扎在萨姆心上。耽搁了几个钟头，远征队重新上路，第二天便抵达了戈马尔多盘踞的山村。

===Original===
Gomaldo’s bodyguard, composed of fifty troops neatly dressed in white uniforms, were drawn up to receive them, and the whole population greeted them with joy. Gomaldo himself stood on the veranda of his house, and, after saluting the expedition, invited the native officers who were to betray him in to dinner. At this moment Keene whispered to Sam and the latter signaled to the native officer, Gomaldo’s treacherous friend who was in charge of him, and this man gave an order in a low voice, whereupon the whole expedition discharged their rifles, and half-a-dozen of the bodyguard fell to the ground. In the meantime two of the native officers threw their arms round Gomaldo and took him prisoner, and his partisans were seized with a panic. Sam took command of his men, who outnumbered the loyal natives, and in a few minutes he had unchallenged control of the post without losing a single man, killed or wounded. Gomaldo was intensely excited and upbraided Sam bitterly when taken before him, but upon being promised good treatment he became more tractable. Sam gave orders that the villagers should bury the dead, among whom he regretted to see the body of the native lieutenant who had brought him food when they were starving; and then, after a rest of several hours, the expedition set out on the return journey, Gomaldo and his men accompanying it as prisoners.

===Chinese===
戈马尔多的卫队——五十名军容齐整、一身白色制服的士兵——列队相迎，全村百姓欢欣鼓舞地迎接他们。戈马尔多亲自站在自家游廊上，向远征队敬过礼，便请那些即将出卖他的当地军官们进屋赴宴。就在这时，基恩凑到萨姆耳边嘀咕了一句，萨姆向那位看管他的当地军官——戈马尔多那位吃里扒外的密友——递了个眼色，那人低声下了道命令，全队顿时步枪齐发，六名卫兵应声倒地。与此同时，两名当地军官张开双臂箍住戈马尔多，将他擒获，他的党羽顿时乱作一团。萨姆指挥自己的人马——他们人数压过忠于戈马尔多的本地人——不出几分钟，便未折一兵一卒、无一人伤亡地牢牢控制了这处营地，再无人敢挑战。戈马尔多激愤难当，被押到萨姆面前时把他狠狠数落了一顿，可一旦得到优待的许诺，便安分了些。萨姆吩咐村民掩埋阵亡者；他难过地看到，死者之中就有那位在他们忍饥挨饿时送吃食来的当地中尉。随后休整数小时，远征队踏上归程，戈马尔多和他的部下作为俘虏随行。

===Original===
The news of the capture preceded the party, and when, after a march of several days, they arrived at Havilla, Sam was received as a conquering hero by the army. Cleary took the first opportunity to grasp his hand.

“Is it really a great and noble act?” Sam whispered. “I suppose it is, for everybody says so, but somehow it has left a bad taste in my mouth, and I can’t bear the sight of that fellow Keene.”

“Never mind,” said Cleary. “You won’t have to see him long. We’re going to Porsslania in a fortnight, you and I, and you’ll have a chance to turn the world upside down there.”

===Chinese===
擒获戈马尔多的消息跑在了队伍前头。行军数日之后抵达哈维拉（Havilla），全军上下把萨姆当作凯旋英雄来迎接。克利里一得着机会，便紧紧握住他的手。

“这当真是一件伟大而高尚的壮举吗？”萨姆悄声问道，“想必是的吧，人人都这么说。可不知怎么，它在我嘴里留下一股怪味儿，我一看见基恩那家伙就受不了。”

“别往心里去，”克利里说，“你跟他打不了多久交道了。咱们两个，再过半个月就要去波斯兰尼亚（Porsslania）了，到了那儿，你有的是机会把世界搅个天翻地覆。”

## VIII Among the Moritos / 八 在莫里托人中间

===Original===
On the following day headquarters were moved into San Diego. Sam was lodged in the town hall with the general, and Cleary got rooms close by. There were rumors of renewed activity on the part of the Cubapinos, but it was thought that their resistance for the future would be of a guerrilla nature. There was, however, one savage tribe to the north which had terrorized a large district of country, and the general decided that it must be subdued. Sam heard of this plan, but did not know whether he would be sent on the expedition or not, and urged Cleary to use his influence so that he might be one of the party.

===Chinese===
第二天，司令部移驻圣迭戈（San Diego）。萨姆同将军一起被安置在镇公所里下榻，克利里（Cleary）则在近旁弄到了住处。有传言说古巴平诺人（Cubapinos）又要蠢蠢欲动，但大家都认为，他们今后的抵抗将带有游击的性质。不过，北边有一个野蛮部落，把一大片乡野搅得人心惶惶，将军决定非把它降服不可。萨姆听说了这个计划，却不知道自己会不会被派上这次远征，便催克利里去使动他的影响力，好让自己也跻身随队之列。

===Original===
“I’ll manage it for you, old man,” said Cleary, two or three days after the battle. “I’ve got the general in a tight place, and all I’ve got to do is to let him know it and he’ll do whatever I want.”

“What do you mean?”

“Why, he had about as much to do with the San Diego fight as the man in the moon.”

“What?”

“Well, I’ll tell you the story. I’ve run down every clue and here it is. You see somehow Colonel Burton got the orders mixed up that morning and addressed every one of them to the wrong general.”

“Is it possible?” exclaimed Sam. “That explains why they couldn’t understand the orders there in the Third Brigade, and why I took all day to find San Diego. I wonder if it’s true. Why on earth didn’t Gomaldo win then? It must have been a close call.”

“It’s plain enough why he didn’t win,” said Cleary. “That chap Garcia was one of his spies, and a clever one too. He got all he could out of you and me, but that wasn’t much. Then he had the native servant of the general in his pay. As soon as you left on the night before the battle he cleared out too, and he got a statement from the native servant of all the general intended to do. He got the news to Gomaldo by midnight, and before sunrise the Cubapino forces were ready to meet each of our columns when they advanced. They had ambushes prepared for each of them. If the orders had gone out straight we’d have been cleaned out, that’s my opinion. But you see, they all went wrong and the columns advanced along different roads, and poor Gomaldo’s plans all went to pot. I believe he had Garcia hanged for deceiving him. You haven’t seen the general’s servant since the battle, have you?”

===Chinese===
“这事包在我身上，老伙计。”战斗结束两三天后，克利里说，“我已经把将军捏在手心里了，只要让他知道这一层，他让我干什么都成。”

“你这话什么意思？”

“嘿，圣迭戈那一仗，他出的力跟月亮上住的那位老兄差不了多少。”

“什么？”

“好吧，我把原委讲给你听。我把每一条线索都查了个水落石出，真相就是如此。你瞧，不知怎么的，伯顿上校（Colonel Burton）那天早晨把命令全弄混了，每一道都发错了将军。”

“竟有这种事？”萨姆叫了起来，“这就说得通了——为什么第三旅那边看不懂命令，为什么我花了一整天才找到圣迭戈。不知道是真是假。那戈马尔多（Gomaldo）怎么反倒没打赢呢？想必就差一口气。”

“他为什么没打赢，再明白不过了。”克利里说，“加西亚（Garcia）那家伙是他的间谍，还是个精明的间谍。他能从你我嘴里掏的都掏走了，不过也没多少油水。后来他又花钱买通了将军的土著仆人。战斗前夜你一走，他也溜之大吉，还从那土著仆人嘴里套出了一份口供，将军打算干什么，全在里头。半夜之前，他就把消息送到了戈马尔多手上；太阳还没出来，古巴平诺军队已经准备停当，专等我们的各路纵队开进——每一路都给设下了埋伏。倘若那些命令发得顺顺当当，我们早叫人家包了饺子，这是我的看法。可你瞧，命令全发岔了，各纵队走的全是岔路，可怜的戈马尔多的全盘计划都泡了汤。我看他准是把加西亚绞死了，谁叫他骗了他。开战之后，你就一直没见着将军的那个仆人，对吧？”

===Original===
“Now that you speak of it, I don’t think I have,” said Sam. “But he’s a great general all the same, don’t you think so?”

“Of course,” answered Cleary.

“I wonder if all battles are won like that?” said Sam.

“I half think they are,” said his friend. “And then the generals smile and say, ‘I told you so.’”

“Cleary,” said Sam, “I want you to answer me one question honestly.”

“Out with it.”

“Did I have much to do with winning that battle or not?”

“To tell the honest truth, Sam, between me and you, I don’t know whether you did or not. But *The Lyre* will say that you did, and that will settle it for history.”

Sam sighed and made no other reply.

===Chinese===
“经你这么一说，我还真没见着。”萨姆道，“不过他照样是位了不起的将军，你说是不是？”

“那当然。”克利里答道。

“不知道是不是所有的胜仗都是这么打赢的？”萨姆说。

“我倒有一半相信正是如此。”他的朋友说，“然后将军们微微一笑，说一句：‘我早说过会这样。’”

“克利里，”萨姆说，“我要你老老实实回答我一个问题。”

“问吧。”

“打赢那一仗，到底有没有我多少功劳？”

“说老实话，萨姆——就咱俩私底下讲——我不知道有没有。可《里拉报》（The Lyre，谐音「说谎者」）会说有，历史便就此定案了。”

萨姆叹了口气，再没有别的回答。

===Original===
The expedition against the Moritos started out a week later. It consisted of two regiments, one of colored men under a certain Colonel James, the other of white volunteers, with a brigadier-general in command. Sam was assigned to the command of the volunteer regiment with the temporary rank of major, its colonel having been wounded at the battle of San Diego. For a whole day they marched northward unmolested, and encamped at night in a valley in the mountains with a small native village as headquarters. There had been little incident during the day. They had burned several villages and driven off a good many cattle for meat. Sam was surprised to see how handsome the furniture was in the little thatched cottages of the people, perched as they were on posts several feet high. It was a feast day, and the whole population had been in the streets in their best clothes. The soldiers snatched the jewels of the women and chased the men away, and then looted the houses, destroying what they could not take, and finally setting them on fire.

===Chinese===
征讨莫里托人（Moritos）的远征队一星期后开拔。队伍由两个团组成：一个团是有色人种士兵，由某位詹姆斯上校（Colonel James）统领；另一个是白人志愿兵团——全军由一位准将统带。萨姆被派去指挥志愿兵团，临时授少校衔，因为该团的上校在圣迭戈之战中负了伤。他们向北开进了一整天，无人骚扰，入夜后在山间一处谷地扎营，拿一个小小的土人村子当了指挥部。这一天没出什么大事。他们烧了几座村庄，赶走了大批牛群充作肉食。萨姆惊讶地发现，那些架在几英尺高木桩上的小小茅草屋里，家具竟如此讲究。那天正逢节庆，全村人都穿着最体面的衣裳待在街上。士兵们抢走女人的首饰，把男人们赶跑，然后洗劫了那些房屋，带不走的一律砸毁，最后再放上一把火。

===Original===
“It’s better so,” said Sam to his adjutant. “Make war as bad as possible and people will keep the peace. We are the real peacemakers.”

He heard shouts and cries as he passed through the villages, and had reason to think that the soldiers were not contented with mere looting, but he did not inquire. He took his supper with the general at his headquarters. Colonel James and Cleary ate with them, for Cleary was still true to his friend’s fortunes and determined to follow him everywhere. After an evening of smoking and chatting, Sam, Cleary, and Colonel James bade the general good night and started for their quarters, which lay in the same direction. It was a gorgeous moonlight night, such a night as only the tropics can produce, and they sauntered slowly along the mountain road, enjoying the scene.

===Chinese===
“这样反倒更好。”萨姆对他的副官说，“把战争弄得越可怕，人们就越会安分守己。我们才是真正的和平缔造者。”

穿过村庄时，他听得一片叫喊哭嚎，也有理由相信士兵们并不满足于单纯的劫掠，但他没有过问。他在指挥部同将军一起用了晚饭。詹姆斯上校和克利里也同席进餐——克利里依然一意追随朋友的气运，打定主意他走到哪儿就跟到哪儿。抽了一晚上烟、闲话了一晚上之后，萨姆、克利里和詹姆斯上校向将军道了晚安，动身回各自的住处去，三人的住处正在同一个方向。那是个璀璨夺目的月夜，唯有热带才造得出的月夜，他们沿着山间道路信步缓行，一路赏玩着景致。

===Original===
“There is a question that I have been wanting to ask you, Colonel,” said Sam to Colonel James as they walked on together. “What do you think of darkies as soldiers? I have never seen much of them, and as you have a negro regiment, you must know all about it.”

“Well, the truth is, Major,” responded the colonel, “I wouldn’t have my opinion get out for a good deal, but I’ll tell you in confidence. They make much better soldiers than white men, that’s the long and short of it.”

“How can you explain that? It’s most surprising!” cried Sam.

“Well, they’re more impressible, for one thing. You can work them up into any kind of passion you want to. Then they’re more submissive to discipline; they’re used to being ordered about and kicked and cuffed, and they don’t mind it. Besides, they’re accustomed from their low social position to be subordinate to superiors, and rather expect it than not. They are all poor, too, and used to poor food and ragged clothes and no comforts, and of course they don’t complain of what they get from us.”

“You mean,” said Cleary, “that the lower a man is in the scale of society the better soldier he makes.”

“Well,” answered the colonel, “I hadn’t ever put it just in that light, but that’s about the size of it. These darkies are great hands at carrying concealed weapons, too. If it isn’t a razor it’s something else, and if there’s a row going on they will get mixed up in it, but they’re none the worse as soldiers for that.”

“Let’s go up to that point there and take the moonlight view before we turn in,” suggested Cleary.

===Chinese===
“有个问题我一直想问问你，上校。”萨姆同詹姆斯上校并肩走着，说道，“你看黑鬼当兵当得怎么样？这种人我见得不多，你带着一个黑人团，想必门儿清。”

“唔，实话说，少校，”上校答道，“我这看法要是传了出去，麻烦可不小，不过我私下告诉你。他们当兵比白人强得多，长话短说，就是这么回事。”

“这怎么解释？太叫人吃惊了！”萨姆喊道。

“这个嘛，头一条，他们更容易受鼓动。你想让他们激出什么样的火气，就能激出什么样的火气。再一条，他们更服管教；他们挨惯了呼来喝去、拳打脚踢，满不在乎。再者，他们社会地位低下，惯于屈居人下，听命于上级简直视为当然。他们又个个穷，吃惯了粗食，穿惯了破衣，安适享受是想都不想的，我们给他们什么待遇，他们自然毫无怨言。”

“你的意思是，”克利里说，“一个人在社会阶梯上位置越低，兵就当得越好。”

“唔，”上校答道，“我倒从没这么想过，不过大致就是这么回事。这些黑鬼还个个都是私藏凶器的好手。不是剃刀就是别的什么家伙，哪儿起了斗殴，准有他们掺和；不过这倒不碍他们当好兵。”

“咱们上那边的高地上去，赏赏月色再回去睡吧。”克利里提议道。

===Original===
The others agreed, and they began to climb a path leading up to the right. It was much more of a climb than they had expected, and when they had become quite blown they sat down to recover their breath.

“I think we’d better go back,” said Colonel James. “We may lose our way, and it isn’t safe here. The Moritos are known to be thick in these mountains, and they might find us.”

“Oh, let’s go a little farther,” said Cleary, and they set out to climb again.

“The path seems to stop here,” said Sam, who was in the lead. “This must be the top, but I don’t see any place for a view. Perhaps we’d better go back.”

Cleary did not repeat his objection, and they began to retrace their steps. For some time they went on in silence.

“The path begins to go uphill here,” said Cleary, who now led. “I don’t understand this. We didn’t go downhill at all.”

“I think we did for a short distance,” answered Sam.

===Chinese===
另外两人表示赞成，于是他们开始沿一条通向右边的小路攀登。这坡远比他们预想的难爬，等爬得气喘吁吁，他们便坐下来歇口气。

“我看咱们还是回去吧。”詹姆斯上校说，“说不定会迷路，这地方也不安全。这一带山里莫里托人是出了名的多，没准儿会叫他们撞见。”

“哦，再往上走走嘛。”克利里说，于是他们又接着往上爬。

“路好像到这儿就断了。”走在前头的萨姆说，“这儿想必就是山顶了，可我瞧不出哪儿能观景。也许咱们还是回去的好。”

克利里没有再坚持己见，三人便开始往回走。有一阵子，他们都默默赶路。

“路从这儿开始又上坡了。”这回走在头里的克利里说，“这我就不明白了。咱们压根儿没下过坡呀。”

“我记得有一小段是下坡的。”萨姆答道。

===Original===
They went on, still ascending.

“There doesn’t seem to be any path here,” said Cleary. “Do you see it?”

His companions were obliged to admit that they did not.

“We’d better call for help,” said Sam, and the three men began to shout at the top of their voices, but there was no reply. An hour must have elapsed while they were engaged in calling, and their voices became husky, but all in vain.

“Hist!” said Cleary at last. “I think I hear someone coming. I heard the branches move. They have sent out for us, thank fortune! I didn’t like the idea of sleeping out here and making the acquaintance of snakes and catching fevers.”

===Chinese===
他们继续走，仍旧是往上。

“这儿好像根本没有路了。”克利里说，“你们看得见吗？”

两个同伴只得承认看不见。

“咱们还是呼救吧。”萨姆说。于是三个人扯开嗓子高声喊叫，却没有人应。他们喊了怕有一个钟头，嗓子都喊哑了，仍是徒劳。

“嘘！”克利里终于开口，“我好像听见有人来了。我听见树枝响了。他们派人找咱们来了，谢天谢地！我可不乐意睡在这荒山野地里，同蛇打交道，再染上一身热病。”

===Original===
The words, were hardly out of his mouth when three shadowy figures sprang out of the bushes and grasped each of the three men from behind, holding their elbows back so that they could not use their arms, and in a moment a veritable swarm of long-haired, half-clad Moritos were upon them, pinioning them and emptying their pockets and belts. It was quite useless to make any resistance, the attack had been too sudden and unexpected. Cleary cried out once, but they made him understand that, if he did it again, they would stab him with one of their long knives. When the captives were securely bound, the captors began to discuss the situation in their own language, which was the only language they understood. There was evidently some difference of opinion, but after a few minutes they came to some kind of an agreement. The legs of the prisoners were unbound, and they were made to march through the jungle, each one with two guards behind him, who pricked him with their lances if he did not move fast enough. Their only other arms seemed to be bows and arrows. The march was a very weary one, and through a wild, mountainous country which would have been impassable for men who did not know it thoroughly. Occasionally they seemed to be following obscure paths, but as often there was no sign of a track, and the thick, tropical vegetation made progress difficult. For an hour or two they climbed up the half-dry bed of a mountain torrent, and more than once they were ankle-deep in swampy ground. The Moritos passed through the jungle with the agility and noiselessness of cats, but the three white men floundered along as best they could. Their captors uttered never a word and would not allow them to speak.

===Chinese===
话音未落，三条黑影便从灌木丛中窜出，从背后一人一个抓住了三人的手臂，把他们的胳膊肘反剪住，叫他们动弹不得；转瞬间，一大群长发披散、半裸着身子的莫里托人已扑到他们身上，把他们捆了个结实，将他们口袋里、腰带上的东西搜掠一空。抵抗全然无用，这一下来得太突然、太出人意料了。克利里喊了一嗓子，他们便叫他明白：再喊一声，就用长刀捅他。待俘虏们被捆得结结实实，抓他们的人便议论起眼前的局面来，用的是他们自己的语言——那也是他们唯一懂得的语言。他们显然意见有些分歧，但几分钟后总算达成了一致。俘虏们腿上的绳索被解开，被押着穿过丛林行军，每人身后跟着两名看守，走得不够快，长矛便戳将过来。看守们除长矛外仅有的武器似乎就是弓箭。这一路走得好不疲乏，穿行的是一片荒僻的山地，不熟悉地形的人根本无路可走。有时他们像是走在隐约可辨的小径上，但同样常常看不出任何路的影子，茂密的热带草木更叫行进倍加艰难。有一两个钟头，他们沿着一条半干的山涧河床向上攀，还不止一次踩进没踝的沼泽地里。莫里托人穿过丛林，如猫一般轻捷无声，三个白人却只能深一脚浅一脚地勉强跟上。押送他们的人一言不发，也不许他们开口。

===Original===
The sun was just rising over a wilderness of mountains when they came to a small clearing in the woods, apparently upon a plateau near the top of a mountain. In this clearing there were a number of isolated trees, in each one of which, at about twenty feet above the ground, was a native hut, looking like a huge bird’s nest. A small crowd of natives, including women and children, ran toward them shouting, and now for the first time the men of the returning party began to talk too. Some of them tied the legs of their prisoners again and sat them down on the ground, while the others rehearsed the history of their exploit. It was a curious scene to witness. The men as well as the women wore their long, coarse hair loose to the waist. Some of the men had feathers stuck in their hair, and all of them were grotesquely tattooed.

===Chinese===
太阳正从莽莽群山之上升起，他们来到林中一小块空地上，那地方看样子在一座山近顶处的高台上。空地上孤零零立着几棵树，每棵树上离地约二十英尺高的地方，都架着一座土人棚屋，看上去像一个个巨大的鸟巢。一小群土人，其中也有女人和孩子，喊着叫着朝他们跑来；直到这时，归来的一伙人才也头一回开口说起话来。有的重新捆上俘虏的双腿，让他们坐在地上，其余的人便讲起他们这桩奇功的经历来。那场面看上去着实新奇。男人同女人一样，粗长的头发披散到腰际。有的男人头发里插着羽毛，人人身上都刺着奇形怪状的花纹。

===Original===
“I wonder if they’re cannibals?” said Cleary, for there seemed to be an opportunity now for conversation.

“I don’t think there are any in this part of the country,” said Colonel James. “Here comes our breakfast anyway.”

All the inhabitants of the village had been inspecting the captives with great interest, especially the women and children. Two women now came running from the group of tree-houses with platters of meat, and the crowd opened to let them approach.

“Don’t ask what it is,” said Cleary, as he gulped down his rations.

“I can’t eat it!” cried Sam.

“Oh, you must, or you’ll offend them,” said Colonel James.

And they completed their repast with wry faces. When they had finished, one of the warriors, whom they had noticed before on account of his comparative height and the magnificence of his decorations, came up to them and addressed them, to their great surprise, in Castalian. He explained to them that he was the famous savage chief, Carlos, who as head of the Moritos ruled the entire region, and that they were prisoners of war; that he had learned Castalian as a boy from a missionary in the mountains when the land was at peace; and that a palaver would be held on the following day, to which the heads of the neighboring villages would be invited, to determine what to do with them.
