He showed special interest in Sam’s red hair and mustache, and smoothed them and pulled them, asking him if they had been dyed. When he was informed that they had not, he was filled with admiration and called up his favorites to examine this wonder of nature. Sam had noticed that from the moment of his arrival he had been the object of admiration of the women, and this fact was now accounted for.

===Chinese===
“不知道他们是不是食人生番？”克利里说，因为这会儿似乎有了说话的机会。

“我看这一带没有食人的。”詹姆斯上校说，“反正，咱们的早饭来了。”

全村的居民一直饶有兴味地打量着这几个俘虏，女人和孩子尤其起劲。这时两个女人从那片树屋那边端着几大盘肉跑来，人群闪开一条道，让她们上前。

“别问这是什么肉。”克利里边往肚里吞他那份口粮边说。

“我吃不下！”萨姆喊道。

“哦，你必须吃，不然要得罪他们的。”詹姆斯上校说。

于是三人苦着脸把这顿饭吃完了。饭后，有一个武士朝他们走来——此人他们先前就注意到了，他在众人中个头较高，一身装饰也格外气派。令他们大为惊讶的是，他竟用卡斯塔利亚语（Castalian）同他们攀谈起来。他向他们说明：自己就是那位赫赫有名的蛮族首领卡洛斯（Carlos），身为莫里托人的头领，统治着整个这一带；他们是战争俘虏；他小时候国内太平时，曾跟山里的一位传教士学过卡斯塔利亚语；明天要举行一场议事会，邻近各村的首领都将应邀前来，商议如何处置他们。他对萨姆的红头发和小胡子表现出特别的兴趣，又是捋又是扯，问他是不是染的。待到得知并非染色，他满心叹服，把自己的宠臣们都叫上前来，参观这一造化奇观。萨姆早已发觉，自己从一到此地，便是村里女人们艳羡的对象；如今这一事实总算有了着落。

===Original===
The three prisoners had no reason to complain of their treatment during the day. A guard was set upon them, but the ropes by which they were tied were loosened, and they were allowed from time to time to walk about. Most of the morning they passed in much-needed sleep. In the afternoon Carlos visited them again with some of his men, and set to work to satisfy his curiosity as to their country, translating their answers to his friends. His Castalian was very bad, but so was that of his captives; yet they succeeded in making themselves understood without difficulty.

===Chinese===
白天，三个俘虏对所受的待遇倒没有什么可抱怨的。有人看守着他们，但捆他们的绳索松了松，还不时允许他们起来走动走动。上午的大部分时间，他们都用来补了一场急需的觉。下午，卡洛斯带了几个手下的又来看他们，着手满足他对他们国家的好奇心，再把他们的回答转述给自己的伙伴们听。他的卡斯塔利亚语糟得很，可他的俘虏们说的也好不到哪儿去；不过双方要把意思传达明白，倒没有什么困难。

===Original===
“Do you have houses as high as those?” he asked, pointing to the human nests in the trees.

“Yes, indeed,” said Cleary. “Near my home there is a house nearly a quarter of a mile long and twice as high as that tree, and nine hundred people live in it.”

There were murmurs of astonishment as this information was translated.

“What is that great house for?” asked the chief.

“It’s a lunatic asylum.”

“What is that?”

“A house for lunatics to live in.”

“But what is a lunatic?”

Cleary tried in vain to explain what a lunatic was. The Moritos had never seen one.

“We have plenty of such houses at home,” said Sam, “and we have had to double their size in ten years to hold the lunatics; they are splendid buildings. There was one not very far from the college where my friend and I were educated. But some of our prisons are even larger than our lunatic asylums.”

===Chinese===
“你们有跟那些一样高的房子吗？”他指着树上那些人住的鸟巢问道。

“有，当然有。”克利里说，“我家附近就有一座房子，差不多四分之一英里长，比那棵树还高一倍，里面住着九百个人。”

这番话翻译过去后，四下响起一片惊叹的低语。

“那么大的房子是做什么用的？”首领问。

“是疯人院。”

“什么叫疯人院？”

“就是给疯子住的房子。”

“那什么叫疯子？”

克利里费尽唇舌，也没能把疯子是什么讲明白。莫里托人从来没有见过疯子。

“我们老家这样的房子多得很。”萨姆说，“十年里我们不得不把它们的规模扩大一倍，才装得下那些疯子；都是些气派十足的建筑。我跟我这位朋友受教育的那所学院不远，就有一座。不过，我们有些监狱比疯人院还要大。”

===Original===
“What is a prison,” asked Carlos.

“Oh,” said Sam, “don’t you understand that either? It’s a house in which we lock up criminals—I mean men who kill us or rob us.”

“Oh, I see,” replied Carlos. “You mean your enemies whom you take prisoner in battle.”

“No, I don’t. I mean our own fellow citizens who murder and steal.”

“Do you mean that you sometimes kill each other and steal from each other, your own tribe?”

“Yes,” said Sam. “Of course people who do so are bad men, but there are some such among us.”

A great discussion arose among the natives after hearing this.

“What do they say?” asked Colonel James in Castalian.

“They say,” said the chief, “that they can not believe this, as they have never heard of members of the same tribe hurting each other.”

“We do all we can to prevent it,” said Sam. “In our cities we have policemen to keep order; that is, we have soldiers stationed in the streets to frighten the bad men.”

“Do you have soldiers in the streets of your towns to keep you from killing each other!” exclaimed the chief, in astonishment. “Who ever heard of such a thing? I do not understand it,” and, although Sam repeated the information in every conceivable way permitted by his limited vocabulary, he was unable successfully to convey the idea.

“It is strange how uncivilized they are,” he said to his friends.

===Chinese===
“什么是监狱？”卡洛斯问。

“哦，”萨姆说，“这个你也不懂？那是我们把罪犯关起来的房子——我是说，把那些杀害我们、抢劫我们的人关起来的房子。”

“哦，我明白了。”卡洛斯答道，“你说的是你们在战场上俘虏的敌人。”

“不，不是。我说的是我们自己的同胞，他们杀人越货。”

“你是说，你们有时自相残杀、自相偷盗，就在你们本部落之内？”

“是的。”萨姆说，“这样做的人当然是坏人，不过我们中间确实有一些这样的人。”

土人们听了这话，顿时议论纷纷。

“他们说什么？”詹姆斯上校用卡斯塔利亚语问。

“他们说，”首领道，“他们不能相信这话，因为他们从来没有听说过同一个部落的人自相伤害。”

“我们千方百计防止这种事。”萨姆说，“在我们的城市里有警察维持秩序；也就是说，我们派士兵驻守街头，吓唬那些坏人。”

“你们城里街上要驻上士兵，为的是不让你们自相残杀！”首领惊呼道，“谁听说过这种事？我可弄不明白。”萨姆用他那点有限的词汇所能想到的各种说法，翻来覆去把这话重述了几遍，终究没能把意思真正讲通。

“他们不开化到这等地步，真是稀奇。”他对同伴们说。

===Original===
“Do you live on bananas in your country?” asked Carlos.

“No; we eat them sometimes, but we live on grain and meat,” said Sam.

“You must have to work very hard to get it.”

“Yes, we do, sometimes twelve hours a day.”

“How frightful! And is there enough for all to eat?”

“Not always.”

“And are your people happy when they work so hard and are sometimes hungry?”

“Not always,” said Sam. “Sometimes people are so unhappy that they commit suicide.”

“What?”

“I mean they kill themselves.”

There was now another heated discussion.

“What do they say?” asked Colonel James.

“They say that they did not know it was possible for people to kill themselves. I did not know it either. It is very strange.”

“What limited intelligences they have!” exclaimed Sam.

“They say,” continued Carlos, in a somewhat embarrassed manner, “that if you are condemned to death, they wish one of you would kill himself, so that they can see how it is done.”

“There’s a chance for you, Sam,” said Cleary, but Sam did not seem to see the joke.

===Chinese===
“你们国家的人靠香蕉过活吗？”卡洛斯问。

“不；我们有时也吃香蕉，但我们靠谷物和肉食过活。”萨姆说。

“那你们必定得干非常重的活，才挣得来这些吧。”

“是的，有时一天要干十二个钟头。”

“太可怕了！那吃的东西够所有人分的吗？”

“并不总是够。”

“你们的人干这么重的活，有时还要挨饿，他们快活吗？”

“并不总是快活。”萨姆说，“有时人愁苦到极处，就会自杀。”

“什么？”

“我是说，他们弄死他们自己。”

这下又是一场激烈的讨论。

“他们说什么？”詹姆斯上校问。

“他们说，他们原先不知道人竟然能自己弄死自己。我原先也不知道。这事真怪。”

“他们的头脑何等有限！”萨姆感叹道。

“他们说，”卡洛斯接着说，神色有几分窘迫，“如果你们已经被判了死刑，他们但愿你们中间有一位肯自杀，好让他们开开眼，看看是怎么个弄法。”

“你的机会来了，萨姆。”克利里说。可萨姆好像没听出这话里的玩笑。

===Original===
“I am very sorry,” said Carlos, seating himself nearer to Sam, “I am very sorry that we may have to kill you, for I like you; but what can we do? It is a rule of our tribe to kill prisoners of war.”

“I really don’t see what they can do, if that is the case,” said Sam in English. “If that is their law, and they have always done it, of course from their point of view it is their military duty. I don’t see any way out of it. Do you?”

“It wouldn’t break my heart if they failed to do their duty in this case,” said Cleary. “For heaven’s sake, don’t tell him what you think. Let’s keep him feeling agreeable by our conversation. He’s fallen in love with you, Sam. Perhaps he’ll give you to one of his daughters and she may marry you or eat you, whichever she pleases.”

“I wish you wouldn’t joke about these things,” said Sam. “It’s a serious piece of business. There’s no glory in being tomahawked here in the mountains.”

“And I haven’t got my kodak with me either,” said Cleary.

===Chinese===
“我很难过。”卡洛斯说，把身子朝萨姆挪近了些，“想到我们兴许不得不杀掉你们，我很难过，因为我喜欢你们；可我们又有什么法子呢？杀掉战争俘虏，是我们部落的规矩。”

“果真这样的话，我实在想不出他们还能怎么办。”萨姆改用英语说，“倘若这是他们的法律，他们历来又是这么做的，那么在他们看来，这自然是他们军人的天职。我看不出有什么出路。你看得出吗？”

“这一回他们若是不尽这份天职，我才不会伤心呢。”克利里说，“看在老天的份上，千万别把你心里想的告诉他。咱们只管拿话哄他高兴。他看上你了，萨姆。没准儿他会把你配给他的哪个女儿，那位小姐是嫁你也罢，吃你也罢，全凭她高兴。”

“这种事请你别打哈哈。”萨姆说，“这是性命交关的正经事。在这山里让战斧劈死，可算不得什么荣耀。”

“再说，我的柯达相机也没带在身上。”克利里说。

===Original===
“What made you come into my country?” asked Carlos. “Did you not know how powerful I am? And what have I ever done against you?”

“We came because we were ordered to,” said Sam.

“And do you do what you are ordered to, whether you approve of it or not?”

“Of course we do.”

“That is very strange,” said Carlos. “We never obey anybody unless we want to and think he is doing the right thing. I tell my men here what I want to do, and if they agree to it they obey me, but if they don’t I give it up. But you do things that you think are wrong and foolish because you are ordered to. It is very strange!”

“We are military men,” said Sam. “It requires centuries of civilization to understand us.”

“How do you kill your prisoners?” asked Carlos.

“We don’t kill them,” answered Sam.

“I don’t know about that, Sam,” said Cleary in English. “We didn’t take many prisoners at San Diego.”

“That’s a fact,” answered Sam, in the same language. “We didn’t take many. I never thought of that.”

“Don’t tell him, though,” added Cleary.

===Chinese===
“是什么叫你们闯进我的国家来的？”卡洛斯问，“你们不知道我有多强大吗？我又何曾冒犯过你们？”

“我们来，是因为奉了命令。”萨姆说。

“那你们凡是奉了命令的事，不管自己赞成不赞成，都照办不误？”

“那当然。”

“这真是奇怪透了。”卡洛斯说，“我们从来只在这样的情形下才服从别人：自己愿意服从，并且认定那个人做得对。我把我想要做的事告诉我的这些手下，他们若赞成，便听从我；若不赞成，我便作罢。可你们呢，明知事情又错又蠢，只因为是奉了命，便去干。真是奇怪透了！”

“我们是军人。”萨姆说，“要理解我们，得有几世纪的文明打底。”

“你们怎么处死你们的俘虏？”卡洛斯问。

“我们不杀俘虏。”萨姆答道。

“这话我可不敢说，萨姆。”克利里用英语说，“在圣迭戈我们可没抓几个俘虏。”

“这倒不假。”萨姆用同一种语言答道，“是没抓几个。这一点我从来没想过。”

“不过，别告诉他。”克利里又叮嘱一句。

===Original===
“But when you soldiers have to execute an enemy for any reason, how do you do it?”

“We shoot them with rifles,” said Sam.

“Is that all?”

“No; we make them dig their graves first,” interposed Cleary. “That’s a hint to him,” he whispered. “It’s better than the stew pot.”

“Dig their graves first!” exclaimed the chief, and he turned to his men and explained the matter to them. They were evidently delighted.

“What are they saying?” asked James again.

“They say that that is a grand idea, and that they will adopt it. They think civilization is a great thing, and they want to be civilized,” said Carlos.

“There, I knew they weren’t cannibals!” said the colonel.

There was silence for several minutes, and Carlos smoothed Sam’s locks with his hand.

===Chinese===
“可你们军人不管因为什么要处决一个敌人时，是怎么个处决法？”

“我们用步枪枪毙他们。”萨姆说。

“就这些？”

“不止；我们先叫他们把自己的坟坑挖好。”克利里插嘴道。“这是给他递个话儿。”他悄声道，“总比下炖锅强。”

“先挖好自己的坟！”首领惊呼道，随即转向他的手下，把这件事讲解给他们听。他们显然高兴极了。

“他们在说什么？”詹姆斯又问。

“他们说，这是个了不起的主意，他们要把它学过来。他们觉得文明是件了不得的东西，他们也想开化开化。”卡洛斯说。

“瞧见没有，我早说他们不是食人生番！”上校说。

随后静了好几分钟，卡洛斯用手摩挲着萨姆的头发。

===Original===
“We must entertain him,” said Cleary. “Say something, Sam, or he’ll get down on us.”

“Say something yourself,” said Sam, who was thoroughly vexed at his friend’s ill-timed flippancy.

“Does your tribe live in these mountains and nowhere else?” asked Cleary.

“Oh, no. We have brothers everywhere. They are in all the islands, and all over the world.”

“You tell them by your language, I suppose.”

“No, some of them do not speak our language. That makes no difference. We tell our brothers in other ways.”

“How?” said Cleary.

“There are four marks of the true Morito,” said the chief. “Their young men are initiated by torture. That is one mark. Then their chief men wear feathers on their heads. That is the second. And the third mark is that they are tattooed, as I am,” and he pointed to the strange figures on his naked chest; “and the fourth is that they all use the sacred tom-tom when they dance.”

===Chinese===
“咱们得让他高兴。”克利里说，“随便说点什么，萨姆，不然他就要记恨咱们了。”

“你怎么不说。”萨姆说，朋友这时候还轻嘴薄舌，他气得够呛。

“你们部落只住在这些山里，别处都没有吗？”克利里问。

“哦，不。我们的兄弟遍布四方。各处岛屿上都有，全世界都有。”

“我猜，你们是凭语言认出他们的吧。”

“不，他们有些不会说我们的话。这不要紧。我们另有法子认我们的兄弟。”

“什么法子？”克利里问。

“真正的莫里托人有四个记号。”首领说，“他们的年轻人要受酷刑的入门礼，这是一个记号。再者，他们的头面人物头上插羽毛，这是第二个。第三个记号是他们身上都刺了花纹，同我一样。”他指着自己赤裸胸膛上那些奇怪的图形，“第四个是，他们跳舞时全都要敲神圣的手鼓。”

===Original===
“Sam,” said Cleary, “have you got those East Point photographs in your pocket?”

“Yes,” said Sam, thrusting his hand into his bosom.

Cleary rolled over to Carlos as well as his ropes would allow, threw his arms about his neck, and cried out in Castalian, “Oh, my brother, my long-lost brother!”

There was a general commotion. The savages drew their knives, and for a moment there seemed to be danger for the prisoners.

“What on earth are you trying to do, Mr. Cleary?” exclaimed Colonel James. “It seems to me that your pleasantries are in very doubtful taste while our lives are in the balance.”

Cleary made no answer, but went on crying, “Oh, my brothers, my long-lost brothers!”

“What do you mean?” ejaculated Carlos, in a rage. “I will give you one minute in which to explain, and then your head will fall.”

===Chinese===
“萨姆，”克利里说，“你口袋里带着那些东点（East Point）的照片吗？”

“带着。”萨姆说着，把手伸进怀里。

克利里就着身上绳索所允许的幅度，朝卡洛斯那边蹭过去，一把搂住他的脖子，用卡斯塔利亚语高声喊道：“哦，我的兄弟，我失散多年的兄弟！”

顿时一片大乱。野蛮人纷纷抽出刀来，有一瞬间，俘虏们眼看性命难保。

“你到底要干什么，克利里先生？”詹姆斯上校喝道，“依我看，咱们性命悬于一线的当口，你这些打趣的话实在开得不是滋味。”

克利里不答话，只顾一个劲儿地喊：“哦，我的兄弟们，我失散多年的兄弟们！”

“你这是什么意思？”卡洛斯怒喝道，“我给你一分钟把话说明白，过后就叫你的脑袋搬家。”

===Original===
“We are your brothers. We are Moritos. We are your people from a distant island, and you never knew it!”

“Is this true?” asked the chief, looking at Sam and the colonel.

“Swear to it,” whispered Cleary.

“We swear that it is true,” replied the two officers.

“Then prove it, or you shall all three die tonight. I am not to be trifled with. Proceed.”

“Señor,” said Cleary, “you have said that you recognize Morito young men by the fact that they have passed through the torture. We have passed through the torture. My friend will show you the pictures taken of both of us when we were about to be burned at the stake, and also one of himself passing through the ordeal of water. Sam, show him the photos.”

Sam took the two pictures from his pocket and handed them to Cleary, who held them in his hand while Carlos peered over his shoulder.

“You see here,” he said, “that we are tied to the stake. You may recognize our features. You see the expression of pain on our faces. These men standing around are our elder brothers who initiated us. It was done by night in a sacred grove where our ancestors have indulged in these rites for many ages. That wall is part of a ruin of a temple to the god of war.”

Carlos evidently was impressed. He took the dim print, with its fitful lantern-light effects, and studied it, comparing the faces with those of his prisoners. Then he showed it to his followers, and they all spoke together.

===Chinese===
“我们就是你们的兄弟。我们就是莫里托人。我们是从一座遥远岛屿上来的、你们自己的人，而你们从来不知道！”

“这是真的吗？”首领问，眼睛看着萨姆和上校。

“赌咒发誓。”克利里低声道。

“我们发誓，这是真的。”两位军官答道。

“那就拿出凭据来，不然你们三个今夜都得死。我可不是能随意戏弄的。说吧。”

“塞尼奥尔（Señor），”克利里说，“您说过，你们凭受过酷刑入门这一条，来认莫里托的年轻人。我们正是受过酷刑入门的。我的朋友这儿有几张照片，拍的是我们两人即将被绑上火刑柱烧死时的情形，还有一张，拍的是他本人正在经受水刑考验。萨姆，把照片给他看。”

萨姆从口袋里掏出那两张照片，递给克利里；克利里拿在手里，卡洛斯从他肩膀后面凑过来端详。

“您看这儿，”他说，“我们绑在柱子上。您也许认得出我们的相貌。您看我们脸上痛苦的表情。站在四周的这些人，是给我们行入门礼的兄长们。仪式是夜里在一座圣林中举行的，我们的祖先在那儿行这套礼节，已经不知多少代了。那堵墙，是一座战神庙废墟的一部分。”

卡洛斯显然被打动了。他接过那张影像昏暗、带着摇曳灯笼光效果的照片，细细揣摩，把照片上的面孔同俘虏们的面孔一一比对。然后他拿给随从们看，众人顿时你一言我一语议论起来。

===Original===
“They say,” said their chief at last, “that they believe you speak the truth. But how do we know that the old man was initiated too?”

“He is an old man,” said Cleary. “He had a picture like this in his pocket when he was young. We all carry them with us as long as they hold together. But they will wear out. You may see that this one is wearing out already.”

“That is true,” assented the chief. “But your picture proves against you as well as for you. You have no feathers in your heads there, and you are wearing none now,” and he proudly straightened up those on his head.

“In our country we have not many feathers as you have here,” answered Cleary. “The birds do not come often to that land, it is so cold. Only our greatest men wear feathers. When we reach home and grow old and wise and valiant, perhaps we shall all have feathers. This old warrior of ours has feathers at home, but he does not carry them on journeys. My young friend and I are yet too young. We have a picture of our old friend here with his feathers.”

“Good heavens!” exclaimed Sam. “What are you driving at. We’ll be worse off than ever now.”

“Just you let me manage this affair,” said Cleary. “Give me that photo of the dress-parade at East Point that you showed me last week.”

Sam did as he was told. It represented the dress-parade at sunset, the companies drawn up in line at parade-rest and the band in full blast going through its evolutions in the foreground, with a peculiarly magnificent drum-major in bearskin hat and plumes at the head, swinging a gorgeous baton.

Cleary exhibited it to Carlos.

“There is our elderly friend,” said he, indicating the drum-major. “He is leading the national war-dance of our people. There is the tom-tom,” he added triumphantly, pointing at the bass-drum, which was fortunately presented in full relief.

===Chinese===
“他们说，”首领终于开口，“他们相信你们讲的是实话。可这位老人也受过入门礼，我们又怎么知道呢？”

“他上了年纪啦。”克利里说，“他年轻时，口袋里也带着一张这样的照片。这种照片，只要还没磨烂，我们人人都随身带着。可它们总会磨坏的。您看，这一张就已经开始磨损了。”

“这话不假。”首领表示同意，“可你们这张照片，既替你们作证，也拆你们的台。照片上你们头上没有羽毛，如今你们头上也没插着。”说着，他得意地把头上的羽毛扶得笔直。

“在我们国家，羽毛不像你们这儿这么多。”克利里答道，“我们那地方太冷，鸟儿不常去。只有我们最伟大的人物才插羽毛。等我们回到家乡，上了年纪，又添了智慧，又建了武功，那时节，我们兴许人人都有羽毛。我们这位老武士在家里是有羽毛的，只是出门上路不带了。我这位年轻朋友和我呢，年纪还太轻。我们这儿有一张照片，就是我们这位老朋友插着羽毛的样子。”

“天哪！”萨姆失声道，“你这是要搞什么名堂？这下咱们的处境要比先前更糟了。”

“这事你只管交给我来办。”克利里说，“把你上礼拜给我看的那张东点阅兵照片拿给我。”

萨姆依言照办。照片上是日落时分的阅兵式：各连队成横列、以稍息姿势立定，军乐队鼓号齐鸣，正在前景中变换队形，队首是一位格外威风的军乐队指挥，头戴熊皮帽，帽上翎羽招展，手中挥着一根华丽的指挥棒。

克利里把照片拿给卡洛斯看。

“这位就是我们的老朋友。”他指着那位军乐队指挥说，“他正领着我们全族跳国舞。那就是手鼓。”他得意洋洋地补充道，手指着那面大鼓——幸好那面大鼓正对着镜头，拍得醒目立体。

===Original===
Carlos was taken aback, and he made a guttural exclamation of surprise.

“Do you dress like that when you are at home?” he asked of Colonel James.

“I do,” replied the colonel majestically.

“Then I bow down before you,” said the chief, kneeling down and touching the ground with his forehead three times. “But,” he added, as he rose to his feet, “you have not yet proved that we are brothers. Where are your tattoo-marks? Look at mine!”

“Sam, strip,” whispered Cleary, and Sam tore off his coat and shirt, displaying the masterpieces of the artistic boatswain. A cry of admiration went up from the assembled savages. Carlos rushed at him, threw his arms about his neck, and rubbed his nose violently against his.

“For heaven’s sake, save me, Cleary!” cried Sam. “My nose will be worse than Saunder’s, and Marian is prejudiced against damaged noses.”

Cleary thought it best not to interfere, and finally the chief grew tired of this exercise. He hardly paid any attention while Cleary showed the modest tattoo-marks on his arms, and Colonel James exhibited equally insignificant symbols on his, for he, too, had been tattooed in his youth. He was too much engrossed in Sam’s red hair and his variegated cuticle.

“Here is the picture of the water-ordeal which you forgot to look at,” said Cleary, as he collected the photographs. “This is my friend again with his head in the water and his legs stretched out in supplication to the god of the temple.”

Carlos looked at it in ecstasy.

“Oh, my brothers!” he cried. “To think that I should not have known you! You torture each other just as we do. You are tattooed just as we are! You have bigger feathers and bigger dances and bigger tom-toms. You are bigger savages than we are! Come, let us feast together.”

===Chinese===
卡洛斯愣住了，喉咙里发出一声惊异的咕噜声。

“你们在家里都是这身打扮？”他问詹姆斯上校。

“正是。”上校威严地答道。

“那我要向你下拜了。”首领说着，跪下去，以额头触地三次。“不过，”他站起身来又说道，“你们还没有证明我们就是兄弟。你们的刺花在哪儿？瞧我的！”

“萨姆，脱衣服。”克利里低声道。萨姆一把扯掉外衣和衬衫，露出那位手艺高明的水手长留下的杰作。围拢来的野蛮人齐声发出一阵赞叹。卡洛斯猛冲上前，搂住他的脖子，用自己的鼻子在他鼻子上狠狠地蹭。

“看在老天的份上，救救我，克利里！”萨姆喊道，“我这鼻子要变得比桑德斯（Saunder）的还惨了，玛丽安（Marian）对破鼻子是有偏见的。”

克利里觉得还是不去干预为好，好在那首领终于蹭累了。克利里亮出自己胳膊上不起眼的刺花，詹姆斯上校也给他看自己胳膊上同样无关紧要的图案——他年轻时也刺过花——卡洛斯却几乎看都不看。他的心思全叫萨姆的红头发和他那斑驳多彩的皮肤占满了。

“这张是水刑考验的照片，您方才忘了看。”克利里一边把照片收拢，一边说，“这还是我这位朋友，头浸在水里，两条腿直直伸着，正向庙里的神明祈告。”

卡洛斯心醉神迷地看着照片。

“哦，我的兄弟们！”他喊道，“想不到我竟没有认出你们！你们彼此施刑，同我们一模一样！你们身上刺花，同我们一模一样！你们有更大的羽毛、更大的舞蹈、更大的手鼓。你们是比我们更大的野蛮人！来，让我们一同欢宴。”

===Original===
The repast was soon prepared in the center of the clearing. The prisoners, now unbound, washed and happy, were seated in the place of honor on each side of the chief. A huge pot of miscellaneous food was set down in the midst, and they all began to eat with their fingers, the chief picking out the tidbits for his guests and putting them in their mouths. They were so much delighted with the results of the day’s work that they ate heartily and asked no questions. When the meal was over, Cleary turned to the chief and thanked him in a little oration, which was received with great favor.

“We have found our brothers,” he said in conclusion, “and you have found yours. You believe us now when we say that we have come to bless you and not to injure you. We will not take your land. We will generously give you part of it for yourselves. You see how we all love you, the aged warrior and the redheaded chief as well as I. Why will you not come with us when we set out on our journey to our great chief, or why, at any rate, will you not send your chiefs with us, to tell him that you have received us all as brothers and that we shall always be friends and allies?”

===Chinese===
盛宴很快就在空地中央备好了。俘虏们此刻已松了绑，洗漱一新，欢欢喜喜地被安排坐在首领两侧的上座。一大锅各色杂拌的食物摆在中间，众人都伸出手指吃起来，首领专给他的客人们拣精拣肥，直接送进他们嘴里。他们对这一天办事的成果满心欢喜，一个个吃得酣畅淋漓，什么也没有多问。饭毕，克利里面向首领，发表了一小篇演说向他致谢，大受众人赞许。

“我们找到了我们的兄弟，”他末了总结道，“你们也找到了你们的。如今你们该相信我们的话了：我们是来造福你们的，不是来伤害你们的。我们不会夺走你们的土地。我们还要慷慨地把其中一部分送给你们自己。你们都看得出我们多么爱你们——那位年高的武士、这位红头发的首领，同我一样。等我们动身去见我们的大首领时，你们为什么不随我们同去呢？退一步说，你们至少也该派几位首领随我们同去，告诉他说，你们已把我们全都当作兄弟相待，我们将永远是朋友和盟邦。”

===Original===
Carlos translated this speech sentence by sentence. Cleary was a good speaker, and they were impressed by his style as well as by his argument. They palavered together for some time; then Carlos arose and addressed his guests, but particularly Sam, whom he considered as the leader.

“Brothers,” he said, “we are indeed brothers by the torture, tattoo, tom-tom, and top-feather. We did not know who you were, we did not understand you. We wished to be left in peace. We did not want to have the Castalians come here and rob us. We did not want their beads and their brandy. We wanted to be let alone. But you are our brothers. You are greater savages than we are. Why should we not go with you? The chiefs of our other villages are coming tomorrow at sunrise. I will conduct you back to your great chief with them, and we shall all rejoice together.”

It was now nearly dark. Carlos apologized for not having accommodation for his guests in his tree-hut, but provided comfortable blankets on the ground and had a fire built for them in a secluded place near the village. The three men were soon sleeping peacefully, and they did not awake until the sun had already risen.

===Chinese===
卡洛斯把这篇演说逐句翻译了过去。克利里是个出色的演说家，众人既折服于他的辞令，也折服于他的道理。他们一起商谈了好一阵子；随后卡洛斯起身，向客人们致辞，尤其是朝着萨姆——他把他当成了为首的那位。

“兄弟们，”他说，“凭着酷刑、刺花、手鼓和头顶的翎羽，我们确确实实是兄弟。我们原先不知道你们是谁，原先不理解你们。我们但愿太太平平不受打扰。我们不愿意卡斯塔利亚人到这儿来抢掠我们。我们不稀罕他们的玻璃珠子和白兰地。我们只求别来纠缠。可你们是我们的兄弟。你们是比我们更大的野蛮人。我们为什么不随你们去呢？我们其余各村的首领明天日出时一齐到这里。我将同他们一起，送你们回去见你们的大首领，我们要一同欢庆。”

这时天已擦黑。卡洛斯抱歉自己的树屋腾不出地方款待客人，便在地上铺了舒适的毯子，又在村子附近一处僻静的地方给他们生了一堆火。三人不久便安然睡去，一觉醒来，太阳已经升得老高了。

===Original===
To F. C.

===Chinese===
献给 F. C.

## II East Point / 二 东点

===Original===
But our relation to our environments will change, however much pleased we may be with them, and “Cap” Jinks found himself gradually growing too old for his brigade. The younger boys and their parents began to complain that he was unreasonably standing in the way of their promotion, and a fiery mustache gave signs to the world that he was now something more than a boy. Still he could not bring himself to relinquish the uniform and the white plume. A life without military trimmings was not to be thought of, and there was no militia at Homeville. Consequently he remained in the Boys’ Brigade as long as he could. When at last he saw that he must resign—he was now two-and-twenty—he felt that there was only one course open to him, and that was to join the army; and he broached this plan to his parents. His mother did not like the idea of giving up her only son to such a profession, but Colonel Jinks took kindly to the suggestion. It would bring a little real militarism into the family and give a kind of ex post facto justification to his ancient title. “Sam, my boy,” said he, “you’re a chip of the old block. You’ll keep up the family tradition and be a colonel like me. I will write to your Uncle George about it tomorrow. He’ll get you an appointment to East Point without any trouble. Sam, I’m proud of you.”

Uncle George Jinks, the only brother of the Colonel, was a member of Congress from a distant district, who had a good deal of influence with the Administration. The Colonel wrote to him asking for the cadetship and rehearsing at length the young captain’s unusual qualifications and his military enthusiasm. A week later he received the answer. His brother informed him that the request could not have come at a more opportune moment, as he had a vacancy to fill and had been on the point of calling a public examination of young men in his district for the purpose of selecting a candidate; but in view of the evident fitness of his nephew, he would alter his plans and offer him the place without further ceremony. He wished only that Sam would do credit to the name of Jinks.

===Chinese===
不过，人同自己周遭环境的关系，无论我们对它多么称心，终归是要变的；“金克斯上尉”发觉自己在少年旅里渐渐嫌大了。年纪小些的孩子和他们的父母开始抱怨，说他占着位子挡人晋升，太不成话；唇上生出的那一抹火红髭须也向世人表明，他如今已不止是个孩子了。然而他仍旧狠不下心来舍弃那身军装和那支白翎。没有军旅装点的人生是想也不敢想的，何况霍姆维尔又没有民兵。于是他在少年旅里能赖多久便赖多久。等到终于看清非退不可时——他这时已经二十二岁——他觉得自己面前敞开的路只有一条，那就是投军；他把这打算向父母提了出来。母亲并不喜欢把独子交给这样一门营生，金克斯上校却对这主意颇觉投缘。这能给家里添一点真正的军人气息，也给他那个古老的头衔来一个事后的追认。“萨姆，我的孩子，”他说，“你真是老木头上劈下来的一块料，有其父必有其子。你要把家传的风气撑下去，将来像我一样做个上校。我明天就写信给你乔治叔叔（Uncle George）。他会不费吹灰之力给你弄个东点（East Point）的名额。萨姆，我为你骄傲。”

乔治·金克斯叔叔是上校唯一的兄弟，在远方一个选区任国会议员，对政府颇有几分影响。上校写信给他求这个学员名额，又把这位年轻队长的种种过人资历和他的军事热忱铺陈了一大篇。一星期后，回信到了。兄弟告诉他，这封信来得再凑巧也没有：他手头正有个空缺要补，本来都打算在本选区公开考选一批青年，从中择定一人；但念及侄子条件显然合适，他愿意改变计划，不再多费周章，把这位子径直给他。他只希望萨姆日后能为金克斯这个姓氏争光。

===Original===
It was on a beautiful day in June that “Cap” Jinks bade farewell to Homeville. The family came out in front of the house, keeping back their tears as best they could at this the first parting; but Sam, though he loved them well, had no room in his heart for regret. There was a vision of glory beckoning him on which obliterated all other feelings. The Boys’ Brigade was drawn up at the side of the road and presented arms as he drove by, and he saw in this the promise of greater things. As he sat on the back seat of the wagon by himself behind the driver, he took from his pocket the old original “hero,” the lead officer of his boyhood, and gazed at it smiling. “Now I am to be a real hero,” he thought, “and all the world will repeat the name of Sam Jinks and read about his exploits.” He put the toy carefully back in his breast pocket. It had become the talisman of his life and the symbol of his ambitions.

The long railway journey to East Point was full of interest to the young traveler, who had never been away from home before. His mind was full of military things, but he saw no uniforms, no arms, no fortifications anywhere. How could people live in such a careless, unnatural fashion? He blushed with shame as he thought to himself that a foreigner might apparently journey through the country from one end to the other without knowing that there was such a thing as a soldier in the land. What a travesty this was on civilization! How baseless the proud boasts of national greatness when only an insignificant and almost invisible few paid any attention to the claims of military glory! The outlook was indeed dismal, but Sam was no pessimist. Obstacles were in his dictionary “things to be removed.” “I shall have a hand in changing all this,” he muttered aloud. “When I come home a conquering general with the grateful country at my feet, these wretched toilers in the field and at the desk will have learned that there is a nobler activity, and uniforms will spring up like flowers before the sun.” Where Sam acquired his command of the English language and his poetic sensibility it would be difficult to say. It is enough to know that these faculties endeavored, not without success, to keep pace with his growing ambition for glory.

===Chinese===
六月里一个晴美的日子，“金克斯上尉”辞别了霍姆维尔。一家人站在屋前，在这头一回的离别面前强忍着眼泪；萨姆虽然深爱他们，心里却腾不出一点儿懊悔的余地。有一幅荣耀的幻景在前方召唤他，把其余一切情感都抹去了。少年旅在路边列队，在他驱车经过时举枪敬礼，他在这一幕里看到了更大前程的朕兆。他独自坐在马车上车夫身后的后座，从口袋里掏出那个最初的“英雄”——他童年时代的铅制军官——含笑凝望着它。“如今我要做真正的英雄了，”他想，“全世界都要传诵萨姆·金克斯的名字，读他的功业。”他把这玩具小心地放回胸前的口袋。它已经成了他此生的护符、他抱负的象征。

去东点的漫长火车旅程，对这位从未离过家的年轻旅人来说处处是趣味。他满脑子都是军事，可四处竟看不到一件军装、一杆枪、一座工事。人们怎么能这样漫不经心、违反常性地过日子呢？他一想到一个外国人竟然可以在这个国家从这头旅行到那头，却不知道这片土地上竟有士兵这种东西，便羞愧得满脸通红。这对文明是何等的滑稽戏！举国自负其伟大，夸下豪言，可理会军事荣耀之号召的，只有微不足道、几乎看不见的一小撮人——这等自傲的资本又是何等没有根基！前景委实黯淡，萨姆却不是悲观主义者。在他的词典里，“障碍”一词的释义是“待清除之物”。“这一切都要经我一手来改变，”他出声喃喃道，“等我以凯旋将军之身还乡、感恩戴德的祖国拜倒在我脚下的时候，这些在田间、在书桌前苦熬的不幸劳作者便会知道，世上还有更高贵的营生，军装也会像向日的花朵一样遍地开出来。”萨姆这一套英文的本事、这一副诗人的情肠是从哪里得来的，很难说清。只消知道：这两样本事都在竭力追赶他日益膨胀的功名心，而且追赶得不算失败。

===Original===
Sam’s first weeks at East Point were among the happiest in his life. Here, at any rate, military affairs were in the ascendant. His ideal of a country was simply an East Point infinitely enlarged. His neat gray uniform seemed already to transform him into a hero. When he thought of the great soldiers who had been educated at this very place, he felt a proud spirit swelling in his bosom. One night in a lonely part of the parade-ground he solemnly knelt down and kissed the sod. The military cemetery aroused his enthusiasm, and the captured cannon, the names of battles inscribed here and there on the rocks, and the portraits of generals in the mess-hall, all in turn fascinated him. As a new arrival he was treated with scant courtesy and drilled very hard, but he did not care. Though his squad-fellows were almost overcome with fatigue, he was always sorry when the drill came to an end. He never had enough of marching and countermarching, of shouldering and ordering arms. Even the “setting-up” exercises filled him with joy. When cavalry drills began he was still more in his element. His old teamster days now stood him in good stead. In a week he could do anything with a horse—he understood the horse, and the horse trusted him. When he first emerged from the riding-school on horseback in a squadron and took part in a drill on the great parade-ground, he was prouder than ever before. He went through it in a delirium, feeling like a composite photograph of Washington and Napoleon. When the big flag went up in the morning to the top of the towering flagstaff, Sam’s spirits went up with it, and they floated there, vibrating, hovering, all day; but when the flag came down at night, Sam did not come down. He was always up, living an ecstatic dream-life in the seventh heaven.

===Chinese===
萨姆在东点的最初几个星期，是他一生中最幸福的日子之一。在这里，至少军事是当令得势的。他理想中的国家，无非是一个放大到无穷倍的东点。他那身整洁的灰军装，仿佛已经把他变成一位英雄。一想到那些恰好在此地受过教育的伟大军人，他便觉得胸中鼓荡起一股豪气。一天夜里，他在练兵场上一处冷僻的所在郑重跪下，亲吻了草皮。军人公墓激起了他的热忱，缴获的大炮、各处镌刻在岩石上的战役名字、饭厅里将军们的画像，又先后令他心醉。作为新来者，他受的礼遇寥寥，操练又极严酷，他却毫不在意。同小队的伙伴们几乎被操练拖垮，他却总在操练收场时意犹未尽。行军、折返，肩枪、放枪，他永远没有个够。连“队列体操”都叫他满心欢喜。等到骑兵操练开始，他更是得心应手。当年赶车把式的日子如今大有用处。不出一星期，他在马身上什么都做得到——他懂得马，马也信任他。当他头一回随一支骑兵中队骑马从马术学校出来、参加大练兵场上的操练时，他比以往任何时候都更骄傲。他是在一阵痴迷谵狂中把操练走完的，自觉是华盛顿与拿破仑的合成照片。清早，大旗升上高耸旗杆的顶端，萨姆的心气也随之一齐升上去，整日价飘在那里，颤动着，回旋着；可到了夜里降旗，萨姆却不肯降下来。他总是高高在上，在第七重天上过着心醉神迷的梦一般的生活。

===Original===
One night as Sam lay in his tent dreaming that he had just won the battle of Waterloo, he heard a voice close to his ears.

“Jinks!”

“Yes, sir.”

“Here is an order for you to report at once up in the woods at old Fort Hut. The password is ‘Old Gory’; say that, and the sentinel will let you out of camp. Go along and report to the colonel at once.”

“What is it?” cried Sam. “Is it an attack?”

“Very likely,” said the voice. “Now wake up your snoring friend there, for he’s got to go too. What’s his name?”

===Chinese===
一天夜里，萨姆躺在帐篷里，正梦见自己刚刚打赢滑铁卢一役，忽听得一个声音贴着他的耳朵响起。

“金克斯！”

“到，长官。”

“这儿有一道命令，命你即刻到林子里老哈特堡（Fort Hut）去报到。口令是‘老光荣’（Old Gory）；说出口令，哨兵就会放你出营。快去，立刻向堡里的上校报到。”

“出了什么事？”萨姆喊道，“是敌人来进攻吗？”

“多半是。”那声音说，“现在把你那位打呼噜的朋友叫醒，他也得去。他叫什么名字？”

===Original===
“Cleary,” answered Sam, and he proceeded gently to awaken his tent-mate and break the news to him that the enemy was advancing. It was not easy to rouse the young man, but finally they both succeeded in dressing in the dark, and hastened away between the tents across the most remote sentry beat. They were duly challenged, whispered the countersign, and in a few moments were climbing the rough and thickly wooded hill to the fort.

“I wonder who the enemy is,” said Sam.

“Enemy? Nonsense,” replied Cleary. “They’re going to haze us.”

“Haze us? Good heavens!” said Sam. He had heard of hazing before, but he had been living in such a realm of imagination for the past weeks that the gossip had never really reached his consciousness, and now that he was confronted with the reality he hardly knew how to face it.

“Yes,” said Cleary, “they’re going to haze us, and I wonder why I ever came to this rotten place anyhow.”

===Chinese===
“克利里（Cleary）。”萨姆答道，接着便轻轻地唤醒他的帐友，把敌军正在推进的消息告诉他。那小伙子颇不容易叫醒，好歹两个人在黑暗中穿戴停当，匆匆从帐篷之间穿过去，走的是最偏僻的一段哨兵巡逻线。他们照例被喝问，低声答了口令，不多时便已攀登那座粗砺而林木丛生的小山，向堡垒而去。

“我倒想知道敌人是谁。”萨姆说。

“敌人？胡扯。”克利里答道，“他们是要捉弄咱们。”

“捉弄咱们？我的老天！”萨姆说。捉弄新生的事他早有耳闻，可过去这几个星期他一直生活在想象的王国里，那些闲言从来没有真正进入过他的意识；如今当真撞见了现实，他简直不知道如何面对。

“可不是，”克利里说，“他们要来捉弄咱们了。我也不明白，我当初干吗要上这个破烂地方来。”

===Original===
“Don’t, don’t say that,” cried Sam. “You were at Hale University for a year or two, weren’t you? Did they do any hazing there?”

“Not a bit. They stopped it all long ago. The professors there say it isn’t manly.”

“That can’t be true,” said Sam, “or they wouldn’t do it here. But why has it kept up here when they’ve stopped it at all the universities?”

“I don’t know,” said Cleary, “but perhaps it’s wearing uniforms. I feel sort of different in a uniform from out of it, don’t you?”

“Of course I do,” exclaimed Sam. “I feel as if I were walking on air and rising into another plane of being.”

“Well—ye-es—perhaps, but I didn’t mean that exactly,” answered Cleary. “But somehow I feel more like hitting a fellow over the head when I’m in uniform than when I’m not, don’t you?”

===Chinese===
“别、别这么说。”萨姆喊道，“你在黑尔大学（Hale University）待过一两年，是不是？他们在那儿也捉弄新生吗？”

“一点儿也不。早就全禁绝了。那儿的教授们说，这不是男子汉的作为。”

“那不可能是真的，”萨姆说，“不然他们在这儿也就不会这么干了。可各处大学都禁绝了，为什么偏偏这儿还留着呢？”

“我不知道，”克利里说，“也许是因为穿军装吧。我穿上军装，就觉得自己跟不穿的时候不一样，你不这样吗？”

“当然这样。”萨姆嚷道，“我觉得自己像踩着空气走路，正往更高一层存在升上去。”

“呃——是——也许吧，可我不完全是那个意思。”克利里答道，“不过不知怎么的，我穿上军装就比不穿的时候更想照谁的脑袋来一下，你不吗？”

===Original===
“I hadn’t thought of that,” said Sam, “but I really think I do. Do you think they’ll hit us over the head?”

“There’s no telling. There’s Captain Clark of the first class and Saunders of the third who are running the hazing just now, they say, and they’re pretty tough chaps.”

“Is that Captain Clark with the squeaky voice?” asked Sam.

===Chinese===
“这个我倒没想到过，”萨姆说，“不过我细想想，还真觉得是这样。你看他们会照咱们的脑袋来一下吗？”

“说不准。如今操办捉弄新生这档子事的，据说是一年级的克拉克上尉（Captain Clark）和三年级的桑德斯（Saunders），两个都是硬茬子。”

“就是那个说话吱吱叫的克拉克上尉？”萨姆问。

===Original===
“Yes, he spoiled it taking tabasco sauce when he was hazed three years ago. They say it took all the mucous membrane off his epiglottis.”

There was silence for a time.

“Saunders is that fellow with the crooked nose, isn’t he?” asked Sam.

“Yes; when they hazed him last year they made him stand with his nose in the crack of a door until they came back, and they forgot they had left him, and somebody shut the door on his nose by mistake. But he’s an awfully plucky chap. He just went on standing there as if nothing had happened.”

“Splendid, wasn’t it?” cried Sam, beginning to see the heroic possibilities of hazing. “Do you suppose that they have always hazed here?”

“Yes, of course.”

===Chinese===
“对，三年前他挨捉弄的时候灌塔巴斯科辣酱（tabasco sauce），把嗓子毁了。据说那辣酱把他会厌上的黏膜全烧掉了。”

两人沉默了一会儿。

“桑德斯就是那个歪鼻子的家伙，对不对？”萨姆问。

“对；去年他们捉弄他，叫他鼻子顶着门缝站着等他们回来，结果他们忘了这茬，又有人误把门一关，夹了他的鼻子。可那是个有骨气的家伙，硬气得很。他就那么一直站着，好像什么事也没发生。”

“真了不起，是不是？”萨姆喊道，开始看出捉弄新生这桩事里蕴含的英雄气象来。“你想他们是不是从古到今都在这儿这么捉弄？”

“是啊，当然。”

===Original===
“And that General German and General Meriden and all the rest were hazed here just like this?”

“Yes, to be sure.”

Sam felt his spirits soaring again.

“Then I wouldn’t miss it for anything,” said he. “It has always been done and by the greatest men, and it must be the right thing to do. Just think of it. Meriden has walked up this very hill like you and me to be hazed!” There was exultation in his tone.

“Well, I only hope Meriden looked forward to it with greater joy than I do,” said Cleary, with a dry laugh. “But here we are.”

===Chinese===
“那么杰曼将军（General German）和梅里登将军（General Meriden），还有其余那些人，当年也都是像这样在这儿挨的捉弄？”

“是啊，那还用说。”

萨姆只觉得胸中的豪气又腾起来。

“那我说什么也不能错过。”他说，“这事从古就有，而且是那些最伟大的人干的，那它必定是正当的事。你想想看：梅里登当年就走过你我此刻走的这座山，来挨捉弄！”他的语气里透着狂喜。

“哼，我只巴望梅里登当年盼这桩事的时候，心情比我此刻快乐些。”克利里干笑着说，“反正——到了。”

===Original===
Before them under the ruined walls of the old redoubt called Fort Hut, stood a small group of cadets, indistinctly lighted by several moving dark-lanterns. While they were still twenty yards away, two men sprang out from behind a tree, grasped them by the arms, tied their elbows behind them, and, leading them off through the woods for a short distance, bound them to a tree out of sight of the rest, and left them there with strict injunctions not to move. It never entered into the head of either of the prisoners that they might disobey this order, and they waited patiently for events to take their course. As far as they could make out by listening, some others of their classmates were already undergoing the ordeal of hazing. They could hear water splashing, suppressed screams and groans, and continual whispering. The light of the lanterns flickered through the trees, now and then illuminating the topmost branches. Presently a man came and sat down near them, and said:

===Chinese===
在他们面前，在那座唤作哈特堡的旧堡垒的残垣下，站着一小群学员，几盏移动的遮光提灯把他们照得影影绰绰。离他们还有二十码，两个人影便从一棵树后蹿出来，抓住他们的胳膊，把他们双肘反剪捆住，押着在林子里走了一小段，把他们绑在一棵其余人看不见的树上，丢下他们，还严厉叮嘱不许动。两个俘虏脑子里压根没有闪过违抗这道命令的念头，只耐心等待事情按部就班地发展。凭耳朵推断，班里另一些同学已经在领受捉弄的考验了。他们听得见泼水声、压低的尖叫和呻吟，还有连绵不断的耳语。提灯的光在树间闪烁，时时照亮最高的枝桠。不多时，有个人走来，在他们近旁坐下，说道：

===Original===
“Don’t get impatient. We’re nearly ready for you.” It was the voice of one of their two captors.

“May I ask you a question, sir?” said Sam.

“Blaze away,” responded the man.

“Was General Gramp hazed at this same place, do you know?”

“Yes,” said the man. “In this very same place. And while he was waiting he sat on that very log over there.”

Sam peered with awe into the darkness.

===Chinese===
“别不耐烦。就快轮到你们了。”——正是捉他们两人的那两个中的一个的声音。

“我可以问您一个问题吗，长官？”萨姆说。

“有话就问。”那人应道。

“您可知道格兰普将军（General Gramp）当年是不是就在这个地方挨的捉弄？”

“是，”那人说，“就在这同一个地方。他等候的时候，就坐在那边那根木头上。”

萨姆满怀敬畏地朝黑暗中张望。

===Original===
“May I—do you think I might—just sit on it, too?” asked Sam.

“Certainly,” said the cadet affably, untying the rope from the tree and leading Sam over to the log, where he tied him again.

Sam sat down reverently.

“How well preserved the log is,” said Sam.

“Yes,” said the guard; “of course they wouldn’t let it decay. It’s a sort of historical monument. They overhaul it every year. Anyway it’s ironwood.”

Sam thought to himself that perhaps some day the log might be noted as the spot where the great General Jinks sat while awaiting his hazing, and tears of joy rolled softly down over his freckles. He was still lost in this emotion when steps were heard approaching and the lantern-light drew nearer.

===Chinese===
“我可不可以——您看我能不能——也去坐一坐它？”萨姆问。

“当然可以。”那学员和气地说着，从树上解下绳子，把萨姆领到那根木头跟前，重新把他绑好。

萨姆毕恭毕敬地坐下了。

“这木头保存得真好。”萨姆说。

“是啊，”看守说，“他们当然不会让它朽掉。这算得一件历史纪念物了。他们每年都检修一回。再说这是铁木。”

萨姆心里想，也许有朝一日，这根木头会作为伟大的金克斯将军当年等候受捉弄时坐过的地方而名传后世，喜悦的泪水便顺着他的雀斑轻轻滚落下来。他还沉浸在这份激动里，便听得脚步声走近，提灯的光也愈来愈亮了。

===Original===
“Come, Smith, bring the prisoners in,” said the same voice that had waked Sam in his tent. He looked at the speaker and recognized the tall, hatchet-faced, crook-nosed Saunders. Two or three cadets unfastened Sam and Cleary, still, however, leaving their arms bound behind them, and brought them to the open place under the wall where Sam had first seen them. Sam now saw nothing; walking in the steps of Generals Gramp and German, he felt the ecstasy of a Christian martyr. He would not have exchanged his lot with anyone in the world. Cleary, however, who possessed a rather mundane spirit, took in the scene. Twenty or thirty cadets were either standing or seated on the ground round a circle which was illuminated by several dark-lanterns placed upon the ground. In the center of the circle were a tub of water, some boards and pieces of rope, and two large baskets whose contents were concealed by a cloth.

===Chinese===
“来吧，史密斯（Smith），把俘虏带进来。”说话的正是帐篷里唤醒萨姆的那个声音。他朝说话人看去，认出了那高个子、斧削脸、歪鼻子的桑德斯。两三个学员解开萨姆和克利里——胳膊仍反绑在身后——把他们带到墙下那片空地上，也就是萨姆起初望见他们的地方。萨姆这时什么也看不见了；他踏着格兰普将军和杰曼将军的脚印前行，体味着一个基督教殉道者的狂喜。拿世上的任何人同他换位置，他都不干。克利里却是个心性较为俗气的人，把全场看了个分明：二三十个学员或站或坐在一圈四周，圈里靠几盏放在地上的遮光提灯照亮。圈中央有一只水桶、几块木板、几截绳子，还有两只大筐，筐中之物用一块布遮着，看不出是什么。

===Original===
“Come, boys,” squeaked Captain Clark, a short, thickset fellow who looked much older than the others and who spoke in a peculiar cracked voice. “Come, let’s begin by bracing them up.”

“Bracing” was a process adopted for the purpose of making the patient assume the position of a soldier, only very much exaggerated—a position which after a few minutes becomes almost intolerable. Cleary and Sam were promptly taken and tied back to back to an upright stake which had escaped their observation. They were tied at the ankle, knee, waist, under the arms, and at the chin and forehead. By tightening these ropes as desired and placing pieces of wood in between, against the back, the hazers made each victim stand with the chest pushed preternaturally forward and the chin and abdomen drawn preternaturally back. Cleary found this position irksome from the start, and soon decidedly painful, but Sam was proof against it. In fact, he had been practising just this position for eight or ten years, and it now came to him naturally. Cleary soon showed marks of discomfort. It was a warm night, and the sweat began to stand out on his forehead. As far as he was concerned the hazing was already a success, but Sam evidently needed something more.

===Chinese===
“来吧，伙计们，”克拉克上尉吱声说道。他是个矮墩墩、结实粗壮的家伙，看上去比其余人老成得多，说话带着一口古怪的破嗓子。“来，咱们先给他们上‘绷姿’（bracing）。”

“绷姿”是一种手段，目的在使受刑者摆出军人的姿势，只是夸张得无以复加——这姿势摆上几分钟便几乎无法忍受。克利里和萨姆立刻被拿住，背贴背捆在一根他们先前不曾留意的立桩上。脚踝、膝盖、腰身、腋下，乃至下巴和额头，处处捆到。捉弄的人随心收紧这些绳子，又在背后垫进木块，便叫每个受刑者胸膛挺得超乎自然地前凸，下巴和肚腹收得超乎自然地后凹。克利里从一开始就觉得这姿势难受，很快就明显地疼痛起来，萨姆却全然扛得住。说实在的，这个姿势他恰恰练了八到十年，如今做来自然而然。克利里很快露出了难受的神色。那是个暖夜，汗珠开始从他额头上冒出来。就他而论，这场捉弄已经大功告成，可萨姆显然还欠点儿火候。

===Original===
“Here, give me the tabasco bottle,” whispered Clark to Smith.

As the latter brought the article from one of the baskets, Sam said to him in a low voice,

“Did General Gramp take it out of that same bottle?”

“Yes,” said Smith; “strange to say, it’s the very same one, and all through his life afterward he took tabasco three times a day.”

Sam rolled his eyes painfully to catch a glimpse of the historic bottle. Clark took it and applied it to Sam’s lips. It was red-hot stuff, and the whole audience rose to watch its effect upon the victim at the stake. Sam swallowed it as if it had been lemonade. In fact, he was only aware of the honor that he was receiving. He had only enough earthly consciousness left to notice that one of the cadets in the crowd was photographing him with a kodak, and accordingly he did not even wink.

===Chinese===
“喂，把塔巴斯科瓶子递给我。”克拉克低声对史密斯说。

史密斯从一只筐里把那件东西取来时，萨姆低声对他说：

“格兰普将军当年用的是不是这同一只瓶子？”

“是，”史密斯说，“说来也怪，正是这同一只；他后半辈子一天三顿都拿塔巴斯科辣酱佐餐。”

萨姆痛苦地转着眼珠，想瞅一眼那只具有历史意义的瓶子。克拉克接过去，往萨姆嘴唇上便招呼。那是火辣辣的东西，全体观众都站起来看它对桩上这位受刑者的效力。萨姆把它咽了下去，仿佛喝的是柠檬水。说实在的，他只意识到自己正在领受的荣誉。他身上剩下的那一点凡间知觉，只够注意到人群里有个学员正用柯达相机（kodak）给他照相，于是他连眼皮也不曾眨一下。

===Original===
“By Jove, he’s lined with tin,” ejaculated Saunders, whose deflected nose gave him a sinister expression. “You ought to have had his plumbing, Clark.”

“Shut up and mind your own business,” said Clark. “Come, let’s give him the tub. This won’t do. That other chap’s happy enough where he is.”

Sam was untied again and led forward to the middle of the ring, the faithful Smith still keeping close to him.

“Is that an old tub?” whispered Sam, still standing stiffly as if his body had permanently taken the “braced” shape.

“I should say so. All the generals were ducked in it. Kneel down there and look in. Do you see that round dent in the middle? That’s where General Meriden bumped his head in it. He never did things by halves.”

===Chinese===
“天哪，他肚子里衬了铁皮。”桑德斯失声道，他那偏塌的鼻子给他添了一副凶相。“克拉克，你真该查一查他的五脏管道。”

“闭嘴，少管闲事。”克拉克说，“来，让他上水桶。这一味不顶事了。另外那家伙待在原地已经够快活了。”

萨姆又被解开，押到圈子当中，忠心耿耿的史密斯依旧紧随着他。

“那是一只旧桶吗？”萨姆低声问，身子仍旧直挺挺地站着，仿佛他的身躯已经永久定了“绷姿”的形。

“可不是。将军们全都在这只桶里浸过。跪下去往里瞧。看见当中那个圆坑了吗？那是梅里登将军在里头磕出来的。他做事从不半途而废。”

===Original===
Sam did as he was told, and he felt that he was in a proper attitude upon his knees at such a shrine. To him it was holy water.

“Now, Jinks,” squeaked Clark.

“Yes, sir,” answered Sam.

“Stand on your head now in that tub, and be quick about it.”

Sam fixed his mind upon General Meriden in the same circumstances, drew in his breath, and endeavored to stand on his head in a foot of water, holding on to the rim of the tub with his hands. His legs waved irresolutely in the air with no apparent unity of motive, and bubbles gurgled about his neck and shoulders.

===Chinese===
萨姆依言而行，觉得跪在这样一座圣祠跟前，正是自己应有的姿态。在他，那便是圣水。

“听着，金克斯。”克拉克吱声道。

“是，长官。”萨姆应道。

“现在头朝下，倒立到那只桶里去，动作快些。”

萨姆把心思集中在身处同一境地的梅里登将军身上，吸足一口气，便往那一尺深的水里倒栽下去，双手扣住桶沿。他的双腿在空中迟疑不决地乱摆，全无统一的意向，气泡在他脖颈和肩头汩汩地翻涌。

===Original===
“Grab his legs!” shouted Clark.

Two cadets obeyed the order, and Clark took out his watch to time the ordeal. The instants that passed seemed like an age.

“Isn’t time up?” whispered Saunders.

“Shut up, you fool, haven’t I got my watch open?” replied Clark. “But, good heavens!” he added, “take him out—I believe my watch has stopped.” And he shook it and put it to his ear.

Sam was hauled out and laid on the grass, but he was entirely unconscious. His tormentors were thoroughly scared. Fortunately they had all gone through a course of “first aid to the injured,” and they immediately took the proper precautions, holding him up by the feet until the water ran out of his mouth and nose, and then rolling him on the tub and manipulating his arms. At last some faint indications of breathing set in, and they concluded to carry him down to his tent. Using two boards as a stretcher, six of them acted as bearers, and the procession moved toward the camp. Cleary would have been forgotten, had he not asked them to untie him, which they did, and he followed behind, walking most stiffly. As they neared the camp the party separated. Two of the strongest took Sam, whose mind was wandering, to his tent, and Clark made Cleary come and spend the night with him, lest anxiety at Sam’s condition might impel him to report the matter to the authorities. How they all got to their tents in safety, and how the password happened to be known to all of them, we must leave it to the officers in command at East Point to explain. Sam was dropped upon his bunk without much consideration. The two cadets waited long enough to make sure that he was breathing, and then they decamped.

===Chinese===
“抓住他的腿！”克拉克喊道。

两个学员奉命抓住，克拉克掏出怀表，给这场刑罚计时。过去的一瞬一瞬，漫长得像一个世纪。

“时间还没到吗？”桑德斯低声问。

“闭嘴，你这傻瓜，我不是开着怀表吗？”克拉克答道。“可是，天哪！”他又说，“把他拉出来——我的表怕是停了。”说着他摇了摇表，把它贴在耳朵上。

萨姆被拖出水桶，放在草地上，人已经完全失去了知觉。这班折磨他的人着实吓坏了。所幸他们都受过“伤员急救”课程的训练，立即采取了恰当的措施：把他双脚倒提，直到水从口鼻里流尽，然后把他伏在桶上滚动，又揉动他的双臂。终于有了几分微弱的呼吸迹象，他们便决定把他抬下山送回帐篷。拿两块木板权当担架，六个人充当杠夫，这支队伍便朝营地进发。克利里本来险些被忘在脑后，亏他开口求他们松绑——他们解了——他才跟在后面，走得僵直无比。将近营地时，一行人分作几路。两个最壮实的架着神志恍惚的萨姆回他的帐篷，克拉克则拉克利里去自己帐里过夜，生怕他为萨姆的伤势一着急，把这件事上报官长。他们这伙人怎么全都安然回到各自帐篷，那口令又怎么人人心知肚明，我们只好留给东点的长官们去解释了。萨姆被不大经心地撂在铺位上。那两个学员守到确知他有了呼吸，便溜之大吉。

===Original===
“It’s really a shame,” said Smith to Saunders, who tented with him, before he turned over to sleep; “it’s really a shame to leave that fellow there without a doctor, but we’d all get bounced if it got out.”

===Chinese===
“真是作孽，”史密斯睡前对同他合帐的桑德斯说，“把那家伙撂在那儿，连个医生也不给请，真是作孽。可这事一旦张扬出去，咱们个个都得卷铺盖。”说罢他才翻身睡去。

## XIV Home Again / 十四 重返家园

===Original===
When Sam got up and began to undress to take his bath, his head swam so that he was obliged to lie down again. He tried again two or three times, but always with the same result, and finally he rang for a servant and sent for an army surgeon. The doctor came at once, took his temperature with a thermometer, and, after examining him, pronounced that he had a bad attack of fever, probably typhoid. He advised him to go to the hospital, and before noon Sam found himself comfortably installed in a hospital bed, screened off by a movable partition from a ward of fever patients. The doctor’s surmise proved to be correct, and for weeks he was dangerously ill, much of the time being delirious. He suffered once or twice also from relapses, and showed very little recuperative force when the fever finally left him. Meanwhile he was very low-spirited. The idea preyed upon his mind that he was no soldier and could never be one, and he felt that the resulting depression had a great deal to do with his protracted illness. Cleary was assiduous in his attentions, but, intimate as they were, Sam could never bring himself to confess his culpable weakness to him. As he became convalescent he had other visitors, and among them Mr. Cope, the inventor of explosives and artillery.

===Chinese===
萨姆起身脱衣，准备洗澡，只觉天旋地转，只好重新躺下。他又试了两三回，回回如此，末了只得摇铃唤来仆人，派人去请一位军医。医生立刻赶到，用体温计给他量了体温，检查一番之后，断定他染上了凶险的热病，多半是伤寒。医生劝他进医院；不到中午，萨姆便已舒舒服服地安顿在一张病床上，由一道活动隔屏同一间住满热病病人的病房隔开。医生的推测证实无误，此后一连数周他病势凶险，大半时间都在高烧说着胡话。其间病情还反复过一两回；热病终于退去时，他也几乎显不出多少复元的气力。这段日子里，他情绪极其低落。有一个念头苦苦咬噬着他的心：他已经不配做军人，而且永远做不成了；他觉得，由此而生的心灰意懒，同他这场迁延不愈的病大有干系。克利里（Cleary）探望照料，殷勤备至；然而两人虽说亲密无间，萨姆却始终没法向他吐露自己那份难辞其咎的怯懦。待到渐渐康复，探望他的人多了起来，其中便有那位发明炸药与火炮的科普先生（Mr. Cope）。

===Original===
“I am at work at a great invention which I shall owe partly to you and partly to the Emperor,” said he on one occasion. “Do you remember that at that execution the Emperor said that the perfect soldier has no conscience or reason?” Sam winced. “And then you called my attention to the fact that the men performed their part like machines. That set me thinking. I am always on the lookout for suggestions, and there was one ready-made. Do you see? Why shouldn’t a machine be made to take the place of a soldier? A great idea, isn’t it? Now you see we’ve already done something in that line. A torpedo is simply an iron soldier that swims under water and needs no breath, and does as he is told. Think how absurd it is in battle to have a field-battery come up under fire at a gallop! They swing round, unlimber, load, and fire, then harness again, swing round again, and off they are. Meanwhile perhaps half the men and horses have been killed. Wouldn’t it be better to have the whole battery a machine, instead of only the guns? The general could stay behind out of range, as he does today, and direct the whole thing with an electric battery and a telescope. It is not a difficult matter when you once accept the principle, and the principle can be extended to cavalry and infantry just as well. It will be a great thing for the nations that are best at mechanics, and that means you and us.”

===Chinese===
“我正在钻研一项伟大的发明，这桩发明，一半得归功于您，一半得归功于皇帝。”有一天他说道，“您可记得，在那回行刑的时候，皇帝说过，完美的士兵既没有良心，也没有理智？”萨姆不由得瑟缩了一下。“后来又是您提醒我，说那些士兵执行各自的差事时活像机器。这一下可让我开了窍。我一向随处留心寻找启发，而这里就有一个现成的。您明白吗？为什么不可以造出一部机器来顶替士兵呢？伟大的想法，不是吗？您瞧，在这条路上我们已经有了一点作为。鱼雷便是一个铁打的士兵，会在水下泅水，用不着呼吸，叫他干什么他便干什么。想想看，打仗的时候，一队野炮冒着炮火疾驰而上，这是何等的荒唐！他们转过炮位，卸下炮架，装填，开火，然后重新套马，再转个向，扬长而去。这时候，人员和马匹恐怕已经折损了一半。倘若把整支炮队都变成一部机器，而不只是那几门炮，岂不更好？将军可以像如今这样远远待在射程之外，用一组电池和一具望远镜指挥全局。一旦接受了这个原则，其余并不难办，而且这原则同样可以推广到骑兵和步兵。这对那些最精于机械的国家将是一件天大的好事——那就是说，贵国和我们。”

===Original===
“I don’t see,” said Sam, “how you can get on without the courage of brave men.”

“Courage! Why, what is more courageous than a piece of steel? It wouldn’t be easy to frighten it. And it is just so with all soldierly qualities. Do you want obedience? What is more obedient than a machine? I suppose you admit that a human soldier may disobey orders sometimes.”

“Perhaps,” said Sam, blushing uneasily.

===Chinese===
“我不明白，”萨姆说，“没有勇士的胆气，你们这套路怎么行得通。”

“胆气！嗐，还有什么比一块钢铁更有勇气的？想吓倒它可不容易。军人的一切美德莫不如此。您要服从吗？还有什么比机器更服从的？我想您总得承认，血肉的士兵偶尔也会违抗军令的吧。”

“也许吧。”萨姆说，不安地红了脸。

===Original===
“You may be sure that a steel soldier won’t unless he is disabled, and a human soldier may be disabled too. Then the Emperor said a soldier should not reason. There’s no danger of a steel soldier trying that.

> “ ‘Theirs not to reason why.
 

 Theirs but to do and die.’

“Why, the Light Brigade at Balaklava won’t be in it with them. And it’s just the same with regard to conscience. A piece of steel has no conscience. What we want is a machine soldier. A soldier must be obedient, and he must be without fear, conscience, or a mind of his own. In all these respects a machine can surpass a man. Why, you yourself, in praising those Tutonian soldiers, said that they went like clockwork. That’s the highest military praise possible.”

===Chinese===
“您尽管放心，钢制的士兵绝不违令，除非他被打坏了——血肉的士兵也一样会被打坏嘛。皇帝还说过，士兵不该动脑子。钢制士兵要想动那个念头，是毫无危险的。

> “ ‘他们不问为什么，
 

 唯有听命赴死。’

“嗐，巴拉克拉瓦（Balaklava）的轻骑兵旅同他们一比也要黯然失色。至于良心，道理也一模一样。一块钢铁是没有良心的。我们要的正是机器士兵。士兵必须服从，还必须无所畏惧、没有良心、没有自己的主见。在所有这些方面，机器都能胜过人。就说您自己吧，您称赞那些图托尼亚（Tutonian）士兵时，不也说他们行动起来像钟表一样严丝合缝么？那已是军人所能得到的至高赞语了。”

===Original===
Sam was much disturbed by this conversation. Mr. Cope went on to tell how his Government had spent £23,000 to fire a single shot and test one of his new projectiles, but Sam was not interested. Then the inventor began to rally him about the lack of interest of soldiers in the inventions which they used.

“If you had had to depend on yourselves for inventions,” he said, “you would still be fighting with crossbows, or perhaps more likely with your teeth and fingernails. No soldier ever invented anything. We inventors are the real military men.”

===Chinese===
这番谈话让萨姆心绪难宁。科普先生接着又讲起，他的政府如何花了两万三千英镑，只为发射一发炮弹、试验他的一种新式炮弹，可萨姆兴味索然。随后这位发明家开始打趣他，笑士兵们对自己平日使唤着的那些发明竟这般缺乏兴趣。

“倘若发明的事都得靠你们自己，”他说，“你们如今还在用弩弓打仗呢，或者更可能，还在用牙齿和指甲厮打。士兵从来没有发明过任何东西。我们这些发明家，才是真正的军人。”

===Original===
At last Sam’s unconscious tormentor took his departure, and the invalid rang for the hospital orderly so that he might tell him not to let him in again. To his surprise a new orderly appeared, a negro whose face was strangely familiar.

“What is it, sah?” he said.

“Is that you, Mose?” cried Sam. “Why, it’s almost as good as being at home again.”

===Chinese===
这位浑然不觉地折磨着他的人总算告辞去了，病人便摇铃唤医院勤务员，想吩咐他往后别再放此人进来。不料应铃而来的是个新勤务员——一个黑人，那张脸却说不出的眼熟。

“啥事呀，长官？”他说。

“是你吗，莫斯（Mose）？”萨姆叫了起来。“嗬，这简直跟重返家园差不多啦。”

===Original===
“Bress my soul, Massa Jinks—I mean General, have you been a-hurtin’ yourself again?” and the man chuckled to himself till his whole body shook. Under Mose’s care Sam made more rapid progress and soon was able to go out in a sedan-chair, borne by three men, like a mandarin. The winter passed away and spring was about to set in. There was no prospect of active service in Porsslania, the Powers being unable to agree upon any policy. The Emperor had already gone home, and the various armies were much reduced in strength. Cleary had been ordered to return by his newspaper, and had taken passage in a passenger steamer for the first of May.

“Why can’t you come with me?” he said to Sam. “You’re entitled to a leave of absence, and when you get to Whoppington you can apply for some other berth.”

Sam followed this wise advice and obtained a furlough of three months, and on the day fixed for sailing they embarked for home.

===Chinese===
“老天爷保佑，金克斯老爷——俺是说将军，您又把自个儿弄伤啦？”那人自个儿嘿嘿直乐，笑得浑身乱颤。在莫斯的照管下，萨姆康复得快多了，不久便能坐着三个人抬的轿子出门，像个官老爷似的。冬天过完，春天将临。波斯兰尼亚（Porsslania）这边看不出有什么仗可打——列强们在任何一项政策上都谈不拢。皇帝已经启程回国，各国驻军也都大幅裁减。克利里接到报社的调令，命他回去，已在五月一日的班轮上订了舱位。

“你怎么不跟我一道走？”他对萨姆说。“你有资格休假，到了沃平顿（Whoppington），再谋个别的差事也不迟。”

萨姆依了这条高明的主意，获准三个月休假，到了定好的启航之日，两人便登船踏上归途。

===Original===
Sam was still an invalid, but the voyage did him a great deal of good, and before they had been a week at sea he began to look quite like his old self. There were few passengers who interested him, but he became acquainted with one man of note, a Porsslanese literatus, who was attached to the legation at Whoppington, and sat on the other side of the captain of the steamer at meals. This gentleman, who bore the name of Chung Tu, was greatly interested in military matters and listened to Sam’s accounts by the hour. The night before their arrival at St. Kisco, the regular dinner was, as usual, converted into a banquet, and a band was improvised for the occasion. At the close of dinner the martial hymns of all nations were played, ending with “Yankee Doodle.” It was impossible to resist the impulse to laugh as this national jig brought up the rear, and Sam was much displeased that the foreigners on board, and there were many, should have laughed at his country. When he went up on deck he found Cleary conversing with Chung Tu, and he placed his steamer-chair beside theirs and joined the conversation.

===Chinese===
萨姆仍是个病号，可这趟航行对他大有好处，出海不满一个星期，他看上去已颇有几分旧日的模样。乘客中引得起他兴味的寥寥无几，不过他结识了一位知名人士——一位波斯兰文人，在沃平顿的公使馆里任职，用饭时坐在轮船船长的另一侧。这位先生名叫仲图（Chung Tu），对军事极感兴趣，萨姆讲起的种种见闻，他一听便是几个钟头。抵达圣基斯科（St. Kisco）的前夜，平日的晚餐又照例升格成了宴会，还临时凑起了一支乐队。宴席将罢，奏起了各国的军歌，最后以《扬基·杜德尔》（Yankee Doodle）收尾。这支国民快步舞曲一压轴，谁都忍不住要发笑；船上的外国人又多，他们竟拿萨姆的祖国取笑，萨姆为此很是不痛快。他走上甲板，见克利里正同仲图交谈，便把自己的甲板躺椅挪到他们近旁，加入了谈话。

===Original===
“It’s a great pity,” said he, “that we have such a national air as ‘Yankee Doodle.’ It holds us up to ridicule.”

“Do you think so?” answered Chung Tu, who spoke English perfectly. “That depends upon the point of view. You see you take the military point of view. We Porsslanese are not a military nation. We do not think much of armies. We do not try to spread our territory by force, and we never encroach on our neighbors’ land, although we are really overcrowded. Perhaps that is the reason people dislike us. We are not much of an empire either. We have very little central authority, and only a handful of officials. We have free speech, and even the Emperor can be freely criticized without fear. We have no conscription, and no one need carry a passport, as they have to in some countries. We are almost a democracy. We have no exclusive hereditary rank. Anyone may become a mandarin if he learns enough to deserve it. We only wanted to be left alone without armies, and we did not want to buy guns and ships. That is all. We are almost a democracy, and that is the reason that I have always studied your history with care. I have studied your state papers and your hymns. I have made a special study of them, and I have come to the opposite conclusion from you as to ‘Yankee Doodle.’ It seems to me to be the work of a great poet and prophet.”

===Chinese===
“真太遗憾了，”他说，“我们有《扬基·杜德尔》这样一支国曲。它让我们成了笑柄。”

“您当真这么想？”仲图答道，他的英语讲得无可挑剔。“这就要看从什么角度看了。您瞧，您用的是军人的眼光。我们波斯兰人不是尚武的民族。我们不把军队当回事。我们不用武力拓展疆土，也从不侵占邻邦的土地，尽管我们实在拥挤得很。也许人们不喜欢我们，正是为了这个。我们也算不得什么帝国。中央的权柄微乎其微，官吏也寥寥可数。我们有言论自由，连皇帝也尽可以放心大胆地批评。我们没有征兵制，谁也不必随身携带护照，不像有些国家的人那样。我们几乎就是一个民主之邦。我们没有排他的世袭等级。谁只要学有所成、当之无愧，谁就可以做官。我们只求人家不来打搅，安安稳稳过日子，不要军队，也不想买枪购船。如此而已。我们几乎就是一个民主之邦，正因如此，我一向仔细研读贵国的历史。贵国的公文和圣歌，我都研读过，还下过专门的功夫，而对于《扬基·杜德尔》，我得出的结论恰与您相反。在我看来，它出自一位伟大的诗人和先知之手。”

===Original===
“What do you mean?” asked Sam.

“Let us consider it seriously,” said Chung Tu. “Have you a copy of it?”

“No,” said Sam, laughing.

“Then please repeat it for us, and I will write it down.”

===Chinese===
“此话怎讲？”萨姆问。

“咱们正正经经地来推敲推敲。”仲图说。“您手头有它的抄本吗？”

“没有。”萨姆笑着说。

“那就劳驾您给我们背一遍，我把它记下来。”

===Original===
Sam began to recite, but he found it difficult to keep his face straight:

> “ ‘Yankee Doodle went to town,
 

 Riding on a pony.


 He stuck a feather in his crown
 

 And called him macaroni.’ ”

“That is not like my version,” said the attaché, pulling a piece of paper from the pocket of his silk jacket. “Here is mine,” and he read it solemnly and with emphasis:

> “ ‘Yankee Doodle came to town,
 

 A-riding on a pony.


 He stuck a feather in his cap
 

 And called it macaroni.’

===Chinese===
萨姆开始背诵，可他发觉要绷住脸实在不容易：

> “ ‘扬基·杜德尔进了城，
 

 骑着一匹小马。


 他往王冠上插了根羽毛，
 

 还管他叫通心粉。’ ”

“这跟我记的版本不一样。”这位随员说着，从绸衣口袋里掏出一张纸来。“我的是这样的。”说罢，他郑重其事、抑扬顿挫地念道：

> “ ‘扬基·杜德尔来到城里，
 

 骑着一匹小马。


 他往帽子上插了根羽毛，
 

 管它叫通心粉。’

===Original===
“Which reading is correct?” he asked of Cleary.

“I’m sure I don’t know,” said Cleary, laughing.

“How careless you are of your country’s literature! In Porsslania we would carefully guard the sayings of our ancestors and preserve them from alteration. You have what you call the ‘higher criticism.’ You should direct it to the correction of this most important poem. I have studied the matter as carefully and accurately as a foreigner can, and I am satisfied that my version is the most authentic. Come now, let us study it. Take the first two lines:

> “ ‘Yankee Doodle came to town
 

 A-riding on a pony.’

“There is nothing difficult in that. You may say that the name is a strange one, and I admit that ‘Doodle’ is a curious surname, but ‘Yang Kee’ is a perfectly reasonable one from a Porsslanese point of view, and leads me to suppose that the wisdom contained in this poem came originally from our wise men. Perhaps the name is put there as an indication of the fact. However, let us accept the name. The hero came to town riding on a pony. That was a very sensible thing to do. Remember that those lines were written long before the discovery of railways or tramcars or bicycles or automobiles. You may say that he might have taken a carriage or one of your buggies, but you forget that the roads were exceedingly bad in those days, as bad as our roads near the Imperial City, and it would have been dangerous perhaps to attempt the journey in a vehicle of any kind. In riding to town on a pony, then, he was acting like a rational man. But let us read the rest of the verse:

===Chinese===
“哪一种读法是对的呢？”他问克利里。

“我实在说不上来。”克利里笑着说。

“贵国的文学，你们也太不上心了！在波斯兰尼亚，我们先贤的言语是要精心守护、不容窜改的。你们有你们所谓的‘高等考据’。真该把这门学问用来校订这首至关重要的诗篇。我尽一个外国人所能，把这件事研究得又仔细又精当，我深信我记的版本最为可信。来吧，咱们这就研读研读。先看头两行：

> “ ‘扬基·杜德尔来到城里
 

 骑着一匹小马。’

“这没有什么难解的地方。诸位也许要说，这名字有点古怪；我承认‘杜德尔’这个姓确实稀奇，可‘杨基’（Yang Kee）在波斯兰人看来完全是个合情合理之名，这倒叫我想，这首诗里蕴藏的智慧，原本出自我们的先哲。说不定这名字正是为了点明这一层才安在那里的。不过，咱们就认下这个名字吧。主人公骑着一匹小马进城。这是极明智的举动。要知道，这几行诗写成的时候，铁路、电车、自行车、汽车都还远未发明。诸位也许要说，他本可以坐马车，或是坐你们那种轻便马车；可你们忘了，那时候的路糟透了，同我们皇城近郊的路一样糟，乘任何一种车辆赶这趟路，恐怕都是险事。这么看来，骑一匹小马进城，他做的正是一个理性之人该做的事。可咱们再读读这一节诗的下半段：

===Original===
> “ ‘He stuck a feather in his cap
 

 And called it macaroni.’

“For some reason or other which is not revealed, he puts a feather in his cap, and immediately he begins to act irrationally and to use language so absurd that the reading itself has become doubtful. What is the meaning of this? A man whose conduct has always been reasonable and unexceptionable, suddenly adopts the language of a lunatic. What does it mean? You have sung this verse for a century and more, and you have never taken the trouble to seek for the meaning.”

===Chinese===
> “ ‘他往帽子上插了根羽毛，
 

 管它叫通心粉。’

“不知出于什么缘故——诗里没有交代——他往帽子上插了一根羽毛，紧接着，他的行事立刻变得毫无理性，说起话来荒唐透顶，以致这几行诗本身的读法都成了疑问。这是什么意思？一个行事向来通情达理、无可指摘的人，忽然说起疯子的话来。这是什么意思？这一节诗你们唱了一个多世纪，却从没费心去寻思过其中的含义。”

===Original===
Sam and Cleary did not attempt to defend their neglect.

“It is clear to me,” proceeded the philosopher, “it is very clear to me that it is an allegory. What is the feather which he puts in his cap? It is the most conspicuous feature of the military uniform, the plume, the pompon, which marks all kinds of military dress-hats. When he speaks of his hero as having assumed the feather, he means that he has donned the uniform of a soldier. He has come to town, in other words, to enlist. Then behold the transformation! He begins at once to act irrationally. The whole epic paints in never-fading colors the disastrous effect upon the intellect of putting on soldier-clothes. You will pardon me, my friends, if I speak thus plainly, but I must open to you the hidden wisdom of your own country.”

===Chinese===
萨姆和克利里并不打算为自己的怠慢辩白。

“依我看，”这位哲人接着说道，“依我看，再清楚不过，这是一篇寓言。他插在帽子上的那根羽毛是什么？正是军装上最显眼的东西——翎毛、绒球，各色军帽上都有那样的点缀。作者说他的主人公插上了羽毛，意思便是他披上了士兵的军装。换句话说，他进城是来投军的。接下来瞧这变化！他立刻行事就不合理性了。整篇史诗以永不褪色的色彩，描绘出穿上军装对人的智力造成的灾难性后果。朋友们，恕我口直，可我必须向诸位揭开贵国自身深藏的智慧。”

===Original===
Sam smiled. The idea of taking offense at any nonsense which an ignorant pagan should say was quite beneath him.

“But that is not all. The style of the language and of the music is most noteworthy. It is highly comical, and its object evidently is to provoke a laugh, and at dinner this evening we saw that its object was attained. All the other martial hymns to which we listened were grave, ponderous compositions from which the element of humor was rigidly excluded. It was left for the author of ‘Yang Kee’ to uncover the ludicrous character of militarism—he has virtually committed your nation to it. He was a genius of marvelous insight. He saw clearly then what but few of your fellow citizens are even now aware of, that there is nothing more comical than a soldier. I am convinced that he was a Porsslanese who had the good fortune to sow in your literature the seed of truth. You think that as a nation you have a sense of humor. I have studied your humorous literature. You laugh at mothers-in-law and messenger-boys and domestic servants, and many other objects which are altogether serious and have no element of humor in them, and at the same time you are blind to the most absurd of spectacles, the man who dresses up in feathers and gold lace and thinks it honorable to do nothing for years but wait for a pretext to kill somebody,” and Chung Tu leaned back in his chair and smiled.

===Chinese===
萨姆微微一笑。要为一个无知的异教徒信口胡诌的什么话动气，这在他可有不屑。

“这还不算完。那词句同乐曲的风格尤其值得注意。它滑稽至极，用意显然就是逗人发笑，今晚席上我们已经看到，这用意是达到了。我们方才听到的其余那些军歌，都是一本正经、沉甸甸的作品，幽默的成分被剔除得干干净净。唯独《杨基》的作者揭出了军国主义滑稽可笑的本相——可以说，他等于让贵国在这上头画了押。他是位见识惊人的天才。他当年就看得分明的事，直到如今，贵国的同胞也少有人明白：世上再没有比士兵更可笑的了。我深信他是位波斯兰人，有幸在贵国的文学里播下了真理的种子。你们以为，贵国作为一个民族是有幽默感的。我研究过贵国的幽默文学。你们笑话岳母，笑话送信的男孩，笑话家仆，还笑话许许多多全然正经、毫无幽默可言的东西；与此同时，对天下最荒唐的奇观——那个浑身插着翎毛、缀着金绦，认为一连几年无所事事、专候一个杀人的口实还算得上体面的人——你们却视而不见。”仲图往椅背上一靠，微微笑了。

===Original===
“It is we who have the sense of humor,” he added. “When our common people laughed at the Emperor in his uniforms, they showed the same sound sense that appears in ‘Yang Kee.’ I thank you, my dear friends, for listening to me so kindly and without anger, but I hope to preach these ideas to your people, and as I take my text from your national hymn, they must listen to me. Then there is another common expression among you which shows, as so many proverbs do, the fundamental truth. When a story is incredible you say ‘Tell that to the marines,’ signifying that only a marine would be stupid enough to believe it. Now what is a marine? As the Anglian poet says, he is ‘soldier and sailor too,’ in other words, he epitomizes the army and navy. It is the military man who is foolish enough to believe anything and who keeps alive the most absurd superstitions and customs. The ancient Greeks cast a sidelight on this truth, for their word for private soldier was ‘idiot.’ And on account of this strange stupidity of soldiers, things that would be disgraceful in private life become glorious in war. Their one virtue is obedience, unqualified by any of the balancing virtues, and they wear liveries to show that they are servile. And then the foolish things they try to do! You are familiar with the Peace Conference—generals and admirals spending weeks in uniform with swords at their sides to determine how to stop fighting, as if there were anything to do but to stop! I believe they had the grace to turn the war pictures in the conference room to the wall. But fancy sending butchers to a conference in the interests of vegetarianism! Of course nothing was done or could be done there. And the Emperor in his uniform, drunk with militarism, wanted us—all our nation—wanted *me*—to kowtow before him as if he were a god! But he did not get what he wanted from us. His own people may grovel before him, but we will not. Oh, these soldiers, these soldiers! You look down on your hangmen and butchers. We look down on our men-butchers, the soldiers, in the same way. We have soldiers just as you have police, but it is a low calling with us, and most people would be ashamed to have a soldier in the family. Pardon me, my dear sirs. Perhaps I have spoken too plainly. I mean nothing personal, but when I think of these wars, I can not control my tongue. Good night.”

===Chinese===
“有幽默感的，正是我们这些人。”他又说道。“我们的平民百姓见了穿军装的皇帝发笑时，他们表现出的，正是《杨基》里那种健全的见识。亲爱的朋友们，多谢你们如此和气、不动气地听我说完。可我还盼着把这些道理讲给贵国的百姓听呢；我既从贵国的国歌里取了题目，他们就不能不听了。你们还有一句口头语，同无数谚语一样，道出了这条根本的真理。听到难以置信的故事，你们便说‘讲给海军陆战队去听’（Tell that to the marines），意思是说，只有海军陆战队员才会蠢得信它。可海军陆战队员是什么呢？正像安格利亚（Anglia）诗人所说，他是‘亦兵亦水手’；换句话说，陆海两军的精义，他一身兼备了。正是军人，才蠢得什么都信，才把最荒唐的迷信和习俗供养下来。古希腊人给这条真理添过一个旁注：他们管列兵叫的那个词，正是‘白痴’（idiot）。也正是由于士兵这等古怪的愚蠢，私生活里丢脸的事，一到战争里便成了光荣。他们唯一的德行是服从，不受任何相济相衡之德的调和；而且他们身穿号衣，以表明自己是供人役使之流。再说他们干的那些蠢事！和平会议诸位是领教过的——一帮将军和海军上将，身着军装、胁佩军刀，花上几个星期去研究怎么制止打仗，仿佛除了住手之外还有什么可研究似的！我记得他们总算还知趣，把会议室里的战争画都掉转过去面对墙壁。可是请想想，派一帮屠夫去出席一个为素食主义张目的大会，成何体统！那会议当然一无所成，也不可能有所成。还有那位身穿军装、醉醺醺沉浸于军国主义的皇帝，他要我们——要我们举国上下——要*我*——向他磕头，仿佛他是天神一般！可他没能从我们这儿得到他想要的。他自己的臣民尽管可以在他面前匍匐爬行，我们却断不肯依。唉，这些军人啊，这些军人！你们瞧不起你们那里的刽子手和屠夫；我们也一样，瞧不起我们那些屠人的家伙——士兵。我们也养着士兵，正如你们养着警察；可在我们那边，这是低贱的营生，谁家要是出了一个当兵的，多数人都要觉得脸上无光。恕我冒昧了，亲爱的先生们。也许我说得太直。我对谁都没有个人之意，只是一想到这些战争，我便管不住自己的舌头。晚安。”

===Original===
So saying, the attaché gathered up his robes and went below.

“Queer chap,” said Sam. “He must be crazy.”

“We’ve treated them rather badly, though,” said Cleary. “I’m glad Taffy hasn’t had any executions, but our minister and all the rest have been insisting on executions of their big people, and no one talks of executing any of ours, although they have suffered ten times as much as we have.”

===Chinese===
说罢，这位随员拢起长袍，到下面船舱里去了。

“古怪的家伙。”萨姆说。“准是疯了。”

“不过，咱们待他们确实够狠的。”克利里说。“幸好塔菲（Taffy）那边没闹出什么处决来。可我们的公使，还有其余各国，一个劲儿坚持要处决他们那边的大人物，却没有一个人提过要处决咱们的人，尽管他们吃的苦头十倍于我们。”

===Original===
“You forget how the affair began,” said Sam. “Suppose the Porsslanese had sent us missionaries to teach us their religion, and these missionaries had gradually got possession of land and also some local power of governing, and then we had ruthlessly murdered some of them and they had seized all our ports for the purpose of benefiting us, do you suppose that we would have risen like those miserable Fencers and massacred anybody? It is inconceivable. They have the strangest aversion to foreigners too.”

“Some of them haven’t,” said Cleary. “Chung Tu is a friendly old soul, if he is cracked. He says he believes the Powers have been turned loose on his country to punish them for having invented gunpowder. He laughs at Cope’s inventions. He says his people set the fashion, and then wisely stopped when they found that such inventions did more harm than good. I think they have a right to complain of us. Why, there’s one of our soldiers in the steerage with seventeen of their pigtails with the scalps still fastened to them as trophies! Old Chung says our ribbons and decorations are the equivalent of the scalps dangling at a savage’s belt. I didn’t tell him we had the genuine article. But, come, you had better turn in. You’ll have a hard day tomorrow. I’ve advertised your coming for all I was worth, and if they don’t give you a send-off at St. Kisco, it isn’t my fault. I’m glad you’re well enough to stand it.”

===Chinese===
“你忘了这桩事的起头了。”萨姆说。“假设波斯兰人派传教士来，教我们信他们的教；这些传教士渐渐攫到了土地，还攫到了一部分地方治理之权；随后我们狠心杀害了他们中的一些人，他们便为了我们的利益，把我们所有的口岸统统占了去——你以为我们会像那些可怜的剑民（Fencers）那样揭竿而起、见人就杀吗？不可想象。再说，他们对异邦人还有一种最古怪的憎恶。”

“他们里头也有不憎恶的。”克利里说。“仲图那老头子就挺和气，尽管脑子有点疯。他说他相信，列强之所以放开手脚糟践他的国家，是为了惩罚他们发明了火药。他笑话科普的种种发明。他说他们的人开的这个风气之先，后来发现这类发明害多于利，便明智地歇手不干了。我认为他们满有权利抱怨我们。嗐，统舱里就有一个咱们的兵，身上带着十七条他们的辫子当战利品，头皮还连在上头没揭下来呢！老仲图说，咱们的绶带和勋章，就相当于野人腰带上晃晃荡荡挂着的头皮。我可没告诉他，咱们手里就有原装真货。好了好了，你顶好去睡吧。明天有你受的。你的到来，我已经拼着老命四处张扬，他们要是不到圣基斯科给你来一场盛大的欢迎，那可怨不着我。真高兴你的身子撑得住。”

===Original===
“I’m not as well as I look,” said Sam. “I’ve lost all my nerve. I’m even worrying a little about all my loot in those cases in the hold. It sometimes seems that I oughtn’t to have taken it.”

“What!” cried Cleary. “Well, you are getting squeamish! After all the fellows you’ve killed or had killed, I shouldn’t mind an ornament or two.”

“Killing is a soldier’s main business,” said Sam. “Oh, well, I suppose looting is, too. I won’t think anything more about it. Good night.”

===Chinese===
“我不像看上去那么好。”萨姆说。“我的胆气全没了。我甚至有点为货舱里那几箱掳来的财物发愁。有时候我觉得，自己当初实在不该拿那些东西。”

“什么！”克利里叫了起来。“嗬，你倒讲究起良心来了！经你亲手杀掉、或是叫人杀掉的人不知有多少，依我看，多一两件摆设又何妨。”

“杀人原是军人的本行。”萨姆说。“嗬，得了，我想掳掠也是。我不再想它了。晚安。”

===Original===
While Sam and his friend were conversing on deck, another conversation which was to have a portentous effect upon the former’s destiny was taking place in the upper corridor of the Peckham Young Ladies’ Seminary at St. Kisco.

“He’s perfectly lovely,” said a young lady, standing barefoot before her door in her nightdress to a group of young ladies similarly attired. “I’ve got his photograph. And I’m not just going to stand still and see him pass. It’s all very well to have the school drawn up in line on the wharf—that’s better than nothing—but I want something more, and I’m going to have it.”

“What will you do, Sally?” they all cried.

===Chinese===
萨姆和他的朋友在甲板上交谈的时候，另一场对前者的命运将有重大影响的谈话，正在圣基斯科佩卡姆女子中学（Peckham Young Ladies’ Seminary）楼上的一条走廊里进行。

“他简直可爱透了。”一位年轻小姐说。她光着脚、穿着睡衣站在自己房门口，对面是一群装束相仿的年轻小姐。“我弄到了他的照片。我可不肯就这么干站着，眼睁睁看他打面前走过。全校列队在码头上迎候——那自然聊胜于无——可我还想要点别的，而且我志在必得。”

“你要干什么呀，萨莉（Sally）？”她们齐声嚷道。

===Original===
“I’m going to kiss him—there!” said she.

“Oh, Sally!”

“Yes, I will too.”

“I believe she will if she says so,” said one of the girls. “She won’t stop at anything. Well, Sally Watson, if you kiss him, I will too.”

“And I!” “And I!” exclaimed the others; but at that moment a step was heard on the stairs, and the Peckham young ladies sought their beds and pretended very hard to be asleep, although their hearts were thumping against their ribs at the mere thought of their daring resolution.

===Chinese===
“我要亲他——就这么办！”她说。

“哎呀，萨莉！”

“对，我就是要亲。”

“她说得出就做得到。”其中一个女孩子说。“她什么事都干得出来。好啊，萨莉·沃森（Sally Watson），你要是敢亲他，我也亲。”

“我也亲！”“我也亲！”其余的人叫道。可就在这时，楼梯上响起了脚步声，佩卡姆的年轻小姐们纷纷钻回各自床上，拼命装出熟睡的模样，虽然单是想到自己那个大胆的决定，她们的心口便扑通扑通直撞肋骨。

===Original===
It was at ten o’clock the next morning that the steamer came alongside the wharf. The city was in gala dress and flags waved everywhere. The day was observed almost as a holiday, and many schools permitted their pupils to take part in the procession which awaited the arrival of Captain Jinks, as Sam was now commonly known in his native land. A reception was arranged for him at the City Hall, and the Mayor came down to the steamer in a carriage with four horses to escort him thither. From the deck Sam could see a banner stretched across the street, on which was an inscription to the “Hero of San Diego, the Subduer of the Moritos, the Captor of Gomaldo, the Conqueror of the Great White Temple, and the Friend and Instructor of the Emperor.” A few months before, Sam would have enjoyed this display without alloy, but now his health was really shattered, and in the bottom of his heart he felt that he was unworthy of it all, for he was not the perfect soldier he had believed he was, and under his uniform beat the heart of a vulgar civilian. His military instincts had their limit; his obedience could only be relied upon under certain circumstances. He was a mere amateur, and had no claim to rank as a military hero at all.

===Chinese===
第二天上午十点，轮船靠上了码头。全城披上节日盛装，彩旗处处飘扬。这一天差不多被当成节日来过，许多学校准许学生加入等候的队伍，迎接金克斯上尉——萨姆如今在祖国通行的称呼——的到来。市政厅为他安排了一场招待会，市长乘着四驾马车亲临轮船，护送他前往。萨姆从甲板上望得见一幅横跨街面的横幅，上面题着献给“圣地亚哥（San Diego）的英雄、摩里托人（Moritos）的镇服者、戈马尔多（Gomaldo）的俘获者、大白庙的征服者、皇帝的朋友兼导师”的字样。若在几个月前，这番排场萨姆受用起来本会纯净无杂；可如今他的身子骨实在垮了，心底深处，他觉得自己当不起这一切，因为他并不是自己原先认定的那种完美士兵，军装底下跳动的，是一颗庸俗平民的心。他的军人本能自有止境；他的服从，只有在某种情势之下才靠得住。他不过是个业余角色，根本无权跻身军事英雄之列。

===Original===
A swarm of reporters settled down upon General Jinks as soon as they could get on board, insisting upon having his opinion as to the growth of the city since he had seen it, the superiority of its climate to that of any part of the world, and the beauty of its women. Sam answered all these questions satisfactorily, and surrendered himself to the committee of citizens who had come on deck to welcome him. His luggage was passed without delay by the Custom House officials, and he was conducted down the wharf toward the carriage which awaited him. With true chivalry young ladies’ schools had been given the best positions on the wharf, and Sam soon found himself passing through a double row of pretty girls. He could hear such remarks as this:

“Isn’t he good-looking!”

“What a lovely uniform!”

“Hasn’t he got a fascinating limp!”

“How pale he is!”

“He does look just like a hero.”

===Chinese===
记者们一上了船，便成群扑向金克斯将军（General Jinks），非要他发表高见不可：自他上次见识以来，这座城市有了多大的发展啦；这里的气候如何胜过世上任何一方水土啦；这里的女人如何美貌啦。这些问题萨姆都答得四平八稳、滴水不漏，随后便听凭登船欢迎他的市民委员会摆布。他的行李经海关官员之手一路放行，他也被引下码头，朝等候他的马车走去。出于地道的骑士风度，各家女子学校被安排在码头上的最佳位置，萨姆很快便发觉，自己正从两排漂亮姑娘中间穿过。他听得见这样的议论：

“他多俊啊！”

“这军装多漂亮！”

“他那一瘸一拐多迷人！”

“他脸色多白啊！”

“他看上去真像个英雄。”

===Original===
Sam flushed slightly at these comments, but suddenly, before he had time to collect his thoughts, a slight form sprang forward from the left and an inviting face presented itself to his, and with the words, “May I, please?” a hearty kiss was planted on his lips. Sam had no time to decline, if he had wished to. A murmur of surprise and delight arose from the crowd, and in another moment another damsel rushed upon him, and then another and another. Before long he was the center of a throng of elbowing young ladies of all kinds, fair, plain, and indifferent, all bent upon giving him a kiss. Sam had indeed lost his nerve; for the first time in his life he capitulated absolutely and let the attacking party work its sweet will. It was with great difficulty that he was rescued by the reception committee and finally seated next to the Mayor in the landau.

===Chinese===
听了这些议论，萨姆微微涨红了脸。可突然间，他还没来得及定神，左侧倏地窜出一条纤细的身影，一张逗人的脸迎面向他凑来，随着一声“可以吗，劳驾？”，一个热辣辣的吻便印上了他的嘴唇。萨姆要想拒绝也来不及了——就算他想拒绝的话。人群里响起一片又惊又喜的低呼，转眼间又一位姑娘朝他扑来，接着又一个，又一个。不多时，他便成了一群推推搡搡的年轻小姐的中心，漂亮的、平常的、不惹眼的，一概铁了心要给他一个吻。萨姆确实丢了胆气；平生头一回，他无条件缴械投降，任凭进攻的一方遂了她们甜蜜的心意。欢迎委员会费了好大的劲才把他抢救出来，末了他总算在四轮敞篷马车里挨着市长坐定了。

===Original===
“What a lot of cabdrivers you have there on the wharf!” said Sam to the Mayor, after their first greetings. “I never saw so many. Hear them crying out to the passengers coming ashore!”

“They’re not cabdrivers,” he answered. “They’re pension agents. They’re not crying ‘Want a cab?’ but ‘Want a pension?’”

“So they are,” said Sam. “What is that tune the young ladies are beginning to sing?”

“Don’t you know?” said the Mayor, laughing. “It’s ‘Captain Jinks.’ You’ll know it well enough before you are here long. Listen.”

===Chinese===
寒暄过后，萨姆对市长说：“你们码头上那许多马车夫倒是壮观！我从没见过这么多。听，他们正冲着下船的乘客叫喊呢！”

“他们不是马车夫。”他答道。“是领抚恤金的掮客。他们喊的不是‘要车吗？’，是‘要抚恤金吗？’”

“可不是嘛。”萨姆说。“小姐们开始唱的这是个什么调子？”

“您不知道？”市长笑着说。“是《金克斯上尉》。您在这儿待不了多久，就会听熟的。听吧。”

===Original===
Sam listened and heard sung for the first time lines that were to be imprinted upon his tympanum until they became a torture:

> “I’m Captain Jinks of the Cubapines,
 

 The pink of human war-machines,
 

 Who teaches emperors, kings, and queens
 

 The way to run an army.”

===Chinese===
萨姆听着，头一回听到了那几句歌词——日后它们将烙在他的耳膜上，直到成为一种苦刑：

> “我是古巴平（Cubapines）的金克斯上尉，
 

 人间战争机器之花，
 

 教皇帝、国王和王后们
 

 怎样统率一支大军。”

===Original===
The news of the kissing reached the City Hall before the procession, and when he alighted there Sam had to kiss an immense number of women who were determined not to be outdone by their sisters at the wharf, while the whole crowd sang “Captain Jinks” in a frenzy of enthusiasm. The reception accorded to Sam at St. Kisco was so elaborate, and the arrangements made to do him honor were so extended, that he was obliged to stay there for several days. Meanwhile the news of his arrival and of his gallantry in kissing his countrywomen, young and old, spread all over the land and took hold of the popular imagination. Invitations to visit various cities on his way across the Continent began to come in, and everywhere Sam was acclaimed as the hero and idol of the people.

===Chinese===
亲吻的消息赶在游行队伍之前传到了市政厅。萨姆在那里下车时，又不得不亲吻一大群妇女——她们打定了主意，决不能输给码头上的姐妹们——全场人群则狂热万分地唱着《金克斯上尉》。圣基斯科为萨姆举行的欢迎如此隆重，为他增光的各项安排又如此繁多，他不得不好在那里盘桓几天。其间，他归来的消息，连同他亲吻自己同胞妇女、不论老幼的骑士风度，传遍了全国，攫住了大众的想象。横穿大陆途中各城市发出的访问邀请接踵而来，所到之处，萨姆无不被誉为人民的英雄和偶像。

===Original===
“It’s great, it’s great, old man!” cried Cleary. “Why, that kissing business is worth a dozen victories! The people here say that no general or admiral has had such a send-off in St. Kisco.
