“I have heard of taming wild beasts that way,” said the general, “but I never quite believed it.”

===Chinese===
他们一觉醒来，听见村里人声嘈杂，便急忙赶了过去。酋长们已经到齐，正同卡洛斯（Carlos）和其他村民寒暄问好。妇女们在头天用饭的那块地上备好了早饭；八点整，远征队便出发了——队伍由三十来名武士组成，有几人身上驮着礼物，是些筐篮和土布。将近那支小小侵略军的司令部时，三个白人走到前头，告诉哨兵：跟在他们后面来的，是一支讲和的使团。

“咱们这桩壮举的经过，可得留给我来讲。”克利里（Cleary）早就打过招呼；两位朋友对他这张嘴的往绩向来佩服，便都点头依允了。

“将军，”他们走进村里他的棚屋时，克利里说道，“我们把莫里托人（Moritos）的酋长全都带来了。他们愿意放下武器，无论什么条件都肯接受。我们已同他们盟誓结好。”

“你们到底是怎么办成的？”将军问。

“这主要得归功于金克斯上尉（Captain Jinks）——哦，我该说金克斯少校了。他们眼看就要杀我们，全凭他那一瞪眼的威势和一张嘴的功力，硬是把他们镇住了，他们便归降于他。”

“这样驯猛兽的法子，我倒是听说过，”将军说，“只是从来不大肯信。”

===Original===
When the chiefs arrived they embraced every soldier they saw and showed every sign of joy. The general ordered a feast to be spread for them and addressed them in English. They did not understand a word of this harangue, but seemed much affected. When they heard that the great general of all was at San Diego, only a day’s march away, they insisted on going thither, and the next day the brigade marched back again, leaving a small garrison behind. The army at San Diego could hardly believe its eyes when at sundown the expedition returned, having fully accomplished its object without firing a shot and accompanied by a band of Moritos. When Cleary’s version of the exploit became known, Sam was openly acclaimed as a hero and the favorite of the army. General Laughter complimented him again, and again mentioned him in despatches. A week later his promotion to be major of volunteers, for meritorious conduct in the field of San Diego, was announced by cable, and again after a few days he was made a colonel. Sam’s cup was full.

===Chinese===
酋长们一到，见着哪个士兵都要拥抱一番，喜悦之情溢于言表。将军吩咐为他们大摆筵席，又用英语向他们发表了一通演说。这番宏论他们一个字也听不懂，却似乎大为动容。听说统帅全军的大将军就在圣迭戈（San Diego）、距此不过一日行程，他们执意要亲自前去；次日全旅便再度开拔回程，只留下一小支守备队。日落时分，远征队归来，一枪未发便圆满达成使命，身后还跟着一队莫里托人——圣迭戈的军队简直不敢相信自己的眼睛。克利里那番壮举的经过一经传开，萨姆便被公开推崇为英雄，成了全军的宠儿。劳夫特（Laughter）将军又一次嘉奖了他，又在战报里把他表彰了一番。一星期后，因圣迭戈战场功勋卓著，他晋升志愿部队少校的任命经由电报下达；没过几天，又升了上校。萨姆这只杯子，如今斟得满满的了。

===Original===
“Sam,” said Cleary one day, “I believe in your luck. You’ll be President some of these days. All the time we were up in the mountains I knew it would come out all right because we had you along.”

Meanwhile the chiefs had tendered their presents to General Laughter and had drunk plentiful libations of whisky and soda with him. They spent a week of festivity in the town and then returned, having agreed to all that was asked of them by their “brothers.”

The rainy season now set in, and operations in the field became difficult. Furthermore, the general had decided that the war was at an end, and officially it was so considered. Some troops were left at San Diego, but the headquarters were removed again to Havilla, and Sam went back with the staff. He found himself received as a great man. His two exploits had made him the most famous officer in the army, even more so than the general in command. Soon after his return to the city one of the civil commissioners, who had been sent out by the Administration, gave a large dinner in his honor at the palace. The chief officers and civil officials were among the guests, as well as two or three native merchants who had remained loyal to the invading army for financial and commercial reasons and had not joined the rebels, who composed nine-tenths of the population. These merchants were generally known in the army as the “patriots,” and were treated with much consideration by the civil commissioners.

===Chinese===
“萨姆，”一天克利里说，“我是信你的运气的。迟早有一天，你要当上总统。咱们在山里那阵子，我自始至终都笃定事情会顺顺当当，因为有你同行。”

这期间，酋长们向劳夫特将军献上了礼物，又同他一杯接一杯地痛饮威士忌兑苏打。他们在城里欢宴了一个星期，然后回山去了——“兄弟们”对他们提出的要求，他们一概应承了下来。

这时雨季来临，野战变得困难了。再说，将军已经认定战事告终，官方也就如此看待。一部分部队留驻圣迭戈，司令部则重新迁回哈维拉（Havilla），萨姆随参谋部回城。他发觉自己被当作一位大人物来接待。两桩功绩使他成了全军最有名的军官，名声比司令将军还要响。回城后不久，政府派来的一位文职专员在总督府设下盛大晚宴，为他接风。来宾中有高级军官和文职官员，还有两三位本地商人——他们出于金融和商业上的利害，始终效忠侵略军，没有附和叛军；而叛军占了人口的十分之九。这些商人在军中通称“爱国志士”，文职专员们对他们礼敬有加。

===Original===
After dinner the host proposed a toast to Sam and accompanied it with a patriotic speech which thrilled the hearts of his audience. He pointed to the national flag which was festooned upon the wall.

“Look at Old Gory!” he cried. “What does she stand for? For the rights of the oppressed all over the earth, for freedom and equal rights, for﻿—”

There was a sound of boisterous laughter in the next room. A young officer ran forward and whispered to the orator, “Be careful; some of those captured rebel officers are shut up in there, and perhaps they can overhear you. Be careful what you say. Some of them speak English.” The commissioner hemmed and hawed and tried to recover himself.

“What does the dear old flag stand for?” he repeated. “For liber﻿—No﻿—for-r-r﻿—Well, ’pon my word, what does she stand for?”

“For the army and navy,” whispered a neighbor.

“Yes,” he thundered. “Yes, the flag stands for the army and navy, for our officers and men, for our men-of-war and artillery, for our cavalry and infantry, that’s what she stands for!”

===Chinese===
饭后，主人提议为萨姆干杯，并伴之以一篇慷慨激昂的爱国演说，听得满座心潮澎湃。他指着墙上结彩一般悬挂的国旗。

“瞧瞧咱们的老光荣旗（Old Gory）！”他高声道，“她代表什么？代表普天之下受压迫者的权利，代表自由和平等的权利，代表——”

隔壁房间里忽然传出一阵哄堂大笑。一名年轻军官抢上前来，对演说者耳语道：“当心；那边关着几个被俘的叛军军官，说不定听得见您的话。说话留神，他们有几个会说英语。”专员嗯嗯啊啊，支吾了半天，竭力定住神。

“这面亲爱的老旗代表什么呢？”他又问了一遍，“代表解——不——代、代、代表——唉，我的天，她到底代表什么呢？”

“代表陆军和海军。”邻座的人悄声提示。

“对！”他雷鸣般地吼道，“对了，这面旗代表陆军和海军，代表我们的官兵，代表我们的军舰和大炮，代表我们的骑兵和步兵——她代表的就是这个！”

===Original===
This was received with great applause, and the speaker smiled with satisfaction. Then gradually his expression became sad.

“I am sorry to say,” he said﻿—“I am ashamed as a citizen of our great land to be obliged to admit, that there are at home a few craven-hearted, mean-spirited men﻿—shall I call them men? No, nor even women﻿—there are creatures, I say, who disapprove of our glorious deeds, who spurn the flag and the noble principles for which it stands and to which I have alluded, who say that we have no business to take away land which belongs to other people, and that we have not the right to slaughter rebels and traitors in our midst. I appeal to the patriotic Cubapinos at this board, if we are not introducing a higher and nobler civilization into these islands.”

The native gentlemen bowed assent.

“Have we not given them a better language than their own? Have we not established our enlightened institutions? For instance, let me cite the custom house. We have the collector here with us﻿—and the post-office. The postmaster is﻿—”

===Chinese===
这番话博得满堂喝彩，演说者满意地微笑了。随后，他的表情渐渐转为悲戚。

“我很沉痛地说，”他开口了——“作为一个伟大国度的公民，我不得不承认这一点，实在引以为耻：我们国内有少数怯懦卑劣之徒——我配称他们为‘男人’吗？不，连‘女人’都不配——我说，那是些东西，他们不赞成我们的光辉业绩，唾弃这面旗帜，唾弃她所代表、也是我方才论及的崇高原则；他们说什么我们无权夺走属于别人的土地，说什么我们无权屠戮身处我们之中的叛军和叛徒。我且请问席上各位爱国的古巴平诺人（Cubapinos）：我们是不是正在把这些岛屿引入一个更高尚、更尊贵的文明？”

几位本地绅士欠身躬首，表示赞同。

“我们岂不是给了他们一种比他们自己的更好的语言？我们岂不是建立了我们开明的制度？譬如说，请允许我举海关为例。海关税务司今晚就在我们中间——还有邮政局。邮政局长是——”

===Original===
“Sh-sh-sh!” whispered the prompter again. “He’s in jail.”

“I mean the assistant postmaster is also with us. And there are our other institutions, the﻿—”

“There’s going to be a prizefight tonight,” cried a young lieutenant who had taken too much wine, at the foot of the table. “Dandy Sullivan against Joe Corker.”

This interruption was too much for the commissioner, who was quite unable to resume the thread of his remarks for several moments. The guests in the meantime moved uneasily in their seats, for most of them were anxious to be off to see the fight.

“Those who carp against us at home,” continued the speaker, trying in vain to find some graceful way of coming to a close, “those who dishonor the flag are the men who pretend to be filled with humanity and to desire the welfare of mankind. They pretend to object to bloodshed. They are mere sentimentalists. They are not practical men. They do not understand our destiny, nor the Constitution, nor progress, nor civilization, nor glory, nor honor, nor the dear old flag, God bless her. They are sentimentalists. They have no sense of humor.”

===Chinese===
“嘘——嘘——嘘！”那位提词的又悄声道，“他坐牢去了。”

“我是说，副邮政局长今晚也在我们中间。还有我们其他的种种制度，譬如——”

“今晚有一场职业拳击赛！”桌子下首一个酒喝多了的年轻中尉嚷道，“丹迪·沙利文（Dandy Sullivan）对阵乔·科克（Joe Corker）。”

这一打断叫专员吃不消，好半天接不上自己话头的线。席间宾客如坐针毡，因为多数人都惦记着赶紧去看比赛。

“国内那些对我们吹毛求疵的人，”演说者接着说，徒劳地想找个体面的收束，“那些玷污这面旗帜的人，偏偏自称满怀人道、一心谋求人类福祉。他们装作反对流血。他们不过是些感情用事之徒。他们不是实干家。他们不懂我们的天命，不懂宪法，不懂进步，不懂文明，不懂光荣，不懂荣誉，也不懂这面亲爱的老旗——上帝保佑她。他们是感情用事之徒。他们没有幽默感。”

===Original===
Here the audience applauded loudly, although the speaker had not intended to have them applaud just there. It occurred to him that he might just as well stop at this point, and he sat down, not altogether satisfied, however, with his peroration and vexed to think that he had forgotten Sam altogether. The party broke up without delay, and Sam walked off with Cleary, who had been present, to see the prizefight.

“The commissioner isn’t much of a talker, is he?” said Cleary. “That was a bad break about the postmaster. I hear they’ve arrested Captain Jones for embezzlement too.”

“Good heavens!” cried Sam, “what an outrage!” And he told Cleary of his narrow escape from complicity in the matter, and how the military operations had prevented him from calling on the contractors. “Civilians don’t understand these things,” he added. “They oughtn’t to send them out here. They don’t understand things.”

“No. They haven’t been brought up on tabasco sauce. What can you expect of them?”

===Chinese===
说到这里，满座掌声雷动，虽然演说者本无意让他们在这儿鼓掌。他转念一想，索性就此打住也好，便坐下了——只是对这收尾不甚满意，又懊恼自己竟把萨姆忘了个一干二净。宴会当即散了席，萨姆同在座的克利里一道走，看拳击去。

“这位专员算不上什么演说家，是不是？”克利里说，“邮政局长那句话失言失得够呛。我还听说，琼斯上尉（Jones）也因贪污给抓起来了。”

“天哪！”萨姆嚷道，“真是岂有此理！”于是他对克利里讲起，这件事上自己如何险些沾上干系，又如何因军事行动缠身，未能去拜访那些承揽商。“这些事，平民是弄不懂的，”他又补了一句，“就不该把他们派到这儿来。他们什么都不懂。”

“可不是。他们不是拿塔巴斯科辣酱喂大的，你还指望他们怎样？”

===Original===
They soon arrived at the Alhambra Theater at which the fight was to take place, and found it in progress. A large crowd was collected, consisting of soldiers and natives in equal proportions. The last round was just finishing, and Joe Corker was in the act of knocking his opponent out. The audience was shouting with glee and excitement, the cheers being mixed with hisses and cries of “Fake, fake!”

“I know Corker,” said Cleary. “Come, I’ll introduce you.”

They pushed forward through the crowd, and were soon in a room behind the stage, where Corker was being rubbed and washed down by his assistants. Sam looked at the great man and felt rather small and insignificant. “Here’s a kind of civilian who is not inferior to army men,” he thought. “Perhaps he is even superior.” He would not have said this aloud, but he thought it.

===Chinese===
两人不久便到了举行比赛的阿尔罕布拉剧院（Alhambra），比赛已在进行。场子里聚着一大群人，士兵和土人各占一半。最后一回合刚见分晓，乔·科克正把对手打翻在地。观众兴高采烈地吼成一片，喝彩声里夹着嘘声和“假打！假打！”的叫嚷。

“我认识科克，”克利里说，“来，我给你引见引见。”

他们从人堆里挤过去，不多时便到了后台的一间屋里，科克正让助手们又擦又洗地伺候着。萨姆打量着这位大人物，自觉渺小得很，无足轻重。“这一号平民并不比军人差，”他想，“说不定还更高一筹。”这话他不会说出口，心里却这样想。

===Original===
“How de do, Joe?” said Cleary, shaking hands. “That was a great fight. You knocked him out clean. Here’s my friend, Colonel Jinks, the hero of San Diego and the pacifier of the Moritos.”

Corker nodded condescendingly.

“We enjoyed the fight very much,” said Sam, not altogether at his ease. “It reminded me of my own experience at East Point.”

“It was a good fight,” said Corker, “and a damned fair one too. I’d like to punch the heads of those fellers who cried ‘fake.’ It was as fair as fair could be, and Dandy and me was as evenly matched as two peas. I always believe in takin’ a feller of your size, and I did.”

“That wasn’t the way at East Point,” said Cleary. “They didn’t take fellows of their size there.”

“That’s against our rules anyway,” said Corker.

===Chinese===
“你好啊，乔？”克利里握着他的手说，“打得出色。你把他干脆利落地撂倒了。这位是我的朋友金克斯上校——圣迭戈的英雄，绥靖莫里托人的功臣。”

科克居高临下地点了点头。

“这场比赛我们看得非常过瘾，”萨姆说，颇有些局促，“它叫我想起本人在东点（East Point）的经历来。”

“这一场打得不赖，”科克说，“而且公平得要命。我恨不得敲扁那几个嚷‘假打’的家伙的脑袋。这比赛公平得不能再公平，我和丹迪旗鼓相当，好比两颗豌豆一个样。我一向主张挑跟自己个头相当的打，我这回就是这么干的。”

“东点可不是这个路数，”克利里说，“在那儿，他们专挑个头不相当的动手。”

“反正那违反我们的规矩。”科克说。

===Original===
“It must be a civilian rule,” said Sam, beginning to feel his superiority again. “The military rule as we were taught it at East Point was to take a smaller man if you could, and you see, the army does just the same thing. We tackled Castalia and then the Cubapines, and they weren’t of our size. We don’t fight the powerful countries.”

“That’s queer,” said Corker, drinking a lemonade.

“It’s perfectly right,” said Sam. “When a man’s in the right, and of course we always are, if he fights a man of his size or one bigger than he is, he gives the wrong a chance of winning, and that is clearly immoral. If he takes a weaker man he makes the truth sure of success. And it’s just the same way with nations.”

Corker did not seem to be much interested by this disquisition, and Cleary dragged his friend away after they had respectfully bade the pugilist good night. A crowd of soldiers was waiting outside to see Corker get into his carriage. They paid no attention whatever to Sam and Cleary.

“When it comes to real glory a prizefighter beats a colonel all hollow,” said Cleary, and they parted for the night.

===Chinese===
“那准是平民的规矩，”萨姆说，优越感又回来了，“我们在东点学的军规是：能挑小的就挑小的。你瞧，军队干的就是这一套。我们先收拾卡斯塔利亚（Castalia），再收拾古巴平（Cubapines），他们都够不上我们的个头。强国，我们是从来不打的。”

“这可就怪了。”科克说，喝着柠檬水。

“这再正当不过了，”萨姆说，“一个人既然站在理上——我们当然是永远站在理上的——倘若他去同个头相当的人、或者比他更壮的人交手，那就是给谬误留下获胜的机会，这分明是不道德的。他若挑弱者下手，真理便稳操胜券。国家之间，也是一模一样的道理。”

科克对这篇宏论似乎不感兴趣，两人恭恭敬敬向这位拳师道了晚安，克利里便拽着朋友走开了。外面一群士兵正等着看科克登车，对萨姆和克利里理都不理。

“论起真正的荣光，拳击手能把上校盖得一丝不剩。”克利里说。当夜两人就此分了手。

===Original===
Sam was retained on the general staff and assigned to the important post of censor of the press. His duties were most engrossing, for not only were the proofs of all the local newspapers submitted to him, but also all other printed matter. One day a large number of handbills were confiscated at a printer’s and brought in for his inspection. He was very busy and asked his native private secretary to look them over for him. In a half-hour he came to him with a translation of the document.

“What does it say?” cried Sam. “I have no time to read it through.”

“It says that governments are made to preserve liberty, and that they get their only authority from the free will of the people who are ruled by them,” answered the clerk.

“That’s clearly seditious,” said Sam. “There must be some plot at the bottom of it. Have the whole edition burned and have the printer locked up.”

===Chinese===
萨姆留在总参谋部，得了个要职——新闻检查官。他的公务缠身得很：不但本地各报的清样都要送他审阅，其余一切印刷品也统统归他管。有一天，一批传单在一家印刷所被查抄，送到他跟前待审。他忙得很，便叫他的本地私人秘书替他过目。半个钟头后，秘书拿着一份文件的译文来了。

“上面写的什么？”萨姆嚷道，“我没工夫从头读一遍。”

“上面说，政府是为保障自由而设的，它们唯一的权力，来自受其统治的人民的自由意志。”秘书答道。

“这分明是煽动叛乱，”萨姆说，“背后一定藏着什么阴谋。把整版全部烧掉，把印刷商关起来。”

===Original===
A few days later a newspaper was brought to him announcing that the Moritos had massacred the garrison stationed among them, that the whole province of San Diego was in revolt, and that the regiment there would probably have to fall back on Havilla. Sam was much scandalized, and sent at once for the native editor.

“What does this mean?” said he.

“Pardon, my colonel,” said the little man apologetically, “this is a newspaper and this is news. I am sure it is true.”

“That is the civilian conception of news,” said Sam, with disdain. “Officially this is not true. We have instructions, as you have often been told, not to allow anything to be printed that can injure the Administration at Whoppington. Anyone can see how this would injure it, and news that can injure it is, from the military point of view, untrue. General Notice is making a tour of the country at home, receiving ovations everywhere on account of the complete subjugation of the islands. What effect will such news have upon his reception? Is it a proper way to treat a general who has deserved well of his country?”

“But,” interposed the editor, “don’t the people know that you are continually sending out more troops?”

“The people do not mind a little thing like that,” said Sam. “When an officer and a gentleman says the war is over, they believe it, and they show their gratitude by voting money to send new regiments. Your action in printing this stuff is most disloyal. I will send one of my assistants around to your office with you to see that this edition is destroyed, and if you repeat the offense you will be deported.”

===Chinese===
几天后，一份报纸送到他手上，报上说莫里托人屠了驻在他们中间的守备队，圣迭戈全省叛乱四起，驻在那里的那个团恐怕不得不退守哈维拉。萨姆震怒不已，立刻把本地的那位编辑传来。

“这是怎么回事？”他问。

“恕罪，上校大人，”那小个子赔着不是，“这是报纸，这可是新闻。我担保是真的。”

“那是平民对新闻的理解，”萨姆不齿地说，“官方认定，这不是真的。我们奉有训令——这话对你说过不知多少遍了——凡有损沃平顿（Whoppington）当局的文字，一概不得付印。谁都看得出这条新闻有损于它；而有损于它的新闻，从军事观点看，便是不真实的。诺蒂斯（Notice）将军正在国内四处巡游，因这些岛屿已告彻底荡平而处处受人欢呼。这样的消息，会给他的接待抹上什么光彩呢？这样对待一位有功于国家的将军，难道是妥当的吗？”

“可是，”编辑插嘴道，“你们源源不断往外派兵，难道人民不知道吗？”

“这点小事，人民是不会计较的，”萨姆说，“军官兼绅士说战争结束了，他们就信，还投票拨款、再派新的团来，以表感激。你刊登这种东西，是最大的不忠。我这就派一名助手随你去报馆，监督销毁这一版；你若再犯，就把你驱逐出境。”

===Original===
The unfortunate man retired, shrugging his shoulders. As he went out Cleary came running in with a copy of the paper.

“Oh! you’ve got a copy of that, have you?” said Sam. “It’s an outrage to print such things, isn’t it?”

“I’m afraid it’s true,” said Cleary.

“What difference does that make?” exclaimed Sam. “It’s the business of an army to conquer a country. We’ve done it twice, and we can do it as often as we like again.”

“Hear, hear!” cried Cleary. “You’re becoming more and more of a soldier as you get promoted. You have the true military instinct, I see. Of course it makes no difference who holds the country, but I’m a little disappointed in the Moritos. As for San Diego, Colonel Booth of your old regiment is in command, and I half think he didn’t back up the Morito garrison out of jealousy toward you. He wanted to have the Morito country go back, so as to belittle our exploit. But we’ll get even with him. I’ve seen the cable-censor, and not a word about it will go home. I have just sent a despatch saying that the whole island is entirely in our hands and that the natives are swearing allegiance by thousands.”

===Chinese===
那倒霉蛋耸耸肩膀退了出去。他前脚刚走，克利里就拿着一份报纸跑了进来。

“哦！你也弄到一份，是吗？”萨姆说，“登这种东西真是岂有此理，你说是不是？”

“恐怕是真的。”克利里说。

“那又有什么相干？”萨姆嚷道，“军队的本分就是征服一个国家。我们已经干过两回了，愿意再干多少回都成。”

“好哇，好哇！”克利里嚷道，“你官越升越大，兵也当得越来越地道了。我看，你有一种真正的军人本能。这国家归谁拿着，当然毫不相干；只是莫里托人有点叫我失望。至于圣迭戈——你老团队的那位布思上校（Booth）在那里指挥，我总疑心他没去救援莫里托守备队，是出于对你的嫉妒。他巴不得莫里托地盘失守，好贬低咱们那桩壮举。不过，这笔账咱们早晚要同他算的。电报检查官那边我去过了，一个字也传不回国内。我刚刚发了一篇通讯，说全岛已尽入我们掌中，土人正成千上万地宣誓效忠。”

===Original===
“That’s right,” said Sam. “It’s really a kindness to the people at home, for if they think it’s true it makes them just as happy as if it were true, and I think it’s positively cruel to worry them unnecessarily.”

“To be sure,” said Cleary. “And if it does get out, we’ll throw all the blame on the Secretary of War and his embalmed beef. They say he’s writing a book to show that a diet of mummies is the best for fighting men﻿—and so the quarrels go on. By the way, I just stopped a piece of news that might have interested you. Do you know that you have suppressed the Declaration of Independence?”

“Nonsense. I haven’t seen a copy of it in two years.”

“Well, here’s a despatch that I got away from the cable-office just in time. It would have gone in another ten minutes. Here it is.”

Sam took the paper and read an account of the printing by a native committee of fifty thousand copies of the Declaration in Castalian, and its immediate suppression by Colonel Jinks, the censor.

“It’s a downright lie,” cried Sam. “I’ll call my native secretary and inquire into this,” and he rang his bell.

===Chinese===
“这就对了，”萨姆说，“这对国内的人民实在是一桩善举。他们只要信以为真，就同真的一样快活；我认为，平白无故叫他们操心，未免太狠心了。”

“可不是，”克利里说，“万一真走漏了出去，咱们就把过错一股脑儿推给陆军部长和他的防腐牛肉（embalmed beef）。听说他正在写一本书，要证明拿木乃伊当口粮，最益于战斗人员——于是这些争吵就没完没了。对了，我刚拦下一条新闻，你听了兴许感兴趣。你可知道，你把《独立宣言》给查禁了？”

“胡说。这两年我连一份都没见过。”

“喏，这份电报，是我赶得正是时候从电报局手里截下来的。再过十分钟它就发出去了。你瞧。”

萨姆接过电稿，读到一条报道：本地一个委员会印了五万份卡斯塔利亚文的《独立宣言》，当即被新闻检查官金克斯上校查禁。

“这是彻头彻尾的谎话！”萨姆嚷道，“我把我的本地秘书叫来问个明白。”说着他摇了铃。

===Original===
“See here, what does this mean?” he asked the clerk who hurried in.

The man thought a minute.

“I do not know the Declaration of Independence,” he said, “but perhaps that paper I translated for you the other day had something to do with it. I have not a copy here.”

“Were they burned?”

“Not yet, sir. They were seized, and are in our depot.”

“Come,” said Sam to Cleary, “let’s go over there and look at it. It’s a half-mile walk and it will do me good.”

===Chinese===
“你过来，这是怎么回事？”他问匆匆进来的秘书。

那人想了一会儿。

“我不认得什么《独立宣言》，”他说，“不过前几天我替您译的那张纸，兴许同它有些干系。我手头没有留存。”

“烧掉了吗？”

“还没有，长官。已经查抄了，全存在咱们的仓库里。”

“走，”萨姆对克利里说，“咱们上那边瞧瞧去。半英里路，走走对我也有好处。”

===Original===
“How are things at San Diego?” asked Sam, as they walked along together. “You’ve been out there, haven’t you?”

“Yes. We’ll have to come in. The Cubapinos have got a force together at a town farther down the river and are threatening us there. We got pretty near them and mined under a convent they were in, and blew up a lot of them, but it didn’t do them much harm, for a lot of recruits came in just afterward from the mountains. That convent was born to be blown up, it seems, for some Castalian anarchists had a plot to blow it up some years ago, and came near doing it, too. We made use of their tunnels, which the monks were too lazy to have filled up. The anarchist plot was found out, and they garroted a dozen of them.”

“What inhuman brutes those anarchists are!” cried Sam. “Think of their trying to blow up a whole houseful of people! I wish we could take some one of the smaller islands and put all the anarchists of the world there and let them live out their precious theories. Just think what a hell it would be! What infernal engines of hatred and destruction they would construct, if they were left to themselves﻿—machines charged with dynamite and bristling with all sorts of explosive contrivances!”

“Something like a battleship,” suggested Cleary.

“Don’t talk nonsense!” exclaimed Sam. “Only Castalian fiends would try to destroy law and order and upset the peaceable course of society in such a way. Do you suppose that any of our people at home would do such a thing?”

“None, outside of the artillery,” answered Cleary. “Well, at any rate, our blowing up of the convent didn’t do much good. There was some talk of putting poison in the river to dispose of them, but of course we couldn’t do that.”

===Chinese===
“圣迭戈那边情形怎样？”两人一路走时萨姆问，“你到那边去过，是吧？”

“去过了。咱们不得不撤回来。古巴平诺人在河下游一座镇子上集结了一支兵力，在那边威胁我们。我们摸到离他们很近的地方，在他们盘踞的一座修道院底下埋了炸药，炸飞了他们好一批人，可也没伤着他们多少元气，因为紧接着又有一大批新兵从山里补充进来。那座修道院，看来天生就是个挨炸的命：几年前有几个卡斯塔利亚无政府主义者就密谋炸它，还差一点儿得手。我们这回用的正是他们挖的坑道——修士们懒得堵，一直没堵。无政府分子的密谋当年败露了，他们绞死了十来个。”

“那些无政府主义者真是惨无人道的畜生！”萨姆嚷道，“想想吧，他们竟要炸掉满满一屋子人！我真想找一座小些的岛，把全世界的无政府主义者统统弄到那儿去，让他们把那套宝贵理论施行到底。想想那会是一副什么人间地狱！他们要是由着性子来，会造出多么凶残的仇恨与毁灭的机器——装满炸药、浑身支棱着各式爆炸机关的家什！”

“有点儿像一艘战列舰。”克利里提了一句。

“别胡说八道！”萨姆喝道，“只有卡斯塔利亚的恶魔，才会想这样毁掉法律与秩序、搅乱社会安宁的运转。你以为，我们国内会有人干这种事吗？”

“没有，炮兵除外。”克利里答道，“唉，反正咱们炸那座修道院，也没炸出多大好处。有人提议往河里下毒把他们解决掉，那种事我们自然干不出来。”

===Original===
“Of course not,” said Sam. “That would be barbarous and against all military precedents. The rules of war don’t allow it.”

“They’re rather queer, those rules,” answered his friend. “I should like my enemies to take notice that I prefer being poisoned to being blown up with bombshells. In some respects they don’t pay much attention to the rules, either. They don’t take prisoners much nowadays. Most of my despatches now read, ‘fifty natives killed,’ but they say nothing of wounded or prisoners.”

“We’re fighting savages, we must remember that,” said Sam.

“Then we’ve got a way of trying our pistols and rifles on natives working in the fields; it’s rather novel, to say the least. I saw one man in the 73rd try his new revolver on a native rowing a boat on the river, and over the fellow toppled and the boat drifted downstream. The men all applauded, and even the officers laughed.”

===Chinese===
“自然干不得，”萨姆说，“那是野蛮行径，违反一切军事成例。战争规则不容许。”

“那些规矩可够古怪的，”他的朋友答道，“我倒要请我的敌人记住：比起挨炮弹炸死，我宁可叫他们毒死。在某些方面，他们也不大把规则当回事。如今他们不大抓俘虏了。我如今发的战报多半写着‘毙敌土人五十名’，负伤的和被俘的却只字不提。”

“我们打的是野蛮人，这一点须记着。”萨姆说。

“再说，我们还有一套拿在田里干活的土人试手枪、试步枪的法子；说得客气些，这也算得新颖。我亲眼看见第七十三团的一个家伙，拿河上摇船的一个土人试他的新左轮手枪，那人一头栽了下去，小船顺水漂走了。士兵们个个喝彩，连军官都笑了。”

===Original===
“Boys will be boys,” said Sam, smiling. “They’re good shots, at any rate.”

“They are that. There were some darkies plowing up there just this side of San Diego, and some of our fellows picked them off as neatly as you please. It must have been eight hundred yards if it was a foot. But somehow I don’t quite like it.”

“War is war,” said Sam, using a phrase which presumably has a rational meaning, as it is so often employed by reasonable people. “It doesn’t pay to be squeamish. The squeamish men don’t make good soldiers. I’ve seen enough to learn that. They hesitate to obey orders, if they don’t like them.”

As he said this they passed a small crowd of boys in the street. They were trying to make two dogs fight, but the dogs refused to do so, and the boys were beating them and urging them on.

“What stupid brutes they are,” said Sam. “They’re badly trained.”

“They haven’t had a military education,” responded Cleary. “But I almost forgot to ask you, have you seen the papers from home this morning? They’re all full of you and your greatness. Here are two or three,” and he took them from his pocket.

Sam opened them and gazed at them entranced. There was page upon page of his exploits, portraits of all kinds, biographies, anecdotes, interviews, headlines, everything that his wildest dreams had imagined, only grander and more glorious. There was nothing to be seen but the words “Captain Jinks” from one end of the papers to the other.

===Chinese===
“男孩子终归是男孩子嘛。”萨姆微笑着说，“不管怎么说，枪法是好的。”

“那可不。就在圣迭戈近郊这一边的地里，有几个黑人雇工正犁着地，咱们几个伙计把他们一个个撂倒，干净利索之至。少说也有八百码，只多不少。可不知怎么，我心里不大是滋味。”

“战争就是战争。”萨姆说。他用的这句话想必有其道理，因为通情达理的人们时常把它挂在嘴边。“心慈手软划不来。心慈手软的人当不成好兵。这个道理我见得够多，早明白了。命令不合心意，他们就要迟疑着不肯执行。”

正说这话，他们打街上一小群男孩子身边走过。孩子们正撺掇两条狗打架，狗偏不肯，孩子们便揍它们、催它们。

“这些蠢畜生，”萨姆说，“训练得太差了。”

“他们没受过军事教育呗。”克利里应道，“咳，我差点儿忘了问你：今早国内的报纸你见着没有？全是讲你和你的丰功伟绩的。这儿有两三份。”说着他从口袋里掏了出来。

萨姆打开报纸，如痴如醉地凝望着。一页又一页全是他的功绩：各式各样的画像、传记、轶事、访谈、通栏标题——他最狂野的梦想里想象过的一切应有尽有，只是更宏大、更辉煌。从报纸这头到那头，除了“金克斯上尉”几个字，旁的什么也看不见。

===Original===
“They’ve even got a song about you,” said Cleary. “Here it is:

> ‘I’m Captain Jinks of the horse-marines.
 

 I feed my horse on corn and beans.
 

 Of course it’s quite beyond my means,
 

 Tho a captain in the army!’ ”

“I don’t altogether like it,” said Sam. “What are the horse-marines? I don’t believe there are any.”

“Oh, that doesn’t make any difference. It seems it’s an old song that was all the go long before our time, and your name has revived it. It will advertise you splendidly. The whole thing is a grand piece of work for *The Lyre*. Jonas has been congratulating me on it. He’d come and tell you so, but he doesn’t want to be seen with you. You’ve censured out everything I’ve asked you to for him, and he doesn’t want people to know about his pull. That’s the reason why he’s never called on you. But he says it’s the best newspaper job he ever heard of. I tell you we’re a great combination, you and I. Perhaps I’ll write a book and call it, *With Jinks at Havilla*. Rather an original title, isn’t it? But I’m afraid that all this talk at home will not make you very popular with the officers here, who knew you when you were only a captain. What would you say to being transferred to Porsslania? They want new men for our army there, and I’ve half a mind to go too for a change and act as the *Lyre*’s correspondent there. They’ll do anything I ask them now.”

“I’d like it very much,” said Sam. “I’m tired of this literary business. But here we are. This is our depot.”

===Chinese===
“人家还给你编了一支歌呢，”克利里说，“你听：

> “我是骑兵陆战队的金克斯上尉，
 

  我拿玉米豆子把我的马儿喂，
 

  这开销自然远非我财力所及，
 

  虽说我在陆军里官拜上尉！”

“这个我可不怎么喜欢，”萨姆说，“骑兵陆战队是些什么玩意儿？我不信真有这么一号。”

“哦，那不要紧。这好像是一支老歌，早在咱们出世之前就风行过，你的名字又把它唱红了。这给你做的广告可漂亮极了。整桩事情，对《竖琴报》（The Lyre）来说是一手漂亮活儿。乔纳斯（Jonas）为这个向我道了好几回喜。他本想亲自来向你道贺，只是不愿让人瞧见他跟你在一起。我求你替他撤下的那些东西，你一样样都撤了，他可不愿意人家知道他走了你的门路。这就是他从不来拜访你的缘故。不过他说，这是他平生所知最漂亮的一桩新闻买卖。告诉你吧，咱们俩凑在一块儿，可真是天作之合。说不定我还要写一本书，就叫《金克斯在哈维拉》（With Jinks at Havilla）。这书名够别致的，是不是？只是我怕国内这一片嚷嚷，叫你在这儿的军官们中间吃不开——他们认识你的时候，你还只是个上尉呢。调到波斯兰尼亚（Porsslania）去，你看怎么样？咱们在那边的军队正缺新人，我也动了心，想换个地方，去当《竖琴报》驻那边的记者。如今我求他们什么事，他们没有不答应的。”

“我很愿意去，”萨姆说，“这种舞文弄墨的差事我腻烦透了。喏，到了，这就是咱们的仓库。”

===Original===
The two men entered the long low building in which confiscated property was stored. A soldier who was acting as watchman showed them where the circulars were piled. Cleary took one and glanced over it.

“As sure as fate, it’s the Declaration of Independence!” he laughed.

Sam took up a copy and looked at it too.

“I believe it is,” he said. “I didn’t half look at it the other day. I’m ever so much obliged to you for telling me and stopping the telegram. But between you and me, the circular ought to be suppressed anyway. What business have these people to talk about equal rights and the consent of the governed? The men who wrote the Declaration﻿—Jeffries and the rest﻿—were mere civilians and these ideas are purely civilian. Come, let’s have them burned at once,” and he called up two or three soldiers, and in a few minutes the circulars formed a mass of glowing ashes in the courtyard.

===Chinese===
两人走进那座存放查抄财物的长而低矮的房子。一个充任看守的士兵领他们到堆放传单的地方。克利里抽出一张，扫了一眼。

“千真万确，正是《独立宣言》！”他笑起来。

萨姆也拿起一张看了看。

“我看也是，”他说，“那天我没怎么细看。多亏你告诉我，又把电报拦了下来，真不知怎么谢你才好。不过这话只限咱俩之间：这传单横竖也该查禁。这些人有什么资格谈什么平等权利、谈什么被统治者的同意？写《宣言》的那帮人——杰弗里斯（Jeffries）之流——不过是些平民，这些念头纯粹是平民的念头。来，马上烧掉。”他唤来两三个士兵，不多时，那些传单便在院子里化作了一堆通红的灰烬。

## XV Politics / 十五 政治

===Original===
But Marian did mind, and for once Cleary was mistaken. She was delighted at the prominence which Sam had achieved, and saw him mentioned as a candidate for President with pride and gratification, but she did not see how that excused his promiscuous osculation of the female population of the country, and she determined that it should cease. She wrote to him frequently and decidedly on the subject, and he reported her protests to Cleary, who absolutely refused to allow them.

“It won’t do,” said he, as they discussed the subject at a hotel in a small city on their line of progress. “This kissing is your strong point. *The Lyre* is backing you up on the strength of it. So is the Benevolent Assimilation Trust, Limited. In every city and town the girls have turned out, and you’ve captured them hands down. If you stop now it will upset the whole business. The Convention delegates are coming out for you by the dozen. Our committee is working it up so that it will be nearly unanimous. There won’t be another serious candidate, and I doubt if they put anybody up against you when you’re nominated. You’re as good as President now, but you must go on kissing. That’s all there is of it.”

Sam wrote to Marian rehearsing these arguments, and he got Cleary to write too, but the letters had no effect. At last he received a telegram from her announcing her intention of meeting him at St. Lewis. She reached that city before him and was present at the station when he arrived, although he did not know it, and from a good point of vantage she saw him kissing the young ladies of that city by wholesale to an accompaniment of “Captain Jinks.” It was more than she could stand, and when she joined her fiancé at the hotel the meeting was very different from the one he had so often pictured to himself. It was a stormy scene, intermixed with tender episodes, but she gave it as her ultimatum that the kissing must cease forthwith, and, in order to give a good reason for it, she insisted that they be married at once. Sam was willing to take this course, and Cleary was called into their counsels. At first he bitterly opposed the project, but Marian’s blandishments finally succeeded, and she gained him as an ally. He was sent as an emissary to the campaign committee and presented the case as strongly as he could for her. The proposition really seemed most plausible. Could anything help the chances of a candidate more than his marriage to a handsome young woman? The committee had doubts on the subject and waited in person on Miss Hunter, but she persuaded them as she had persuaded Cleary, and furthermore convinced them that whether they were persuaded or not the marriage would take place. Marian determined to fix the hour for the next day. She pledged the committee to secrecy, and no word of the proposed wedding got into the papers. At noon a clergyman was called into the hotel, and in Sam’s private sitting-room the pair were married with Cleary and a few of the members of the committee as witnesses. Almost before the ceremony was over they could hear the newsboys crying out the tidings of the event.

===Chinese===
可玛丽安偏偏介怀，克利里这回可是破天荒地失算了。萨姆声名显赫，她满心欢喜；见他被人提名为总统候选人，更觉骄傲欣慰。可她看不出，这怎么能赦免他对举国女性大众不加拣选的亲吻；她拿定主意，这桩事非终止不可。她就这事频频去信，言辞斩钉截铁；萨姆把她的抗议转告克利里，克利里却断然拒绝依从。

“这可使不得。”两人正沿着进军的路线，在一座小城的旅馆里议论这事，克利里说道。“亲吻是你的看家本领。《里拉报》正凭着这个替你撑腰，善意同化托拉斯有限公司（Benevolent Assimilation Trust, Limited）也一样。每个城镇的姑娘们都倾巢而出，你不费一兵一卒便把她们尽数俘虏。这时候要是住手，整盘的营生都得砸锅。大会代表成打成打地站出来拥你。咱们的委员会正在加紧运作，好叫表决近乎全票。不会再冒出第二个像样的候选人，我看等你获得提名时，他们连一个跟你对垒的都懒得推出。你这会儿同当上总统已无两样，可你必须接着亲下去。就这么回事，没有二话。”

萨姆写信给玛丽安，把这些道理原原本本陈说了一遍，又拉克利里也写了信，可这些信全然无效。末了，他收到她一封电报，说她决意到圣刘易斯来同他会面。她赶在他之前抵达那座城，他到站时她正在月台上——他却毫不知情——她占了个视野极佳的位置，眼看他伴着《金克斯上尉》的歌声，把城里的年轻女士们成批成批地亲过去。这超出了她所能忍受的极限；到旅馆同未婚夫相见时，那场面同他无数次私下想象过的会面大异其趣。这是一场疾风骤雨式的争吵，中间也夹着几段温存缠绵的插曲；但她把话作为最后通牒挑明：亲吻必须即刻停止；而为了给这事一个名正言顺的缘由，她坚持两人立即成婚。萨姆愿意照此办理，克利里也被请来共商大计。起初他竭力反对这一着，可玛丽安的巧语温存终告奏效，把他争取成了同盟。他奉派充任密使，前往竞选委员会，竭尽所能替她陈说利害。这主张听上去当真头头是道。一位候选人娶上一位年轻貌美的女子，天下还有什么比这更能抬他的行情？委员会对此心存疑虑，便亲自登门拜会亨特小姐，可她说服了他们，一如她说服克利里；而且进一步叫他们信了：不论被说服与否，这桩婚事都办定了。玛丽安决意把吉时定在第二天。她要委员会立誓保密，拟议中的婚礼便半个字也没漏进报端。正午时分，一位牧师被请进旅馆，在萨姆的专用起居室里，这一对结了婚，克利里和委员会的几名委员充当证婚人。婚礼几乎尚未完毕，他们就听得见报童在街上叫卖这桩喜讯了。

===Original===
“It’s out of the question to talk about a wedding-tour,” said Sam, after the ceremony. “I can’t walk in the streets alone without being mobbed, and with Marian we could not keep the clothes on our backs. Just hear them singing ‘Captain Jinks’ now!”

“Mark my words, dear,” said his wife. “You will see when we get the papers tomorrow with the news of our marriage, that it has made you more popular than ever. Now send out word to the reporters that you will not do any more public kissing.”

In obedience to these orders Cleary, acting as go-between, conveyed the information as gently as he could to the representatives of the press, that as a married man General Jinks expected to be spared the ordeal of embracing all the young ladies of the country.

===Chinese===
“蜜月旅行什么的，想都不用想。”仪式过后，萨姆说道。“我一个人上街都会被围得水泄不通，带上玛丽安，咱们连身上的衣裳都保不住。你听听，这会儿他们不是又唱起《金克斯上尉》来了嘛！”

“记住我的话，亲爱的，”他妻子说。“等明天咱们看到登着咱们婚讯的报纸，你就会明白，这一结婚反倒叫你比从前更得人心了。这就传话给记者们：你不再当众亲吻了。”

克利里奉命行事，居间转达，尽量委婉地把消息告知报界诸位代表：金克斯将军既已成家，恳请体谅，免受拥抱全国年轻女士的苦刑。

===Original===
No one was prepared for the striking effect which this news, coupled with that of the marriage, had upon the newspapers and their readers. The first papers which Sam and his wife saw on the following morning were those of St. Lewis. They expressed sorrow at the fact that Captain Jinks had taken such a resolution when only a handful of the fair women of St. Lewis had had the opportunity of saluting him. Were they less beautiful and attractive than the ladies of St. Kisco who had kissed him to their hearts’ content? Marian was visibly annoyed when she saw these articles, but she advised her husband to wait till they received the papers from other cities. These journals came, but, alas! they went rapidly from bad to worse. The Eastern papers with scarcely an exception took up the strain of those of St. Lewis. Why did Captain Jinks discriminate against the women of the East? He had kissed the whole West. Probably he had also kissed all the women of the Cubapines and Porsslania. It was only the women of the East that he could not find heart to salute in the same way. Here was a hero indeed, who insulted one-half of his own nation! It might have been expected that the Western press would have come to Sam’s support, but they did not. They accused him of gross deception in not announcing that he had been from the first engaged to be married. Their young women had been fraudulently induced to kiss lips which had already been monopolized, but which they had been led to believe to be as free as the air of heaven. Black indeed must be the soul of a man who could stoop to such deception! As the days went on the public became more excited and the attacks more ferocious. It was rumored that his fiancée had married him against his will, that she was a virago and a termagant. Would the country be contented to see the Executive Mansion ruled by petticoats, and by those of a hussy at that? What sort of a hero was the man who could be ordered about by a woman and could not call his soul his own? Then they began to overhaul his record. Was he really the hero of San Diego? Was it not the mistakes of Gomaldo which caused his defeat? Was it not true that the boasted subjugation of the Moritos was brought about by the superstitious fear of the savages inspired by the figures tattooed on the captain’s body? And the capture of Gomaldo, was it anything but a green-goods game on a large scale? What, too, was the burning of the great White Temple but an act of vandalism? And as for the friendship and praise of the Emperor, who was the Emperor, anyway, but an effete product of an exhausted civilization? Then had not Captain Jinks opposed the promotion of men from the ranks? What sort of a democrat was this? Sam felt these thrusts keenly. He had had no idea of the fickleness of the people, and it was hard to believe that in a single day they had ceased to adore him and begun to revile him; and yet such was the case. Marian was also overcome with mortification, and she heaped reproaches upon him for their forlorn condition. Cleary proved himself to be a stanch friend.

“It’s too bad, old man,” he said. “It’ll blow over, but you’ll have to withdraw a while for repairs. The bottom has dropped out of your boom, and of course you can’t be a candidate for President. Let’s go quietly home. I’ll go along with you. *The Lyre* has had to drop you for the time. *Scribblers’* has sent back the first article I wrote for you, and they say your name has lost its commercial value. I’ve seen Jonas. He’s here to make sure of a friendly candidate, and he says you’re out of the question. He’s doing well, I tell you. I asked him how it paid to run a war for half a million a day and get a trade in return of a few millions a year? ‘It’s the people pay for the war and we get the trade,’ said he. He’d like to have you President to help them along, but he says it won’t be possible. It’s a shame. You’d have run so well, if﻿—Your platform of ‘Old Gory, the Army and Navy,’ would have swept everything before it. But never mind. We’ll try it again some day. I suppose your luck couldn’t hold out forever.”

“Thanks, my dear Cleary,” said Sam, grasping his hand. “You’ve been a true friend. I don’t think it makes much difference. I am a sick man, and I must go home as soon as I can.”

===Chinese===
这条消息连同婚讯一道，在报界和读者中间激起的轰动效应，是谁都始料未及的。第二天早晨，萨姆夫妇最先读到的是圣刘易斯的报纸。报上深表痛惜：圣刘易斯的美人儿只有寥寥一小撮曾得机缘向金克斯上尉行此吻礼，他竟在这样的时候立下了这样的决心。难道她们不及圣基斯科的女士们美丽动人么？人家可是把他亲了个心满意足。玛丽安读到这些文章，分明着了恼，但她劝丈夫且等各地报纸寄到再说。报纸是寄来了，可是唉！风头急转直下，一天坏似一天。东部各报几乎无一例外，都接过了圣刘易斯的调子。金克斯上尉为什么歧视东部的女子？整个西部他都亲遍了。古巴平和波斯兰尼亚的女人，只怕也让他亲了个够。唯独东部的女子，他竟狠不下这条心，用同样的方式去亲一亲、致个意。好一位英雄，竟侮辱了自己同胞的一半！按说西部报纸总该出来替萨姆撑腰，却没有。它们指责他存心欺瞒，竟不早点宣布自己打从一开始便订了婚。它们的年轻姑娘是受了诱骗，才去亲那早已被人独占的嘴唇，而她们原本被哄得相信，那嘴唇同天上的空气一样自由无主。肯堕落到使出这般骗局的人，心肠得有多么黑！日子一天天过去，民众越来越激愤，攻击越来越凶狠。有谣传说，他的未婚妻是违背他本人的意愿强嫁给他的，又说她是个悍妇、一个泼辣货。这个国家难道甘心眼看总统府受裙钗的辖制，而且还是个贱妇的裙钗？一个听凭女人差来喝去、连自己的灵魂都做不得主的男人，算哪门子英雄？接着，他们开始彻底清查他的老底。他真是圣迭戈的英雄吗？戈马尔多之所以败亡，岂不是他自己失误所致？吹得震天响的镇服莫里托人之功，岂不是靠上尉身上刺的文身图案在野蛮人心中勾起的迷信恐惧才得手的？至于俘获戈马尔多，那除了一场放大了规模的绿钞骗局（green-goods，旧时流行的假钞骗术）还能是什么？还有焚烧大白庙，那不又是一场野蛮的破坏行径？至于那位皇帝的友谊和夸奖——皇帝究竟算个什么人物？不过是一个耗竭了的文明孵出来的孱弱产物罢了。再者，金克斯上尉不是反对过从行伍中提拔人吗？这算得哪门子民主派？这一记记戳刺，萨姆感着钻心的疼。他从前万万没有想到民心竟这般反复无常：仅仅一天工夫，百姓便不再崇拜他，反倒辱骂起他来——这令人难以置信，然而事实偏偏如此。玛丽安也羞愤得无地自容，把他们落到这般凄惶的田地，一盆盆责备劈头盖脸浇到他身上。克利里倒显出了他是个靠得住的朋友。

“真太糟了，老伙计，”他说。“这阵风头会过去的，可你得暂且退下场子，检修一番。你这行情已经垮到了底，总统候选自然是当不成了。咱们悄悄回家去吧，我陪你一道走。《里拉报》一时也只好把你放下了。《文痞》杂志（Scribblers’）把我给你写的头一篇文章退了回来，还说你的名字已经失了商业价值。我见过乔纳斯（Jonas）了。他到这儿来是要确保推出一个对他们友善的候选人，他说你已经不在考虑之列。他干得不赖，告诉你吧。我问他：一天花上五十万去打一场仗，换回的不过是一年几百万的生意，这买卖怎么个划算法？‘仗是老百姓出钱打的，生意可归我们做，’他说。他倒巴不得你当上总统，好帮衬他们一程，可他说这已经办不到了。可惜呀！你本来准能一路顺风地选下去，要是——你那‘老血旗、陆军与海军’（Old Gory，谐仿星条旗俗称‘老光荣’Old Glory）的政纲，本可以横扫一切、所向披靡。不过别往心里去，咱们来日再卷土重来。我琢磨着，你的运气总不能一辈子常胜不败吧。”

“谢谢你，亲爱的克利里，”萨姆握住他的手说。“你真够朋友。我看这也没什么大不了的。我是个病人，得尽快回家去。”

## V Slowburgh / 五 斯洛堡

===Original===

While Sam accepted the explanations of the editor and Jonas as expressions of wisdom from men who had had a far wider experience than his, he had some faint misgivings as to some of the business enterprises in which his new friends were embarked, and he hinted as much to Cleary.

“Some of those things do sound rather strange,” answered Cleary, as they walked away, “but you must look at the world in a broad way. Is our civilization better than that of the Cubapinos?”

“Undoubtedly.”

“Well, then, we must be conferring a favor upon them by giving it to them. We can’t slice it up and give them only the plums. That would be ridiculous. They must take us for better and worse. In fact, I think we should be guilty of hypocrisy if we pretended to be better than we are. Suppose we gave them a better civilization than we’ve got, shouldn’t we be open to the charge of misrepresentation?”

“That’s true,” said Sam. “I didn’t think of that.”

“Yes,” Cleary went on; “at first I had some doubts about that saloon business particularly, but the more you think of it, the more you see that it’s our duty to introduce them there. It’s all a part of our civilization.”

===Chinese===

萨姆把编辑和乔纳斯（Jonas）那番解释，当作见多识广的长者的真知灼见接受了下来，可对这些新朋友正在经营的一些生意，他心里仍隐隐有些不放心，便把这层意思向克利里（Cleary）露了露。

“那些事儿听着是挺玄乎的，”克利里一边走开一边答道，“可看世界得把眼光放宽些。咱们的文明是不是比古巴平人（Cubapinos）的高明？”

“那还用说。”

“那么，咱们把文明送给他们，就是赏了他们天大的恩典。总不能把它切成小块，光把里头的好东西挑出来给他们——那就可笑了。是好是歹，他们得把咱们照单全收。说实在的，咱们要是装得比实底儿更好，反倒成了虚伪。倘若咱们送他们一份比咱们自己手里还好的文明，人家岂不要告咱们一个名不副实？”

“这话不假，”萨姆说，“我可没想到这一层。”

“可不是，”克利里接着说，“起先我对那桩酒馆生意尤其犯嘀咕，可你越琢磨越明白：把酒馆引进到那边去，正是咱们的本分。它本来就是咱们文明的一部分嘛。”

===Original===

“So it is,” said Sam. “And then people have always done things that way, haven’t they?”

“Yes, of course they have.”

“Then it must be all right. What right have we to criticize the doings of people so much wiser than we are? I think you are quite right. As a correspondent you ought to be satisfied that you are doing the right thing. To me as a soldier it’s a matter of no importance anyway, because a soldier only does what he’s told, but you as a civilian have to think, I suppose, and I’m glad you’re satisfied and can make such a conclusive case of it. What was it that the editor wanted you to tell me?”

“Oh! yes. I came near forgetting. You see what a lot they’re going to do for us; now we must help them all we can. They want you to leave behind with them all the material about yourself that you can get together. You must get photographed at Slowburgh in a lot of different positions, and in your cadet uniform and your volunteer rig when you get it. Then you must let them have all your earlier photos if you can. ‘Hero Jinks as an infant in arms,’ ‘Hero Jinks in his baby-carriage,’ ‘Hero Jinks as a schoolboy’﻿—what a fine series it would make! You know what I mean. Then you must write your biography and your opinions about things in general, and give the addresses of all your friends and relations so that they can all be interviewed when the time comes. You’ll do it, won’t you? It’s the up-to-date way of doing things, and it’s the only way to be a military success.”

===Chinese===

“本来就是，”萨姆说，“再说，人们办事从来都是这么办的，对吧？”

“对，从来都是。”

“那就准没错了。人家比咱们高明那么多，咱们有什么资格对人家的事业说三道四？我看你说得很对。你当记者的，理应确信自己干的是正经事才好。我当兵的倒是无所谓——兵只管听令行事；可你是平民，总得自己动脑子想，你能想得通、把这番道理讲得这么滴水不漏，我真替你高兴。对了，编辑要你捎话给我什么来着？”

“哦！对了。差点儿忘了。你瞧，人家要为咱们出这么大力气，咱们也得全力帮衬他们。他们要你把能搜罗到的关于你自己的材料统统留在他们那儿。你得在斯洛堡（Slowburgh）拍照，摆出各式各样的姿势，穿上你的学员制服拍一套，等你领到志愿兵装束，再穿上拍一套。然后，你还得尽量把早年各个时期的照片都交给他们——什么‘襁褓中的英雄金克斯’‘婴儿车里的英雄金克斯’‘上学堂的英雄金克斯’，凑起来该是多漂亮的一组系列啊！你懂我的意思。接着你还得写一份自传，谈谈你对天下万事的见解，再把亲朋好友的地址统统留下，到时候好挨个儿采访。你会办的，对不对？如今时兴的办事路数就是这样，要当个成名的军人，舍此别无他途。”

===Original===

“If it’s the proper way of doing things I’ll do it,” said Sam.

“That’s a good fellow! I’ll send you a list of questions to answer and coach you as well as I can. I’m dying to get off and have this thing started. Isn’t Jonas great? He’s got just my ideas, only bigger. You see, he explained to me that in this country trusts have grown up with great difficulty, and it was hard work to establish the benefits which they produce for the public. They were fought at every step. But in the Cubapines we have a clean field, and by getting the Government monopoly whenever we want it, we can found one big trust and do ever so much good. I half wish I were a Cubapino, they’re going to be benefited so, and without doing anything to deserve it either. Some people are born lucky.”

“I can’t quite follow all those business plans,” said Sam. “My head isn’t trained to it; but I’m glad we’re going to do good there, and if I can do something great to bring it about, it will give me real happiness.”

“It will, old man, it will. I’m sure of it,” cried Cleary, as he took his leave of Sam in front of the hotel. “Let me know what steamer you’re going by as soon as you get orders, and I’ll try to manage it to get a passage on her too. They often carry newspaper men on our transports.”

===Chinese===

“只要事情理应这么办，我就照办。”萨姆说。

“这才是好伙计！我会寄一张问题单子给你作答，再尽力给你指点指点。我巴不得这就动身，把这一摊事张罗起来。乔纳斯是不是个角色？他的想法跟我的一模一样，只是气派更大。他是这么给我解释的：在咱们国内，托拉斯是千难万难才长起来的，要把它们带给公众的种种好处立起来，费的劲儿可大了，一步一步都有人跟你斗。可在古巴平（Cubapines），咱们面前是一片现成的白地，什么时候想要，就能拿到政府专营权，可以办起一家大大的托拉斯，行天大的善。我简直恨不得自己是个古巴平人——天大的好处眼看要落到他们头上，他们还一点儿都不用费劲去挣。有人天生就是好命啊。”

“那些生意经我实在跟不太上，”萨姆说，“我这脑袋没受过那方面的训练；不过听说咱们要去那边行善，我很高兴，倘若我能干出一番大事促成这件事，那会给我真正的幸福。”

“会的，老伙计，会的。这我拿得准。”克利里在旅馆门前同萨姆道别时嚷道，“你一接到命令，就告诉我坐哪班轮船，我设法也在那条船上弄个铺位。咱们那些运输舰上，常常是捎带新闻记者的。”

===Original===

On the following day Sam went to visit his uncle at Slowburgh, a small seaport of some four thousand inhabitants lying several miles away from the railroad. The journey in the train occupied six hours or more, and Sam spent the time in learning the Castalian language in a handbook he had bought in town. He had already taken lessons in the language at East Point and was beginning to be fairly proficient. He alighted at the nearest station to Slowburgh and entered the rather shabby omnibus which was standing waiting. Sam felt lonely. There was nothing military about the station and no uniform in sight. He no longer wore a uniform himself, and the landscape was painfully civilian. Finally the horses started and the bus moved slowly up the road. Sam was impatient. His fellow countrymen were risking their lives thousands of miles away, and here he was, creeping along a country road in the disguise of a private citizen, far away from the post of duty and danger. He looked with disgust at the plowmen in the fields busily engaged in preparing the soil for next year’s grain.

“What a mean, poor-spirited lot,” he thought. “Here they are, following their wretched plows without a thought of the brave soldiers who are defending their country and themselves so many leagues away. It is the soldier, suffering from hunger and fever and falling on the battlefield in the agony of death, who makes it possible for these fellows to spend their days in pleasant exercise in the fields. The soldier bears civilization on his back, he supports all the rest, he is the pedestal which bears without complaint the civilian as an idle ornament. The soldier, in short, is the real man, the only perfect product of creation.”

===Chinese===

第二天，萨姆动身去斯洛堡看望叔叔。那是座小海港，居民约四千，离铁路线有好几英里。火车一坐就是六个多钟头，萨姆一路捧着在城里买的一本手册自学卡斯塔利亚语（Castalian）。这门语言他在东点（East Point）时已经上过课，如今算得上相当纯熟了。他在离斯洛堡最近的车站下了车，钻进那辆正候着的、颇为寒碜的公共马车。萨姆觉得孤单。车站没有一丝军事气息，放眼望不见一件制服。他自己也不再穿军装了，四下的景致平民得叫人难受。马儿终于起步，车子顺着大路慢慢往上走。萨姆心急如焚：同胞们正在几千里外拿性命冒险，他却装扮成一个平民百姓，在乡间土路上磨磨蹭蹭，远远离开那职责与危险所在的岗位。他厌恶地望着田里正忙着为来年的谷物整地的农夫们。

“这帮东西真是又卑贱又没出息。”他心想。“他们赶着自家那破犁，对几里格外正保卫着他们的国家、也保卫着他们本人的英勇士兵，连想都不想一下。正是士兵——挨着饿、发着烧、在战场上承受临终剧痛扑倒在地的士兵——才使得这帮家伙能天天在田野里做做舒坦的健身运动。士兵把文明驮在背上，把其余一切都扛起来；他是那毫无怨言的基座，把平民当作一件游手好闲的摆设高高托住。总而言之，士兵才是真正的人，是造物唯一完美的成品。”

===Original===

And his heart was filled with thankfulness that he had selected the career of a soldier and that there never could be any doubt of his usefulness to the world. The only other occupants of the omnibus were two men﻿—one of them a commercial traveler, and the other an aged resident of Slowburgh who had been at the county town for the day, as Sam gathered from their conversation.

===Chinese===

他心头充满感恩：幸亏自己选定了军人的生涯，幸亏自己对世界的用处永远无可怀疑。公共马车里除他之外只有两名乘客——一个是旅行推销员，另一个是斯洛堡的老住户；萨姆从他们的谈话里听出，老爷子这天是上县城去了。

===Original===

“I don’t suppose that the war has caused much excitement at Slowburgh?” asked Sam at last, introducing the subject uppermost in his mind.

“It ain’t jest what it was when I went to the war,” said the old man; “but there is a deal o’ talk about it, and all the young men are wanting to go.”

“Are they?” cried Sam, in delight. “And did you serve in the war? How very interesting! Did you offer your life for your country without hope of reward?”

“That’s just what I did, young man, and if you doubt it, here’s my pension that I drew today in town, twelve dollars a month, and they’ve paid it now these thirty-four years.”

===Chinese===

“这仗打到今天，斯洛堡怕是没掀起多大动静吧？”萨姆终于开了口，把他心里头一等的大事提了出来。

“比起俺当年打仗那阵儿是差点儿意思，”老爷子说，“不过谈这仗的可多着哩，小伙子个个都想去。”

“当真？”萨姆欢喜地叫起来，“这么说您当年打过仗？多有意思啊！您是不指望报偿、把性命献给国家的吧？”

“俺献的正是这条命，年轻人。你要是不信，俺今儿在城里刚领的抚恤金就在这儿——一个月十二块，如今都发给俺三十四个年头啦。”

===Original===

“That’s a pretty soft thing,” said the commercial man. “Better’n selling fountain-pens in the backwoods.”

“A soft thing!” cried the old man, “I ought to have twice as much. There’s Abe Tucker gets fifteen dollars because he caught cold on picket duty, and I get a beggarly twelve.”

“Were you severely wounded?” asked Sam.

“Well, no-o-o, not exactly, tho I might just as well ’a’ been. I was down bad with the measles. This is an ongrateful country. Here it is only thirty-five years after the war, and they’re only paying a hundred and forty millions a year to only a million pensioners. It’s a beggarly shame!”

===Chinese===

“这可是桩现成的肥差啊，”推销员说，“总比在穷乡僻壤推销自来水笔强。”

“肥差！”老爷子嚷道，“俺本该拿双份才对。人家艾布·塔克（Abe Tucker）站个岗着了凉，一个月就拿十五块，俺才拿这寒碜的十二块。”

“您伤得重吗？”萨姆问。

“呃，不——不——不，说不上，不过俺跟挂了彩也差不离。俺那年害麻疹，病得不轻呢。这是个没良心的国家。仗打完才三十五年，一年才掏一亿四千万，才养活一百万领抚恤金的。寒碜，真寒碜！”

===Original===

“Were there that many men in the war?” asked the traveler.

“Pretty near it, I reckon. But p’r’aps in thirty-five years there’d be a natural increase. Think of it, a million men throwing away their lives for a nothing like that! I jest tell our young fellers that they’d better stay at home. Why, we’ve had to fight for what we’ve got. You wouldn’t think it, but we’ve had to pass around the hat, and shove it hard under the nose of Congress, too, just as if we were beggars and frauds, and as if we hadn’t sacrificed everything for our country!”

“It’s an outrage,” cried Sam sympathetically. “But I hope you won’t keep the young men from going. I’m going soon, and perhaps the country will be more generous in future.”

“Take my advice, young man, and whenever anything happens to you while you’re away, take down the names of the witnesses and keep their affidavits. Then you’ll be all ready to get your pension as soon as you come back. It took me three years to straighten out mine. Then I got the back pay, of course, but I ought to have had it before. I’ve got a claim in now for eight dollars more a month running all the way back. It amounts to over three thousand dollars, and I ought to have it.”

“Was that for the measles, too?” asked the stranger.

===Chinese===

“打仗的人有那么多吗？”推销员问。

“俺估摸着，差不离吧。不过三十五年下来，兴许也自然添了些数。你琢磨琢磨，一百万人，就为这么点儿玩意儿把命都豁出去了！俺就直跟咱的小伙子们说，还是待在家里好。唉，俺们到手的这点儿可是争来的。你怕是不信，俺们得挨个儿递帽子凑钱，还得把帽子死命往国会鼻子底下凑，倒好像俺们是要饭的、是骗子，倒好像俺们没为国家牺牲过一切似的！”

“这简直是岂有此理，”萨姆深表同情地嚷道，“不过您可别拦着小伙子们不去。我不久也要去了，往后国家兴许会大方些。”

“听俺一句劝，年轻人：往后在外头不管出点儿什么事，都把证人的名字记下来，把他们的宣誓书收好。这样你一回来，领抚恤金就万事齐备了。俺这份抚恤，足足磨了三年才办利索。补发的钱后来倒是拿到了，可本来早该给俺的。俺这会儿还递着一份状子，要求每月再加八块，从早先一路补起。加起来有三千多块呢，这钱理应归俺。”

“这也是为那回麻疹要的？”陌生人问。

===Original===

The old man glared at his interrogator, but did not deign to reply.

“Our Congressman, old Jinks, has my claim,” he said, turning to Sam. “But he doesn’t seem to be able to do anything with it.”

“He’s my uncle,” said Sam, fearing that he might hear something against his worthy relative.

“So you’re George Jinks’ nephew, are you? Are you goin’ to be a captain? Do tell! I read about it in the Slowburgh *Herald* last week. I’m real glad to see you. You’re the first officer I’ve seen in ten years except the recruiting officer last week.”

“Did they have a recruiting officer here, in Slowburgh?” asked Sam.

===Chinese===

老爷子狠狠瞪了盘问他的人一眼，却不屑于答话。

“俺那份状子在咱们国会议员老金克斯手里，”他转向萨姆说，“可他好像办不出个名堂来。”

“他是我叔叔。”萨姆说，心里直打鼓，生怕接着听见什么对他那位可敬长辈不敬的话。

“原来你是乔治·金克斯（George Jinks）的侄子，是吧？你要去当上尉了？真新鲜！俺上礼拜在斯洛堡《先驱报》（Herald）上看到的。见着你真叫人高兴。除了上礼拜那个募兵官，十年里你是俺见着的头一个军官。”

“斯洛堡这儿还来过募兵官？”萨姆问。

===Original===

“Yes, they did, and there was thirteen fellers wanted to go, but he only took five of ’em, and they hain’t gone yet. The rest was too short or too fat or too thin or something.”

“Didn’t any more men want to go than that?”

“No,” said the old man. “They all want to wear soldier-clothes, but they don’t all want to go fighting. They’ve got up a militia battalion for them now, and ’most everybody in town’s got a uniform. I hadn’t seen a uniform in the county before in I don’t know how long﻿—except firemen, I should say.”

“I’m so glad they’ve got them now,” cried Sam. “Doesn’t it improve the looks of the place? It’s so much more homelike and-d-d glorious, don’t you think so?”

The old man had no opportunity to reply, as the bus now drew up at the front door of the principal hotel. The commercial traveler got out first and went into the house; the old man followed, and turning to Sam as he passed him, he said with a glance at the vanishing stranger:

===Chinese===

“来过，可不。有十三个小子报了名，可他只挑了五个，那五个到现在还没走成呢。剩下的不是太矮，就是太肥、太瘦，反正总有毛病。”

“想去的就只有这么几个？”

“可不，”老爷子说，“军装人人都想穿，真去打仗可就不是人人肯的了。如今给他们拉起了一个民兵营，镇上差不多人人都弄了一身制服。这个县里，俺都有多少年没见着制服了——消防队的倒见过，那得另说。”

“如今总算有了，我太高兴了，”萨姆嚷道，“这地方是不是顿时体面多了？又亲切，又——又——又威武，您说是不是？”

老爷子还没来得及答话，马车已在头等旅馆的大门前行住了。推销员头一个下了车，进了屋；老爷子跟着下去，走过萨姆身边时回身对他说，眼睛瞟着那个正消失在门里的陌生人：

===Original===

“He’s a copperhead, that feller.”

He went on toward the barroom door, but called back as he went:

“If you get lonesome over at Jinks’, come in here in the evening. Ask for me; my name’s Reddy.”

===Chinese===

“那家伙是条‘铜头蛇’（copperhead，北方骂反战分子的土话）。”

他朝酒吧间的门走去，一边走一边回头喊道：

“你要是在金克斯家住得闷了，晚上就上这儿来。找俺就行，俺叫雷迪（Reddy）。”

===Original===

Sam did not get out of the omnibus, but told the driver to take him to Congressman Jinks’; and on they went, first to the right and then to the left along the wide and gently winding streets, which would have been well shaded with maples if the yellow leaves had not already begun to fall. They drove in at last through a gate in a wooden fence and round a semicircular lawn to the front of a comfortable frame house, and in a few moments he was received with open arms by his relations.

Congressman Jinks was a widower and had several children, all of whom, however, were away at school except his eldest daughter, a young lady of Sam’s age, and his youngest, a girl of seven. The former, Mary, was a tall damsel with fair hair and a decidedly attractive manner. Mr. Jinks reminded Sam of his father with the added elegancies of many years’ life at the Capital.

“Well, Samuel, I am glad to see you at last. We know all about you, and we’re expecting great things from you,” he cried out in a hearty voice. Sam felt at home at once.

“Come, Mary, show your cousin his room. Here, give me your grip. Yes, you must let me carry it. Now get ready for supper as soon as you can. It’s all ready whenever you are.”

===Chinese===

萨姆没有下公共马车，吩咐车夫送他去国会议员金克斯家。马车继续前行，顺着宽阔而略带弯曲的街道忽而向右、忽而向左，两旁的枫树本该浓荫匝地，只是黄叶已经开始飘零。最后，马车从木栅栏的一道门里驶入，绕过半圆形的草坪，停在一幢舒适妥帖的木板房前。不多时，他便被亲戚们张开双臂迎了进去。

国会议员金克斯是个鳏夫，膝下好几个孩子，不过除了长女——一位与萨姆同岁的年轻小姐——和才七岁的幼女，其余都在外地上学。长女玛丽（Mary）是个高挑的姑娘，一头金发，举止十分动人。这位金克斯先生令萨姆想起自己的父亲，只是添了在京城生活多年熏染出的种种雅致。

“好啊，塞缪尔，总算见着你了。你的事我们全知道，都等着你干一番大事业呢。”他嗓音热诚地嚷道。萨姆顿时有了到家的感觉。

“来，玛丽，带你表哥去看看他的房间。来，提包给我。对，就得让我拎。你们快些收拾停当，下来吃晚饭吧。你们几时得空，饭几时都是现成的。”

===Original===

After supper they all sat round a wood fire, for it was a little chilly in the evening now. Mr. Jinks had his little girl in his lap, and they talked over family history and the events of the day. Sam asked who Mr. Reddy was whom he had met in the train.

“Oh! you mean old Reddy. Was he drunk? No? That’s odd.”

===Chinese===

晚饭后，一家人围着一炉柴火而坐——如今晚间已有些凉意了。金克斯先生把小女儿抱在膝上，大家谈起了家史和当日的大小事。萨姆问起他在火车上遇见的那位雷迪先生是何许人。

“哦！你说的是老雷迪啊。他没喝醉吧？没醉？那可怪了。”

===Original===

“He’d been away for the day drawing his pension,” said Sam.

===Chinese===

“他那天是进城领抚恤金去了。”萨姆说。

===Original===

“Of course,” said Mr. Jinks. “I might have known it. That is his one sober day in the month. He sobers up to go to town, but he’ll make up for lost time tonight. That twelve dollars will last just a week, and it all goes into the barroom till. He’s been that way ever since I was a boy, though they say he was a steady enough young fellow before he went to the war. It’s a curious coincidence, but there are two or three old rum-soaked war veterans like that hanging round every tavern in the country, and I’d like to know how much pension money goes that way. It’s a great system though, that pension system. I see something of it in Whoppington when I’m attending Congress. It distributes the money of the country and circulates it among the people. I like to see the amount increase every year. It’s a healthy sign. I’m trying to get some more for Reddy. It helps the county just that much. Swan, the hotel man, spends it here. I believe in protecting home industries and fostering our home market. I wish you could have heard my speech on the war-tax bill﻿—it covered that point. My, how this war is costing, though! A million dollars a day! But it’s well worth it. The more money we spend and the higher the taxes, the more circulation there is. You ought to see how things are booming at Whoppington. I’m sorry you couldn’t come to see me there, but I had to be here this week looking after election matters in my district. In Whoppington all the hotels are full of contractors and men looking for commissions in the army, and promoters and investors, all with an eye to the Cubapines. You can just see how the war has brought prosperity!”

“I should have liked to see Whoppington very much,” said Sam, “but I suppose I must wait till I come back. It must be very different from other cities. You must feel there as if you were at the center of things﻿—at the very mainspring of all our life, I mean.”

“You’ve hit the nail on the head,” said his uncle. “Whoppington holds up all the rest of the country. There is the Government that makes everything go. There’s no business there to speak of; no manufacturing, no agriculture in the country round﻿—nothing to distract your attention but the power of the Administration that lies behind all the rest. Just think what this country would be without Whoppington! Just imagine the capital city sinking into the ground and what would we all do? Even here at Slowburgh what would be left for us?”

===Chinese===

“那可不，”金克斯先生说，“我本该料到的。那是他一个月里唯一清醒的一天。进城之前他醒醒酒，今儿晚上可要连本带利补回来。那十二块钱只撑得住一个礼拜，末了一个子儿不剩，全进酒吧间的钱柜。打我做孩子那会儿起他就是这副德行，虽说人们讲，他打仗之前本是个挺正派的年轻人。说来也怪，全国哪家酒馆门口不吊着这么两三个酒糟浸透了的退伍老兵，我倒真想知道，抚恤金里头有多少是这么个流法。不过那抚恤金制度可是个了不起的制度。我在沃平顿（Whoppington）出席国会期间，多少见识过一点儿。它把国家的钱分发出去，在老百姓手里流转。每年见涨，我瞧着就高兴，这是兴旺的兆头。我正设法给雷迪再多争一点儿，这可是实实在在帮衬了本县——开旅馆的斯旺（Swan）老板，钱还不是照旧花在这儿？我信奉保护本土实业、培育本地市场这一套。可惜你没赶上听我那篇关于战争税法案的演说，里头正谈到了这一点。天哪，这场仗打得可真费钱！一天一百万美元！可这钱花得值：钱花得越多、税抽得越高，市面上流转得越欢。你真该去瞧瞧沃平顿那副热火朝天的景象。可惜你没能上那儿看我，不过这个礼拜我必须回来，照应本选区的选举事务。在沃平顿，各家旅馆住满了承包商、想在军队里谋个一官半职的人，还有各路发起人、投资人，个个都盯着古巴平。你瞧瞧，这场仗带来了多大的繁荣！”

“我本想极了去见识见识沃平顿，”萨姆说，“看来只好等我回来了。那儿想必跟别的城市大不一样。在那儿，人准会觉得自己正处在万事的中心——我是说，正处在我们全部生活的那根主发条上。”

“你这话说到点子上了，”叔叔说，“是沃平顿把全国其余的地方托起来的。那儿有政府，是政府让一切都转起来。当地说不上有什么买卖，没有制造业，四郊也没有农业——除了那隐在其余一切背后、支配着一切的行政权力，再没有什么分你的心。想想看，这个国家要是没了沃平顿会成什么样子！想象一下首都陷进地里去，咱们大家可怎么办？就说咱们斯洛堡吧，还能剩下什么？”

===Original===

“Wouldn’t we have breakfast tomorrow morning, papa?” asked the little girl in his lap.

“Er-er-well, perhaps we might have breakfast﻿—”

“Wouldn’t we have clothes, papa?”

“Perhaps we might have﻿—but no, we couldn’t either; it’s the tariff that gives us our clothes by keeping all foreign clothes out of the country, and then we shouldn’t have er-er﻿—”

===Chinese===

“那我们明天早上就没有早饭吃了吗，爸爸？”他膝上的小女儿问。

“呃——呃——这个，早饭嘛，兴许还吃得上——”

“那我们没有衣裳穿了吗，爸爸？”

“兴许还能有——不，不，这个也不行；咱们的衣裳全靠关税，靠关税把外国的衣裳统统挡在国门外头；没了它，咱们就没有那个——呃——呃——”

===Original===

“It would upset the post-office,” suggested Sam, coming to the rescue.

“Yes, to be sure, that is what I meant. It would cause a serious delay in the mails, that’s certain.”

“And then there would be no soldiers,” added Sam.

“Of course. How stupid of me to overlook that. How would you like to see no soldiers in the street?”

===Chinese===

“邮局也就乱套了。”萨姆出来解围道。

“对，可不是，我正是这个意思。邮件势必要大大耽搁，这是千真万确的。”

“还有，士兵也一个不剩了。”萨姆又添上一句。

“当然。我怎么把这茬儿给漏了，真笨。街上一个士兵也瞧不见，你乐意吗？”

===Original===

“I shouldn’t like it at all, papa.”

“Yes, my dear boy,” he proceeded, turning to Sam, “I would not want to have it repeated in my district, but I confess that I am always homesick for Whoppington when I am here. That’s the real world there. There’s the State Department where they manage all the foreign affairs of the world. What could we do without foreign affairs? And the Agricultural Department. How could we get in our crops without it? And the Labor Department. Every man who does a day’s work depends on the Labor Department for his living, we may say. And the﻿—”

“The War Department,” said Sam.

“Yes, the War Department. We depend on that for our wars. Perhaps at first that does not seem to be so useful, but﻿—”

===Chinese===

“我才不乐意呢，爸爸。”

“是啊，好孩子，”他转向萨姆接着说，“这话在我的选区我可不愿意让人传出去，不过说句实话，我一到这儿来就想沃平顿，跟害了思乡病似的。那儿才是真正的世界。那儿有国务院，全世界的外交事务全归它掌管。没有了外交事务，咱们可怎么办？还有农业部，没有农业部，咱们的庄稼怎么收得回来？还有劳工部。可以说，凡是做一天工挣一天饭吃的人，哪个的生计不指着劳工部？还有——”

“还有陆军部。”萨姆说。

“对，陆军部。咱们的仗全指望它呢。乍一想，它似乎没有那么要紧，可是——”

===Original===

“Oh! but, Uncle George, surely it is the most useful of all. What could we do without wars. Just fancy a country without wars!”

“I don’t know but you’re right, Sam.”

“And then the Treasury Department depends a good deal on the War Department,” said Sam, in triumph, “for without the War Department and the army it wouldn’t have any pensions to pay.”

“That’s so.”

“Papa,” said Mary Jinks, who had modestly taken no part in a conversation whose wisdom was clearly beyond her comprehension﻿—“papa, why didn’t everybody go to the war like Mr. Reddy, and then they’d all have pensions and nobody’d have to work.”

===Chinese===

“哦！可是乔治叔叔，它分明是顶顶有用的一个。没有仗打，咱们可怎么办？想想看，一个没有战争的国家！”

“我看你说得也在理，萨姆。”

“再说，财政部在很大程度上还得仰仗陆军部呢，”萨姆得意扬扬地说，“要是没有陆军部和军队，它连抚恤金都没的可发了。”

“正是这么回事。”

“爸爸，”玛丽·金克斯说——这场谈话里的高深学问显然非她所能领会，她一直知趣地一声没吭——“爸爸，为什么大家不都学雷迪先生的样子去打仗呢？那样人人都有抚恤金，谁也不必干活儿了。”

===Original===

“It’s their own fault if they didn’t,” answered her father; “and if some people are overworked they have only their own selves to thank for it. I have no patience with the complaints of these socialists and anarchists that the poor are getting poorer and the number of unemployed increasing. In a country with pensions and war taxes and a tariff there’s no excuse for poverty at all.”

“Yes,” said Sam, “they could all enlist if they wanted to.”

===Chinese===

“不去，那是他们自己的错，”她父亲答道，“有些人要是累坏了，也只能怪他们自己。什么穷人越来越穷啦、失业的越来越多啦，这些社会主义者和无政府主义者的抱怨，我可没有耐性听。在一个有抚恤金、有战争税、有关税的国家里，贫穷压根儿就没有借口。”

“说的是，”萨姆道，“他们只要愿意，全都可以去投军嘛。”

===Original===

The following day was spent in driving about the country. Mr. Jinks was obliged to visit the various centers in his Congressional district, and he took Sam with him on one of these expeditions. The country was beautiful in the clear, cold autumn air. The mountains stood out blue on the horizon, and the trees were brilliant with red and yellow leaves. Sam, however, had no eyes for these things. He was eager to hear about the militia company, and was pleased to see several pairs of military trousers, although they were made to do duty with civilian coats. Such for him were the incidents of the day. After supper in the evening he bethought him of old Reddy’s invitation to the hotel barroom, and thinking that he might learn more about the local military situation there, he excused himself and hied him thither. He found the room crowded with the wiseacres of the place, the Bohemian, drinking element perhaps predominating. The room was so full of smoke that, as Sam entered, he could hardly distinguish its contents, but he saw a confused mass of men in wooden armchairs tipped at every conceivable angle, surrounding a tall round stove which was heated white hot. The room was intensely warm and apparently totally wanting in ventilation.

“Here’s my friend, Captain Jinks,” said a husky voice which Sam recognized as that of old Reddy. “Here, take this chair near the fire.”

Sam accepted the offered chair, although he would have preferred a situation a little less torrid.

===Chinese===

第二天就在坐车游乡中打发了。金克斯先生必须到本选区的各个中心地点去跑一趟，其中一趟便带上了萨姆。清冽的秋日晴空下，乡间景致宜人：群山在地平线上显出一带黛蓝，满树木叶红黄斑斓。然而这一切萨姆都视而不见。他一心惦记着打听民兵连的事，路上望见几条军裤便喜不自胜——尽管这些军裤配的都是便装上衣。在他，这便是当天数得着的大事了。当晚晚饭后，他想起老雷迪邀他去旅馆酒吧间的话，寻思在那儿或许能多探听些本地军情，便告了个便，径直往那儿去了。只见屋里挤满了此地的诸路高人，放浪形骸的酒客一伙兴许还占着上风。满屋子烟雾弥漫，萨姆进门时几乎看不清屋里的家什，只见黑压压一团人，歪在东倒西歪、什么角度都有的木扶手椅里，围着一座烧得炽白滚烫的高圆炉子。屋里热得烤人，而且显然丝毫不通一点风。

“这位是我的朋友，金克斯上尉（Captain Jinks），”一个沙哑的嗓音说道，萨姆听出是老雷迪。“来，坐这把炉边儿的椅子。”

萨姆领情坐下了，虽说他也宁愿挑个不那么火烧火燎的位子。

===Original===

“Gentlemen, this is Captain Jinks,” said the old man, determined to get all the credit he could from his acquaintance with Sam. “Captain, this is my friend, Mr. Jackson.”

Mr. Jackson was a tall, thin, narrow-chested man with no shoulders, a rounded back, and a gray, tobacco-stained mustache. His face was covered with pimples, and a huge quid of tobacco was concealed under his cheek. He was sitting on a chair tipped back rather beyond the danger-point, and his feet rested on the rim which projected from the stove halfway up. He made no effort to rise, but slowly extended a grimy, clammy hand which Sam pressed with some hesitation.

“Glad to make your acquaintance, Captain,” he drawled in a half-cracked voice that suggested damaged lungs and vocal organs. “Shake hands with Mr. Tucker.”

===Chinese===

“诸位，这位就是金克斯上尉，”老爷子说，打定主意要把结识萨姆的好处一个不落地占尽。“上尉，这位是我的朋友，杰克逊先生（Mr. Jackson）。”

杰克逊先生是个瘦高个子，胸膛窄瘪，肩膀溜得几乎没有，后背佝偻，蓄着一撇烟渍斑斑的灰白八字胡。他满脸粉刺，腮帮子里还藏着一团嚼烟。他坐在一把往后仰得过了危险临界点的椅子上，两只脚翘在炉子半腰凸出的炉圈上。他没有起身的意思，只慢吞吞伸出一只乌黢黢、潮腻腻的手，萨姆迟疑了一下才握上去。

“幸会幸会，上尉，”他拖长了调子说，那半哑不哑的嗓音，听着就像肺和发声的器官都受了损伤。“再跟塔克先生（Mr. Tucker）握个手。”

===Original===

Mr. Tucker, a little, old, red-faced man on the other side of the stove, advanced and went through the ceremony suggested.

“We were just a-talking about them Cubapinos,” explained Reddy. “The idee of them fellers a-pitching into us after all we’ve done for ’em. It’s outrageous. They’re only monkeys anyway, and they ought to be shot, every mother’s son on ’em. Haven’t we freed ’em from the cruel Castalians that they’ve been hating so for three hundred years?”

“They seem to be hating us pretty well just now,” said a man in the corner, whose voice sounded familiar to Sam. He turned and recognized the commercial traveler of the day before.

“They’re welcome to hate us,” answered Jackson, “and when it comes to a matter of hating I shouldn’t think much of us if we couldn’t make ’em hate us as much in a year as the Castalians could in three hundred. They’re a blamed slow lot and we ain’t. That’s all there is of it. What do you think, Captain?”

===Chinese===

塔克先生是炉子另一边的一个红脸膛的小老头儿，他走上前来，照方才说的那套礼数走了一遭。

“俺们刚才正念叨这帮古巴平人哩，”雷迪解释道，“咱们为他们掏心掏肺，他们倒反咬咱们一口。真是岂有此理。反正他们不过是一群猴儿，一个个都该枪毙，一个也别留。咱们不是把他们从狠毒的卡斯塔利亚人（Castalians）手里解放出来了吗？他们恨那帮人恨了整整三百年哪。”

“眼下他们恨咱们，恨得似乎也挺见工夫嘛。”角落里一个人说，那嗓音萨姆听着耳熟。他转过头去，认出了头天火车上那位推销员。

“恨咱们？欢迎之至，”杰克逊答道，“要说恨人这门手艺，咱们要是一年之内叫他们恨咱们的份儿赶不上卡斯塔利亚人三百年挣下的，那我可就把咱们瞧扁了。他们是慢得要死的一路货色，咱们可不是。就是这么回事。你看呢，上尉？”

===Original===

“I fear,” said Sam, “that they don’t quite understand the great blessings we’re conferring on them.”

“What blessings?” asked the drummer.

“Why,” said Sam, “liberty and independence﻿—no, I don’t mean independence exactly, but liberty and freedom.”

“Then why don’t we leave them alone instead of fighting them?”

“What an idee!” exclaimed Tucker. “They don’t know what liberty is, and we must teach ’em if we have to blow their brains out.”

===Chinese===

“我怕的是，”萨姆说，“他们还不大明白咱们正赐给他们的那许多洪福。”

“什么洪福？”推销员问。

“这还用问，”萨姆说，“自由和独立——不，确切说，我要说的不是独立，是自由和解放。”

“那咱们干嘛不放过他们，反倒跟他们打？”

“这是什么话！”塔克嚷道，“他们哪儿懂自由是个什么东西，咱们就得教教他们——哪怕得把他们的脑浆子打出来。”

===Original===

“You’re too hard on ’em, Tucker,” drawled Mr. Jackson. “We mustn’t expect too much from pore savages who live in a country so hot that they can’t progress like we do.” Here Mr. Jackson took off his hat and wiped the beads of perspiration from his brow with a red bandana handkerchief. “Don’t expect too much from cannibals that have their brains half roasted by the tropical sun.”

“That’s a fact!” said someone in the throng.

“Yes,” said Jackson, crossing his legs on a level well above his head, “them pore critters need our civilization, that’s what they need,” and he dexterously squirted a mouthful of tobacco juice on the white-hot stove, where it sizzled and gradually evaporated. “We must make real men of ’em. We must give ’em our strength and vigor and intelligence. They’re a dirty lot of lazy beggars, that’s the long and short of it, and we must turn ’em into gentlemen like us!”

===Chinese===

“你对他们太苛刻了，塔克。”杰克逊先生慢条斯理地说。“一帮可怜的野人，住在那么热的一个国度里，热得没法儿像咱们这样进步，咱们不能对他们指望太高。”说到这儿，杰克逊先生摘下帽子，用一方红印花大手帕抹了抹额头的汗珠。“一帮脑子叫热带的太阳烤得半熟的吃人生番，别对他们指望太高。”

“这话不假！”人群里有人说。

“可不是，”杰克逊说着，把两条腿一盘，翘到比自个儿脑袋还高的份上，“这帮可怜虫缺的就是咱们的文明，缺的就这个。”他利利索索地啐出一口烟汁，正吐在烧得炽白的炉子上，嗞嗞响着，慢慢蒸发干了。“咱们得把他们造就成真正的男子汉，得把咱们的力气、元气、脑力都传给他们。说白了，他们就是一帮脏兮兮的懒叫花子，咱们得把他们调教成跟咱们一样的绅士！”

===Original===

A general murmur of approval followed this outburst.

“I hear,” said Sam, anxious to get some definite information as to the warriors of the town, “I hear that several Slowburghers are going to the war.”

“Yes,” said Tucker, while Jackson after his effort settled down into a semi-comatose state, “six of our boys are a-going. There’s Davy Black, he drives the fastest horse in these parts, and Tom Slade. Where is Tom? He’s generally here. They’ll miss him here at the hotel, and Jim Thomson who used to be bartender over at Bloodgood’s, and the two Thatchers﻿—they’re cousins﻿—that makes five.”

===Chinese===

这一番高论博得一片嗡嗡的附和。

“我听说，”萨姆急于打听些本镇武人的确切消息，便开口道，“听说有几位斯洛堡人要开拔去打仗了。”

“是哩，”塔克说。这时杰克逊卖过一番力气，已经陷入半昏迷的状态。“咱们的小伙子有六个要去。有戴维·布莱克（Davy Black），这一带就数他赶的马最快；还有汤姆·斯莱德（Tom Slade）。汤姆呢？他往常都在这儿的，旅馆这边要惦记他了。还有吉姆·汤姆森（Jim Thomson），从前在布拉德古德（Bloodgood）店里当酒保的；还有撒切尔家那两个小子——他们是表兄弟——这就五个了。”

===Original===

“The village ought to be glad they are going to represent her at the front,” said Sam.

“From all I can hear,” said the commercial man, “I think they are.”

“Naturally,” cried Sam, “it will reflect great glory on the place. You ought to be proud of them.”

“It’ll help the insurance business here,” said a young man who had not yet spoken.

“How is that?” asked Sam. “I don’t exactly see.”

===Chinese===

“这几位要去前线替本镇扬名，全镇该引以为荣才是。”萨姆说。

“照我听到的风声，”推销员说，“我想他们是引以为荣的。”

“那还用说，”萨姆嚷道，“这会给本地增添莫大的光彩。你们该为他们骄傲才是。”

“这还能提一提本地的保险生意哩。”一个先前还没开过口的年轻人说。

“此话怎讲？”萨姆问，“我还真没想明白。”

===Original===

“Well, it’s this way. You see I’m in the insurance business and I can’t write a policy on a barn in this township, there’s been so many burned; and while I don’t want to say nothing against anybody, we think maybe they won’t burn so much when the Thatchers clear out.”

“Nothin’ ain’t ever been proved against ’em,” said Tucker.

“That’s true,” said the young man, “but perhaps there might have been if they’d stayed. They say that Squire Jones was going to have Josh Thatcher arrested next week for his barn, but he’s agreed to let up if he’d go to the Cubapines. Maybe that isn’t true, but they say so.”

“I venture to say that it is a mistake,” said Sam, who had been much pained by the conversation. “Young men who are so patriotic in the hour of need must be men of high character.”

“Maybe they are and maybe they aren’t,” replied the insurance agent, “but old Mrs. Crane told me she was going to buy chickens again next week for her chicken-yard. There was so many stolen last year that she gave up keeping them, but next week she’s beginning again, and next week the Thatchers are going away. It’s a coincidence, anyhow.”

===Chinese===

“是这么回事。您瞧，我是干保险的。这个镇区里谷仓烧得太多，我连一张谷仓保单都开不出去；我倒不是想说谁的坏话，只是大伙儿寻思，等撒切尔家一挪窝，兴许就不会烧得这么勤了。”

“人家可从来没被抓住过什么凭据。”塔克说。

“这话不假，”年轻人说，“可他们要是待下去，兴许就有了。人们讲，琼斯乡绅（Squire Jones）本来下礼拜就要为谷仓那桩案子拿办乔希·撒切尔（Josh Thatcher），后来讲定了，只要他去古巴平，就高抬贵手。兴许没这回事，可人们都这么传。”

“我敢说这一定是以讹传讹，”萨姆说，这番谈话叫他心里很不受用。“国难当头之际这样爱国的小伙子，品格必定是高尚的。”

“也许是，也许不是，”保险代理人答道，“不过克兰太太（Mrs. Crane）告诉我，下礼拜她要重新买些鸡放进鸡圈里去。去年偷得太多，她干脆不养了；可下礼拜她又要开张，而下礼拜撒切尔家也正好动身。反正，这总归是个巧合。”

===Original===

“Oh, boys will be boys,” said Reddy. “When they get a good pension they’ll be just as respectable as you or me. Here comes Tom Slade now, and Josh Thatcher, too.”

The door had opened, and through the smoke Sam descried two young men, one a slight wiry fellow, the other a large, broad-shouldered, fair-haired man with a dull expression of the eye.

“Who says ‘drinks all around’?” cried the former. “Everybody’s blowing us off now.”

“Here,” said Jackson, waking up, “I’ll do it, hanged if I don’t. You fellows are a-goin’ to civilize the Cubapinos, and you deserve all the liquor you can carry.”

===Chinese===

“嗨，男孩子终归是男孩子，”雷迪说，“等他们领上一份像样的抚恤金，就跟你我一样体面了。瞧，汤姆·斯莱德来了，乔希·撒切尔也来了。”

门开了，隔着烟雾，萨姆认出两个年轻人：一个精瘦干练，另一个高大宽肩、一头金发，眼里却是一副呆滞的神情。

“谁来说一句‘一人一杯，全场都请’？”先前那个精瘦的嚷道，“如今人人都抢着请咱们的客。”

“来，”杰克逊醒过神儿来，“我请，我要不请就不是人。你们这帮小伙子要去开化古巴平人了，凭这个，能扛多少酒就受用多少，受之无愧。”

===Original===

He got up and approached the bar and the crowd followed him, and soon everyone was supplied with some kind of beverage.

“Here’s to Thatcher and Slade! May they represent Slowburgh honorably in the Cubapines and show ’em what Slowburghers are like,” said Jackson, elevating his iced cocktail.

The health was heartily drunk.

===Chinese===

他起身走向酒柜，众人跟了上去，不多时人人手里都端上了一杯什么喝的。

“敬撒切尔和斯莱德！”杰克逊举着他那杯冰镇鸡尾酒说，“愿他们在古巴平替斯洛堡挣得体面，让那帮人见识见识斯洛堡人是何等人物。”

这一杯，大家干得痛痛快快。

===Original===

“And here is to that distinguished officer, Captain Jinks. Long may he wave!” cried old Reddy.

“Speech, speech!” exclaimed the convivial crowd.

===Chinese===

“再敬那位功名赫赫的军官，金克斯上尉！愿他高高飘扬，长飘不倒！”老雷迪嚷道。

“讲话！讲话！”这帮酒兴正浓的汉子齐声嚷嚷。

===Original===

“Gentlemen,” responded Sam, “I am a soldier and not an orator, but I am proud to have my name coupled with those of your honored fellow townsmen. It is a sign of the greatness of our country that men of just the same character are in all quarters of this mighty republic answering their country’s call. Soon we shall have the very pick of our youth collected on the shores of these ungrateful islanders who have turned against their best friends, and these misguided people will see for themselves the fruits of our civilization as we see it, in the persons of our soldiers. Permit me in responding to your flattering toast to propose the names of Mr. Reddy and Mr. Tucker as representatives of an older generation of patriots whose example we are happy to have before us for our guidance.”

This, Sam’s first speech, was received with great applause, and then Josh Thatcher proposed three cheers for Captain Jinks, which were given with a will. The only perverse spirit was that of the commercial traveler, who had sat in the corner reading an old copy of the Slowburgh *Herald*, and now on hearing the cheers, took a candle and went upstairs to bed.

“That man’s no good,” said Reddy with a shake of his head. While the whole company were expressing their concurrence with this sentiment, Sam bade them good night and took his leave.

===Chinese===

“诸位，”萨姆答道，“我是个军人，不是演说家；但我的名字能同诸位敬爱的同乡联在一起，我深感荣幸。在这伟大的共和国的四面八方，正是同样品格的人在响应祖国的召唤，这正是我们国家伟大的明证。不久，我们青年中的精华，就将汇集在这些背弃至交、忘恩负义的岛民的海岸上；这些误入歧途的人，将从我们军人的身上，亲眼看到我们文明的果实，一如我们自己所见。承蒙诸位厚爱，举杯相祝，请容我在答辞中提议：为雷迪先生与塔克先生干杯——他们是老一辈爱国者的代表，有幸把他们的榜样摆在眼前，指引我们前行。”

这是萨姆生平头一篇演说，博得了热烈的掌声；接着乔希·撒切尔提议为金克斯上尉欢呼三声，众人都卖足了力气喊了出来。唯一存心别扭的是那位推销员：他一直坐在角落里翻一份旧《先驱报》，这会儿听见了欢呼声，便端起一支蜡烛，上楼睡觉去了。

“那家伙不是个好东西。”雷迪摇着头说。满屋子的人正纷纷表示赞同这番话时，萨姆道了晚安，起身告辞了。

## VII The Battle of San Diego / 七 圣迭戈之战

===Original===
During the next few days there was much activity in the army. It was clear that there was an expedition in preparation. All sorts of rumors were floating about, but it was impossible to verify any of them. Some said that Gomaldo was advancing with a large army; others, that he had surrendered and that the army was about to take peaceable possession of the islands. Meanwhile Sam’s position in the 200th Infantry was most unpleasant. Foster was a popular man in the regiment, and he had set all the officers against him. It was unfortunately a Slewey regiment, and it was too late for Sam to change sides﻿—a thing which he was quite ready to do. He made up his mind never to mention the two admirals again, and regretted that he had named them once too often. He complained to Cleary.

“I’m afraid,” he said, “that there’s no chance of my doing anything. The colonel will see to it that I am out of the way if there’s anything to do. I might as well have stayed at East Point.”

“Brace up, old man! I’ve got an idea,” said Cleary. “I’ll fix you all right. Just you wait till tomorrow or the day after.”

The next day in the afternoon Sam received an order to report at once at the headquarters of General Laughter. He hastened to obey, and was ushered into the presence of that distinguished officer in the palace. It was an impressive sight that met his eyes. The general was believed to weigh some three hundred pounds, but he looked as if he weighed nearer five hundred. He was dressed in a white duck suit with brass buttons, the jacket unbuttoned in front and showing his underclothes. He was suffering a good deal from the heat, and fanning himself incessantly. Several members of his staff were busied talking with visitors or writing at desks, but the chief was doing nothing. He was seated in a superb armchair with his back to a pier-glass.

“Ah! captain,” he said. “I’m glad to see you. Have a whisky and soda? I’ve assigned you to duty on my staff. Report here again tomorrow at ten and have your things moved over to the palace. Major Stroud will show you your quarters, captain!”

Major Stroud advanced and shook hands with Sam. He was every inch a soldier in appearance, but old enough to be a retired field-marshal. The three indulged in whiskies and soda, and Sam took his leave after a brief formal conversation. He found Cleary waiting for him in the street.

===Chinese===
此后几天，军中活动频繁。显然有一支远征正在筹备之中。各式各样的谣言满天飞，却哪一条也无从核实。有人说戈马尔多正率大军逼近；又有人说他已经投降，我军即将和平接管各岛。与此同时，萨姆在第二百步兵团里的处境十分难堪。福斯特在全团人缘极好，他已撺掇得全团军官群起与萨姆作对。不巧这个团是斯柳伊派的团，萨姆要改换门庭已经太迟——虽说这桩事他本来就千肯万肯。他打定主意，从此再不提那两位海军上将，只后悔自己把他们的名字多说了一回。他向克利里诉苦。

“我看，”他说，“我是没有机会干出什么名堂了。一有差事，上校保管把我支得远远的。我还不如当初就待在东点算了。”

“振作点，老伙计！我有个主意，”克利里说，“我保管给你安排得妥妥当当。你只管等到明天，至多后天。”

第二天下午，萨姆接到命令，即刻前往劳夫特（Laughter）将军的司令部报到。他赶紧奉命前往，被人引进宫中，来到那位显赫军官的面前。映入他眼帘的是一派威严气派的景象。据信将军体重约三百磅，可看上去足有将近五百磅。他穿一身缀着铜扣的白帆布西装，上衣敞着怀，露出里面的内衣。他热得够呛，手里不停地扇着扇子。司令部的几名参谋有的正忙着接待来访者，有的伏案书写，唯独这位长官无所事事。他端坐在一把气派非凡的扶手椅里，背对着一面穿衣镜。

“啊！上尉，”他说，“见到你很高兴。来杯威士忌兑苏打？我已经派你到我的参谋部任职。明天上午十点再来这里报到，把你的行李搬到宫里来。斯特劳德少校（Major Stroud）会领你看你的住处，上尉！”

斯特劳德少校走上前来，同萨姆握了握手。论相貌，他十足是个军人，只是年纪大得足以当一名退役的元帅了。三人痛饮了几杯威士忌兑苏打，客套寒暄几句之后，萨姆便起身告辞。他发现克利里正在街上等他。

===Original===
“How on earth did you do it?” cried Sam.

“It’s the B.A.C.L.,” said Cleary.

“The what!”

“The Benevolent Assimilation Company, Limited. What do you suppose? With *The Daily Lyre* thrown in too.”

“Oh! thank you, thank you, my dear, dear friend,” ejaculated Sam, with tears in his eyes. “I was beginning to think that my whole life was a failure, and here I am just in the very best place in the world. I won’t disappoint you, I won’t disappoint you!”

===Chinese===
“你到底是怎么办到的？”萨姆嚷道。

“是B.A.C.L.出力啊。”克利里说。

“是什么？”

“善意同化有限公司（B.A.C.L.）呗。你当是什么？连《里拉报》都一并搭上了呢。”

“哦！谢谢你，谢谢你，我亲爱的、亲爱的朋友，”萨姆眼含热泪，脱口而出，“我本来已经开始觉得，我这一辈子算是全完了，如今却一下子置身于全世界最好的位置上。我绝不让你失望，绝不让你失望！”

===Original===
